Also ask "Could they take on all the work?" Comedian with 9 to 11 cities: "I wouldn't make like a bazillion pages" (no page per client type per city). [calls 049, 058, 116, 067; playbook VI 2.2]
- City size sets the unit. A ~500,000-population city (Hamilton, ~15,000 small businesses) justifies targeting the whole city; a 3 to 4 million metro (Toronto) is too big, go neighborhood ("junk removal Leslieville", "landscape lighting Montreal"); "If I was a dentist... I'm not trying to go after the entire city of Toronto. I'm trying to go after a neighborhood"; a patient wants a dentist within five km; a ~2 million metro (Cincinnati): "make location pages for the suburbs" based on what people search; Montreal "boroughs" work as location pages; DC realtor: "the top 20 neighborhoods", "Realtor in neighborhood 1, realtor in neighborhood 2"; Toronto tarot: roughly 40 suburbs, one page each; realtors break pages down "by neighborhoods", east/west, community names, "by the beach", because volume is lower but far easier for a low-authority site. [playbook III 3.5, VI 2.2; calls 119, 075, 071, 044, 020, 038, 121]
- Neighborhood pages in a big city need authority first: "movers Toronto" is 62% difficulty and the North York / Leslieville / Queen West / Yorkville pages are "a really great keyword" but need blog content and topical authority before they move. [call 101]
- Zip and PIN codes: no, "people aren't typing in zip codes", they type the city name. County pages only when the keyword tool shows the county is searched ("Indian River" had almost nothing, so no page); a mortgage broker with pages for all 11 cities in his county should not extend to all 94 counties unless data shows viability: "if there's a county that has population 500 and 85% of them are searching mortgage broker near me... it would be viable"; if nobody searches it, "it adds kind of like a waste of space on your site." [calls 020, 103, 039]
- Spell it the way people type it: "St." not "Saint" for Port St. Lucie (all the volume is on "St."), "Boca Raton" not "South Florida", "Cincinnati" not "Ohio", specific cities not "GTA"; "Orange county, it is very broad", so page per city ("sign language interpreter Irvine"). Elliot's post-update nuance: keep location keywords very specific, "medical sign language interpreter Orange County" or "...Irvine County, California." [calls 103, 058, 071, 006, 040, 039; playbook III 3.5]
- Prefer small towns and suburbs over the big nearby city. "Stouffville is such a small place. Nobody is probably going after that keyword"; "think about it as a user... I'm getting married in King City. I'm looking for a King City photographer"; "Toronto wedding photographer" is "multimillion dollars to rank for", so "narrow down to specific cities... to the point of neighborhoods even." Toronto has ~50,000 small businesses, so a Mississauga agency pushing a Toronto page "might just dilute everything"; Vaughan, King City, Stouffville "might have no search volume... 10", but "nobody's going after all those small ones." "The ones that we've had the most success with is we go after the small towns"; a florist delivering to a town of 5,000 next to a big city: "I would rather go after that keyword." Florida cities of 83,000, 66,000, 58,000: "am I doing the right thing by creating a page for each city? Yes, yes." Expect "one or two clicks a month from each of those, but they'll be much more qualified." [calls 006, 007, 088, 038, 014]
- Why the big city is hard: a location page usually cannot outrank a genuine local competitor's homepage (their de-facto money page, most-backlinked page) without dedicated backlinks; Toronto competitors also get local publication links; around Toronto "40 or 50 small towns" each have a wedding photographer with a GBP "only optimizing for there", so outside-the-city pages "might be a little bit more difficult." Alternative for photographers: venue pages you have shot ("Ancaster Mills sessions") that double as galleries. [playbook VI 2.2; calls 004, 014]
- Counter-case: a photographer who moved to a suburb was told to target "wedding photographer Hamilton", not the tiny town, "because Hamilton's a much bigger place"; a web designer an hour from Orlando can make an Orlando page ("you're not technically saying you're in Orlando, you're just saying web design Orlando"), targeting "Orlando web design company" because "Orlando web design" is 1,300 volume at ~53 difficulty (needs DA ~53+, she had 4), with small wins like "Cocoa Beach web design" (~20 searches) to build momentum; a studio-town photographer adds a separate page for the bigger nearby town (Schaumburg). [calls 096, 085, 058]
- Zero-volume local keywords are still worth a page when difficulty is near zero: "15% of Google searches are brand new every single day"; "somebody might look up that keyword in that small town and then you're the only one"; higher population usually means some volume, so confirm city + keyword first; national KD can mislead (a term can show KD 88 nationally with no single city carrying that volume). "Sell your house fast Delaware Ohio" had 7 difficulty and the #1 result had zero referring domains: "this tells me I can rank without getting backlinks." [calls 038, 117, 082, 119; playbook III 3.5, VI 1.2]
- Don't take the city list from an AI: run each city through a keyword tool ("North Hollywood electrician"), find the easiest, build those first; decide from data, "not necessarily based on what Claude's saying." [calls 073, 039]
- Too small or too new to rank at city level: target state or province keywords first, then build pages for the top 10 to 20 highest-population cities (West Virginia agency: "the top 10 places population wise in the state"). Cleaning-business coaching: instead of one "how much does it cost to start a cleaning business" page fighting Stripe and Wix, build state/province versions ("in Ontario", "in BC", "in Ohio") because rules, taxes, and suppliers differ, with real cost screenshots. [playbook VI 2.2; calls 051, 024]
- Nationwide or virtual business with a local GBP: "would we create a local page on the website specifically to St. Louis? Yeah, for sure", then suburb pages "one a week"; remote bookkeeper: "Start with the area closest to you... Bookkeeper in Detroit. And then you can have your service pages mention you operate or service all of Michigan or all of the US", because the website ranking for the city and the GBP "feed back into each other"; optimize around the city you can win (St. Petersburg) and mention you serve Florida and nationwide; a virtual tarot service still optimizes the homepage for the home city and adds surrounding cities; furniture rental serving SF, Las Vegas, Phoenix from LA: "If you are servicing different areas, absolutely"; "Northwest Lien" going national keeps the domain and should "create a location page and see if that drives any traffic"; a US and UK agency makes service location pages rather than one page for both countries. Kira's caution: if you are not hyperlocal, the GBP (which forces a service area) may not be the top lead priority. [calls 013, 012, 068, 030, 058, 114, 105, 106, 092]
- Only for places you are based in or genuinely serve: "Unless you're actually based in that location. I wouldn't." A real office or "a virtual address" legitimizes "SEO for businesses in Hamilton"; check whether people search "central Florida" or specific towns. [call 004]
- "Near me" is never the target: not in the keyword, URL, or copy, "the sign of somebody who doesn't know how to do SEO"; "you can't put near me on the page to rank for it", Google uses the searcher's vicinity, and if "near me" is already your top query, leave the page alone; "near me" and "near you" do nothing; "dentist near me" is 90,000 searches country-wide and "really hard to rank for"; "near me is probably going to be the most broad SEO keyword on planet Earth", and a location keyword like "Mechanic Killeen, Texas" (ranking 6th) is "significantly better for your SEO strategy overall." Support proximity with real signals instead: explicit service-area names, a visible address, GBP, and citations (BrightLocal, about $200 one time, into Yelp, Bing, Apple Maps, Facebook, 411, Yellow Pages). [playbook II 2.5, III 2.3, VI 2.1; calls 013, 113, 109, 044, 096; course: finding-keywords]
- Tie each page to a Google Business Profile where possible: Pavitra's location page tied to her GBP ranks, the other does not; best is a GBP per area if you have a physical location, otherwise location pages; a GBP "won't help outside its city" (Newmarket landscaper not showing for Aurora) but location pages "will definitely help"; you probably will not enter a new city's map pack without a GBP there, and widening your GBP service area "might dilute" your current map pack; subleasing an office for a second GBP: "technically you shouldn't do that." Physical address beats service area: "If you don't have physical address, you can have a service area, which is still helpful, but it's not as efficient." [calls 023, 073, 074, 114, 052]
- Expansion recipe: step one, GBPs in the new areas (otherwise no maps, no local reviews, no local signals); step two, "a page on the website for those locations... a dedicated landing page for them. So that's a location page"; step three, local press, local backlinks, chamber of commerce, sponsorships. Two regions (Ontario and New Jersey/NYC): GBP in both plus a location page for both, reviews get split, maybe start with one market. One GBP per location, each linking to its own location page; a temporary second location (one year minimum) still gets its own GBP; a seasonal business in two countries (Ibiza summers) gets "two pages set up" and a GBP per location; rental properties at different addresses can each have a GBP. [calls 022, 024, 117, 105, 114, 109]
- GBP hygiene that backs the pages: each service-area page linked from the matching GBP service category; GBP description first line says what you do and where; two GBPs (commercial vs residential roofing) are fine for two audiences if each points to an optimized page, not a portfolio landing page; with four GBPs (Clermont, Orlando, Naples, Fort Myers), choose one as the main location of the website; NAP identical everywhere. [calls 005, 056, 094, 070; playbook V 4.4; book ch. 18]
- Separate website instead of location pages when the market is far or very competitive: Albany, three hours from NYC, because visitors wonder "are they coming out?"; a six-clinic dental group ("why is that information all on the same website?"); a Botox clinic expanding to another state; otherwise pages per area ("Hamilton Botox", "downtown Hamilton Botox", "Dundas Botox Clinic"). [calls 074, 075, 117]

### What goes on it, top to bottom (the structure)

- The governing rule: "You're changing the entire content each time. It is a lot of work, but if it makes you money, then it's worth it." The structure can be a template, the content cannot: "just make sure all those pages are 100% unique. They mention local landmarks, they mention local things." [calls 021, 088]
- Opening build (Vancouver web designer, one page per area: North Van, Burnaby, Surrey, downtown): H1 "website design in Burnaby"; H2 like "Premier website design in Burnaby"; then local specifics: "Burnaby has over, I don't know, 15,000 small businesses and only X percent of them have a website. But those who do have a website and who have like a properly mobile optimized website... are getting more business"; then local businesses you built sites for, or, if none yet, local sites whose design you love ("if you're looking for something similar, you can always contact us"). [call 005]
- Denroi's heading skeleton (web developer, Wyoming): "Web developer in Wyoming, there's your title tag, there's your heading one. Down that page you'll have headings: Why should you hire a web developer in Wyoming? Why your Wyoming business needs professional web development. How a web developer can help businesses in Wyoming. Really build out that page for the service and keyword and repeat that process for every single area." [call 023]
- Pest-control heading pattern: H1 "Fort Myers Pest Control" ("You can repeat the Heading 1 tag as a Heading 2 tag sometimes"); H2s "Why choose us in Fort Myers", "Kind words our customers say in Fort Myers", "Fort Myers Pest Control Services"; the individual services under that are H3s carrying the city ("Pest control in Orlando"). Denroi's Clermont pass: the location appeared only in the H1 and one other heading, "for the rest of this page you don't mention the location at all. So this is an easy optimization you can do across every single location page": "Why choose Infinite Solutions" (you rank for your brand anyway) becomes "Why choose us for pest control in Clermont"; "Kind words from our customers in Clermont"; "Our Clermont pest control services"; buttons "Learn more about pest control in Clermont", "Learn more about spider control in Clermont". [calls 070, 072]
- Vancouver metal roofing page (service + location): title "Metal roofing in Vancouver"; headings changed from "Benefits of metal roofing" to "Benefits of metal roofing for homes in Vancouver", "really emphasize the location since this is a location page"; the thin colour-options sentence made "double or triple the length"; a toggleable FAQ block (most common roof colour, does colour matter, brown or black, does colour affect temperature) that "pads up the keyword density and helps this page rank for more keywords while associating it more to the location"; a cost comparison table ("always works fantastic for Google"); and a bottom section "Are you looking for other roofing services?" relisting every service with a one-sentence description and a link. [call 082]
- The minimal model (The Model Home, Washington DC area page): headline names the city, "one or two sentences," a list of specialties (closet makeovers, kitchen reorganizations, decluttering), and a contact button: "I come here. It says Washington, D.C. I know I'm in the right locality." [call 109]
- Comedian/entertainer version: each city page lists the types of clients you work with, "a little bit more detail about the location and how to book you", plus a section of event types whose buttons go to the generic event-type pages. [calls 067, 049]
- Local content recipes, by business. Newborn/family photographer: "you need to make sure that you're mentioning things that are local to that area", monuments, parks, wedding venues, "Use local landmarks", the hospitals where babies are born; the parks you shoot in and what each is like: "it's really great from the months of May until September... in the middle of the summer it's going to be a lot hotter... here are some things that you can do to prepare." Wedding photographer: "I shoot a lot in Toronto. Here are some of the Toronto events I've done. Here are the venues that I've shot at." Florist: "here are some funeral homes near us that we deliver to. Here are some hospitals, here are some high school or event spaces", same-day delivery windows ("if you're ordering from [city], within six hours or 12 hours, we'll have flowers out to you"), "if you're attending a funeral in blank, here's where we'll deliver flowers within X amount of hours", the wedding venues per page, "So it's totally unique content every single time." GLP-1 telehealth: nearest hospitals if side effects happen, grocery stores with GLP-1 friendly foods, the farmer's market, local gyms (or partner with them), a local hike, "a nice route for you in your neighborhood" for a 5k walk. Auto repair (10 towns in a 25 to 30 mile radius): the specific car struggles in that town, backing a boat into the lake, Texas heat, neighborhoods, streets, lakes, parks. Mortgage: this town's four events this year, loan programs for living there. Electrician: the different utility company in Richmond vs Langley vs Vancouver, whether electricity is more expensive, which permits are needed. Real estate finance: "each city is going to have different bylaws", plus local partner referrals (best mortgage broker or realtor there). Nanny agency: "there's a lot of parks in the area, if you're comfortable we can take your kids to the park". Betrayal therapy: Alberta oil-field work schedules, Ashley Madison in Toronto. Landscaping: the town's trees, bugs, landscaping, then "here are some of the projects that we've recently completed in small town" linking to the project pages (the projects help the location page rank). Junk cars (Kira's team method): "Oh, you're in Dallas. Like, let's talk a little bit about Dallas... Buying a junk car in Houston is helpful because Houston has this and that and Dallas has that. Now we have differentiated pages." [calls 070, 021, 074, 002, 039, 117, 019, 071, 010, 107]
- Remote or non-delivery businesses anchor in local hooks the same way (nearest hospitals, grocery stores carrying your product, local gyms and trails). [playbook VI 2.2]
- How to generate the copy: reuse the blog interview prompt per location, "what do you like the most about this location? How's the lighting in this location? What if it's rainy? Can we still shoot here?", then turn the answers into the page. [call 021]
- Testimonials from that area only: Boca Raton testimonials on the Boca Raton page, not mixed ("Yep"); reviews on a location page should come from that location's own GBP ("ask which Google Business Profile the reviews come from"), under a city-bearing heading ("Kind words our customers say in Fort Myers"). [calls 032, 070; playbook III 3.4]
- Services on the page: link only the services relevant to that town (if the content only supports 10 of the 36 services, link those), keep a note so every service is covered across the set of pages, and weave the links into the content rather than "three links at the bottom... related" (acceptable only if there is no alternative); links should be "somewhat high quality" where they expand the knowledge. [call 039]
- Neighborhoods inside a town page: a town page can list its neighborhoods; a DC mover got a "neighborhoods we serve in Washington D.C." section with links to local neighborhood news sites. [playbook VI 2.2; calls 049, 061]
- FAQs: location FAQs belong on the page (see the roofing example), but never repeat identical FAQ text verbatim across location pages. [call 082; playbook III 2.10]
- The locations hub page: "have a button in your footer and your header that says locations ... it goes to a page that has all the different locations", grouped (Chicago suburbs together), each linking to its own page; Leanne's version is a "Locations served" overview plus one page per city. A hub that is "just like, a page of links. It's useless. People don't really need that" gets rebuilt as a hierarchy: the top page lists the two counties (Suffolk, Nassau) with the top towns under each (Town of Hempstead, North Hempstead); clicking a town opens that town's own page, which lists the places within it; "You're probably going to be creating like 10 or 20 pages." The hub must make it obvious which country the pages serve ("a user that lands on that hub page can tell those pages are in the US for the US, not for someone in the UK"). [calls 021, 067, 049, 023; playbook VI 2.2]
- What is a blog post instead: "we offer this service in this town or the city, that can be a page"; informational or comparison content is a blog post. Community content for a realtor (best wineries, coffee shops, fruit farms, festivals, what it's like to live in Port Dalhousie, walkability, retiring there) "they're all blog posts", written factually from your own point of view "like a tour guide", not static community pages; "hidden gems" and "the five best coffee shops in this area" posts with phone photos in front of each. Static information you want to rank for the money keyword (the parks you shoot at) stays on the location page, "because that's like static information that you want people to know and you want to rank for the money keyword." Opening a new location: "I wouldn't say do a blog for that, David. I would just say make the location page." Support a city page with "best venues / best entertainers in Dallas" posts, never generic "about Dallas" blogs. Realtor lifestyle pages like "what does it mean to get divorced in North Potomac" are a separate neighborhood-page type. [calls 019, 020, 021, 027, 054, 005]

### Layout and design

- Same ordering fix as a homepage: move "who we work with" and the design approach higher, with pictures, and keep it scannable so a human "can actually consume it" and decide to hire you. [calls 043, 020]
- Keep the site shell identical across location pages: a set of landing pages with a differently coloured menu made "This site looks like a different site." [call 010]
- Per-city navigation helps: competitor Massey gives each city page its own navigation listing that city's services, each service (spider control) with its own page; "A lot of people get lazy... they don't have unique navigations for each city. And it does help." Suggested build: a Locations button, five to seven location pages, each listing landscape / irrigation / pest control with deeper pages under them. [call 070]
- Templated blocks are the tell: two pest-control pages looked identical at the top, only the "why choose" block and services section differed, the reviews widget was the same, and the services block was a shortcode outputting the same headings on every page: "If this is the same or similar on every page, that's not good." The review widget was also "either not showing up as text on the page or it's slow." [call 070]
- Do not park area-served pages under About Us on an old template; redesign and build proper service pages first. [call 109]
- No references list at the bottom ("Don't do references like this. Just internal link or external link throughout the content"). [call 010]
- Mobile basics for a local page: "Can someone call you with one click? Can they find your contact page or checkout easily?"; a clickable phone number. [book ch. 20; call 070]

### Visuals

- Real photos of you and your work, not stock: replace the Unsplash woman ("I've seen that photo a million times") with a picture of yourself. [call 049]
- Geotag the photos on location pages: "It's better to do it than not do it... if it takes you an extra five minutes to geotag your images... why not do that additional step"; Kenneth adds GPS coordinates to images on his location pages. Matt's caveat: geotagging matters most for GBP photos and posts; on the website "I don't know that that has like a significant impact", and never fake-geotag stock photos as your city. [calls 005, 063, 003]
- Map: an embedded Google Map is slow in PageSpeed; use a screenshot image (webp) that links to the GBP / Google Maps; the HeyTony footer map image links to the GBP CID URL; one member pins a footer map to Orange County city hall "to anchor that in Google." [calls 070, 054, 040]
- Project and venue imagery: recent local projects linked from the town page (landscaping); venue sessions as galleries (photographer). [calls 071, 014]

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO

- Word count: "You can do four or five hundred words. If it's not competitive, you don't need a lot of information"; "if there's no volume and no difficulty, you could probably get away with four or five hundred words." If competitive, audit who ranks and match their depth; 2,000 words is not too much "just make sure it's formatted in a way where if a person's reading it ... they can actually consume it." Michael's rule (confirmed by Matt): look at the three main competitors for that keyword in that city, "see the length of their local page and then write the same or a few extra words, maybe 100 or 200 words." [calls 020, 038, 002; playbook III 3.5]
- Too long is real: Elliot's AI-spammed location pages were each "an incredibly long piece of content" (4,000 to 6,200 words) with identical sections repeated on every page. [call 010]
- Title tag and H1 lead with service + city: "Website design in Hamilton, Ontario" as the single H1; title examples "Hamilton Web Design, your local web design company", "Top Web designers in Hamilton 2022 reviews", "Web Design website Development Hamilton", all with "the focus keyword right at the beginning or very, very close to the beginning"; meta examples "Hamilton's award winning web design and development agency" and "Massive Web Design is an established Hamilton web design firm..."; a cash home buyer's H1 "Looking to sell your Delaware home quickly without hassle" becomes "Sell your house fast, Delaware, Ohio" or "We buy houses in Delaware, Ohio"; "couples counseling in Midtown Manhattan" in the H1; "Interior designer in Wellington, Florida offering residential commercial services" as a title. The location in the H1 is what tells Google the page is different ("if in my one tag I put that specific like Skokie newborn... then it's like oh, kind of knows this is the keyword"). [course: no-h1-heading-multiple-h1-headings-defined, title-tags, meta-descriptions; calls 119, 113, 043, 050]
- Meta description: keyword near the start, not the end ("keywords, especially in your meta description, have much more weight to Google when they are earlier"). The keyword "does not have to be exact, but it has to be related": "metal roofing in Vancouver" and "Vancouver metal roofing contractor" are the same intent; avoid awkward over-optimized headings ("Metal roofing is the last roof you'll ever need" became "Why a metal roof is important in Vancouver"). [call 082]
- City, state, or neighborhood in the keyword: the homepage keyword attaches to the state; city pages use city + state or city only depending on volume ("we buy junk cars Dallas" 210/mo, KD 9, vs 20/mo with "Texas"); neighborhood pages use keyword + neighborhood + city, not the state. [call 107]
- URL: service slash location, /security-guard/toronto/, and drop the "services" parent ("Having the services parent is the unnecessary one there ... it extends the URL ... too redundant"); a hyphen form (security-guard-toronto) is fine, but the slash makes categorizing easier "if you are building out dozens or hundreds of location pages ... over 30 pages"; "/landscape-lighting-montreal" style simplification; subfolder, not subdomain (yoursite.com/irvine, not irvine.yoursite.com). [calls 023, 109, 073]
- Uniqueness rules: similar titles across the set ("sell your house fast in Franklin County, Ohio", "...Portsmouth, Ohio") are fine "but make sure that all the text on the rest of the page is completely unique... It should be, like, totally, totally, totally unique", not reworded; if they are not ranking, they are not different enough or target the wrong keyword. Forty service-area pages for one client: "some of them do really well for her, and some of them don't"; it is "dangerous territory" unless "you're talking about those local areas and you're not duplicating any content. At all." Copy-paste-with-city-swapped at scale risks demotion; multiplying pages is not the risk, duplicate copy across them is. [calls 119, 118; playbook VI 2.2, IV Canonicals]
- Competitors who rank with near-duplicate pages "either have backlinks or the content is actually different and it doesn't look different to you, but it is different"; if they really are swapping the city, "then it's super low competition if you come in with new and different content." Try unique pages for three or four months; only if they do not rank ask "can I do something simpler? ... Maybe you can make them all the same" (a podcast guest's duplicates ranked in low competition, "I don't think that works for everybody"). [calls 070, 021]
- Low-stakes exception: duplicate booking pages (escape room, Anaheim vs Connecticut, identical descriptions) only need a different title tag and meta description ("booking for Anaheim"), which "will make it not duplicate"; a page meant to rank for its own local keyword needs real unique body copy. [call 017; playbook IV Canonicals, VI 3.3]
- Schema for multi-city: each city page carries its own LocalBusiness schema naming only that city; a full separate schema per physical location / GBP; the homepage schema lists every service-area city. [call 064; playbook IV Schema]
- Disambiguation is automatic: London, Ontario vs London, UK needs nothing on the page, "your phone number is going to be on the site... it's going to know the area code, it's going to know your address." [call 117]
- Research in the right country and language: set the keyword tool to your country ("if I search this in the United States, it's going to be very different than if I search this in Canada"); for Quebec use a private browser set to Canadian French (the dialect and mindset differ from France). [course: keyword-research; calls 015, 013]
- Track the set: filter GSC or Looker Studio for every query containing the city word ("Hamilton") to see "whether people are searching for SEO or digital marketing, marketing or website design or any variation"; when a city page shows a different town as a top query ("Duluth, Georgia" on the Atlanta page), build a new "Duluth IT services" page rather than rewriting Atlanta. [course: tracking-your-growth; call 038]
- Leave them alone after publishing: "you wouldn't touch them again ... leave them for three to six months"; give it "three plus months and stop fiddling with keywords"; expect 3 to 6 months, up to a year, for a new location page (or a relocation) to move. [calls 014, 069, 054, 114; playbook VI 2.2, VI 5.2]

### CTAs and conversion

- They should convert or book, "not necessarily right from that page, but like, at least have a link saying like, here's, you know, go book an appointment"; they can also link out to one relevant blog post. [call 002]
- Add a "book a free consultation / book a free call" CTA on each location page; the minimal area page ends in a contact button. [calls 043, 109]
- Descriptive CTA text: "never use generic call to action text, contact us. This should always be replaced with whatever you do", e.g. "contact a Vancouver roofer" or "contact a professional roofing company", because "for Google, AI or any crawl bot, they wouldn't be able to tell" what "contact us" leads to. [call 082]
- Local CTAs inside broad content point back to the location page: "If you're looking for a hairstylist in Denver, [link] give us a call"; "if you're looking for website design in West Virginia" at the bottom of an otherwise generic post; "if you're looking for a complete home renovation in [city]" on a general contractor's guides. [playbook VI 2.2; calls 095, 051, 063]
- Proof it pays: a page targeting local, easy-difficulty keywords brought last-minute bookings through the contact form, "boring old local SEO... making sure name, address, phone number are on the site, targeting local keywords, easy difficulty... boring equals money." [call 042]
- Out-of-area inquiries you must decline are still a signal: out-of-area traffic, time on site, "and even contact forms you must decline" are "all sending signals to Google that you're a website that does this thing." [call 026]

### Links, indexing, and where it sits in the site

- Nest location pages under a standalone Locations hub, never inside one service page: "I would have a locations page and then have the locations there"; "right in your navigation, you would say locations or service areas and just start listing each of those different areas"; a Botox clinic's pages "should be in your navigation menu, like locations or something like that." [playbook VI 2.2; calls 020, 097, 117]
- Header or footer: location pages "don't have to be" in the main navigation, "However, we always recommend the most important location pages be somewhere in your navigation, whether that's the main header navigation or in the footer." The footer is fine "as long as those pages are accessible somehow" (driveagency.com lists cities in the footer, each linking to "Atlanta Digital Marketing Agency", "Miami Digital Marketing Agency", copying that is "a good strategy"); add the list as a fifth footer column or its own footer section; footer link text can be just the city ("you can still name it like Orlando. You don't need to call it the keyword you're shooting for in the footer because it's just a link"); "our areas of service" in the footer plus an "areas we serve" block on the homepage. Footer local signals (NAP, navigation) matter because Google "looks for that to determine if it's a local business." [calls 023, 082, 085, 088, 081]
- Homepage links index faster: link new local pages directly from the homepage ("areas we serve"); footer-only links index slower. [playbook VI 2.2]
- Menu depth: one "service area" dropdown ("I want to see one drop down. You could have 10 on here if you want to"); a menu nesting cities, counties, and dumpster sizes under each was "way too much going on... how do you even do this on mobile?... I can't even click those. So why are they there?"; Denroi's version is a Locations dropdown listing cities, hover a city for a side dropdown of "comedian in [city]" services; services and locations can both live in nav dropdowns. [calls 103, 068, 040]
- City lists at the bottom of pages: "It is good. But people do it bad a lot of the times"; if competitors list 12 locations at the bottom, "try it, pick one page to do it on"; a "locations I perform at" section on the main event pages with each city clickable once its page exists; if you list cities in the footer (Santa Barbara to San Diego), link them. [calls 070, 021, 058, 042]
- Never hide them: "if those are secret and you can't get to them from your homepage, Google can't see those"; "If we hide pages, we hide them from people, we hide them from Google" (a competitor's "areas we serve" button led to unlinked clones identical except the city name); Elliot's raw-HTML landing pages were unreachable from the homepage and so would not be indexed. [calls 025, 117, 010]
- Blogs feed location pages: write blogs that link to them with anchors like "Skokie newborn photographer"; "you don't need to include more than one location per blog post... Alternate services and locations" (50 or 100 links in one post is unreadable and unnatural); in every post a line like "if you're looking for a hair stylist in Denver" linked to the local page; blogs for narrower areas build the authority to go after harder keywords later; a "guides" button on each city page can filter the guides. [calls 014, 112, 095, 007, 063]
- Links out of a location page: cross-link neighboring location pages ("if you're actually in [city next door], we also service that area") and other service pages; cap blog links at 2 to 3 per page and "don't link to the same blog post from all the location pages"; service and location pages "do not always have to link to blog posts. If they do, the best chance to do that is in your FAQ section"; Manny: link each service page to the matching city page. [calls 080, 082, 109; playbook IV Internal Linking]
- Link the page to the GBP: use the CID link (whatismycid / Pleper; maps URL ending ?cid=number, permanent, "slight more benefits than just the share button"), anchor text like "movers in D.C." works, or make the address clickable to the CID; the content checklist's version: every page links to the GBP with the GBP target keyword as anchor. [calls 054, 082; email 2025-07-03]
- GBP posts as links: when location pages sat a year without ranking, add links to them in GBP posts whose copy mentions the service and the location, and build more internal links from blog posts that mention the area ("the more internal links you create... the more Google will see that page as important for your site"). Location pages are acceptable blog link targets, "typically you want to find blog posts" as well. [calls 121, 122]
- Indexing: after a batch, Kenneth uses the 10 free daily GSC index requests and "typically get them ranked within about two hours" (not in top 100, then #1 or #2); Victor's neighborhood page hit the top of the AI Overview two days after manual indexing. [calls 065, 001]
- Pacing: "one or two location pages per week. I wouldn't do more than that"; "Just start with one ... do one a week. And if there's 12 in three months, you have all the pages done"; agency cadence 1 blog post a week + 2 city pages a month (a three-month program of 12 topics + 6 city pages); about two pages or posts a week "sniper rifle" not "shotgun"; don't make 20 at once, start with towns showing traction or that are underserved; a 12-location build finishes in ~3 months. [calls 010, 021, 051, 071, 047; playbook VI 2.2]
- Order of build: start with your own city (easiest, ties to the GBP address for consistent NAP), then "work backwards from the cities with the highest population, or the cities that you get the most customers from... Go in the order that brings you the most money"; or start with the easiest-to-rank locations; optimize the main service pages first, then add location pages; a home builder eyeing 20 to 30 counties picks five; a dumpster company with 16 county pages perfects one first ("I don't want you doing the work twice"); stop when you are getting business ("pigs get fat and hogs get slaughtered"), you do not have to finish the list. [calls 068, 020, 050, 075; playbook VI 2.2]
- Timing: a Super-Bowl-driven location page starts ~6 months ahead of the demand spike; when relocating, keep the current-location wording until the physical location closes, then switch every page that mentions the old location. [playbook VI 3.4; call 054]
- Local links that help them: chamber of commerce, city hall, town center; local newspaper press release, chamber, BBB "send local signals that you are based in that area"; if a directory-heavy SERP outranks you, get listed on those directories with links back. [calls 039, 101, 066]
- Multilingual and country notes: make a Spanish version of each local service page, "Just make sure it's actually translated and do the keyword research again, but in Spanish... almost the exact same content, but you're using the keyword in the way that it makes sense"; a Swiss site ranking in French and poorly in English should add German, "Three chances to rank three different audiences"; Spanish-language sites do the same keyword research and posts in Spanish, not just translation. Bilingual URLs on subfolders (/fr/), not subdomains, with hreflang; English slugs under /fr/ are fine if the page is French; re-slug to French keywords only with redirects and after checking GSC so you don't change URLs of French pages already ranking. Country tags in URLs are unnecessary "unless you have like a multi language site ... or different pricing in different currencies"; research in the target country instead. Locale duplicates (Shopify EN-CA vs default): check GSC for the winner; redirect the loser for blog content; don't force a redirect on product pages (use a switcher). Squarespace blog geo metadata: set the post's location only when the content actually mentions the place ("service in Dubai"). [calls 002, 019, 015, 013, 020, 023; playbook IV Canonicals]
- AI Overviews lean broader: write around the full state or province (Ontario rather than Hamilton, Texas rather than Houston). [call 030; playbook V 7.2]

### Don'ts

- Don't swap the city into a template: "You need 100% original, my friend"; a reviews widget or services shortcode that renders the same on every page is the giveaway. [calls 116, 070; playbook VI 2.2]
- Don't mention other cities on a city page, and don't keep Ancaster on the Hamilton page ("that page won't have Ancaster in it. It'll have Hamilton, it'll have Stony Creek"); St. Catharines and Niagara on one page is two topics. [calls 075, 069; course: every-page-1-main-topic]
- Don't inject your city into broad blog posts: David added "in Killeen" to broad-ranking posts on Claude's advice and traffic fell from 40,000; Matt: change them back (reverting all at once is fine, give it 1 to 2 months). Blog posts "cast a big net" nationally, "and as you get traffic from outside of your area, that's going to help you rank inside of your area because that builds authority"; "I would typically avoid using any location names" in blog titles unless it's your location. [calls 004, 095; playbook VI 2.6; course: find-questions]
- Don't use "near me" anywhere on the page or in the URL. [playbook II 2.5]
- Don't build pages for places you don't serve or aren't based in, and don't diversify far away: a Toronto designer's footer listing Toronto, Orlando, and another distant city drew "why are you trying to diversify so far out? Go micro with the cities." [calls 004, 085]
- Don't build the location x service matrix "just in case"; don't take the city list from ChatGPT or Claude; don't chase zips or unsearched counties. [calls 049, 116, 073, 020, 103]
- Don't hide pages or leave them orphaned; don't nest locations under a service page. [calls 117, 025, 020]
- Don't create a hub that is "just a page of links"; don't stop at an "areas we serve" list or map graphic. [calls 049, 061]
- Don't rewrite a page that already ranks for your home city to chase a new town; make a new page. [calls 074, 038]
- Don't put "Why choose [brand]" headings on a location page ("You will rank for your brand name no matter what you do"); put the city in the heading instead. [call 072]
- Don't bolt a references list onto the bottom; don't change the menu colours per page. [call 010]
- Don't go statewide when your city has more work than you can handle ("shooting a shotgun"); "don't just create a page to create a page." [calls 071, 066]
- Don't publish a placeholder blog while waiting for the budget to build the city pages. [call 065]
- Don't sublease an office to fake a second GBP ("technically you shouldn't do that"); don't widen the GBP service area without expecting map-pack dilution. [calls 074, 114]
- Don't write generic "about Dallas" blogs as support content. [call 054]

### From the calls: live examples

- Kenneth (agency, Tampa / St. Pete / Clearwater): "creating detailed location pages and service pages" for each area put a client at #1 to #3 across a 2.5-mile grid overnight; he indexes each batch through GSC and gets them ranked "within about two hours." [calls 063, 065]
- Peter (Bulgaria): a page for each city he wanted, #1 in six cities in about two weeks. [call 056]
- Small-town electrician client: "we built like 40 of them in like a month and a half... She's getting orders from all of these small towns that nobody is going after." [call 117]
- Jennifer's three-month client program: one blog post per week plus "a city specific page... two per month" (six total), no backlinks; client ended "at capacity, looking to hire more people." [call 051]
- Victor (DC movers): service-area pages each linked from the matching GBP service category ("local moves in Washington D.C."); the competitor beating him in the map pack has no service-area page at all but far more reviews and longer history; his neighborhood-specific service page ("very unique, very useful, and very specific") reached the top of the AI Overview in two days. [calls 005, 001]
- Lux (multi-location service): "we keep getting more requests on California because we optimize our California page and blog"; a California resource page plus an LA-niched blog produced the first LA order within a week. [calls 032, 054]
- Chloe (IT services, nine locations): the audit tool correctly compares her Lubbock page to competitors' homepages; "Duluth, Georgia" showing as a top query on her Atlanta page triggered a new Duluth page. [call 038]
- Elliot (betrayal therapy): AI-spammed raw-HTML location pages, unlinked, 4,000 to 6,200 words each with repeated sections and a references list; fix was delete them, then "one or two location pages per week", each unique to its area, with the by-province / by-city list "in your footer kind of thing." "We are not spamming... be intentional." [call 010]
- Annie (Florida pest control, four GBPs): templated Fort Myers / Orlando / Clermont pages fixed with city-bearing H2s and H3s; competitor Massey's per-city navigation as the model. [calls 070, 072]
- Jessica (Long Island): county-and-town hub hierarchy mocked up live with inspect element; Elliot's follow-up: the town links pointed back to the main service page, "a great opportunity for you to start developing specific service pages for those locations." [calls 049, 047]
- Mark (webuyhousescashohio): service-area H1s rewritten to the keyword; the 7-difficulty "Sell your house fast Delaware Ohio" with a zero-referring-domain #1. [call 119]
- Maritza / Marisa (comedian, Boca Raton): 66-page matrix rejected in favour of city pages with event-type sections; Boca Raton testimonials kept on the Boca Raton page. [calls 049, 058, 032]
- Adam (web design, #1 to 2 in the Mississauga map pack, Toronto page bouncing page 2 to 6): drop the Toronto push, build small-market pages. [call 088]
- Prito (Toronto wedding photographer): "GTA" replaced with Hamilton, Stouffville, King City, Mississauga, Oakville pages, backed by narrower-area blogs. [calls 006, 007]
- Brock (Newmarket landscaper, 145 five-star reviews, invisible in Aurora): location pages, not a second GBP. [call 074]
- Monica (realtor): pages a year without ranking, prescribed GBP posts and blog internal links. [call 121]
- Pavitra (interior designer, Wellington FL and Dubai): the GBP-tied page ranks; moving means re-verifying the GBP (keep reviews) or a second profile (lose them). [calls 023, 043]
- Christopher (Valor Mortgage, Tennessee): 11 city pages, same format, different content; told not to extend to 94 counties without data. [call 039]
- David Jones (auto repair, Central Texas): 10 hyperlocal town pages within 25 to 30 miles, services linked selectively. [call 039]
- Jackie (Denver hairstylist): local keyword pages produced last-minute bookings; every blog carries a Denver line linking to the service page, NAP in the footer, link to the GBP. [calls 042, 095]
- Cigar (Vancouver web designer): North Van / Burnaby / Surrey / downtown pages with the "15,000 small businesses" style local hook. [call 005]
- Book cases: Ahmed's Dubai clinic ranking for "doctor near me," "general practitioner Dubai," "medical care in Dubai" at ~8,000 visitors per month; the AC story's winning service pages were "optimized for the city I was in" (Hamilton). [book Introduction, ch. 40]

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- The book has no location-page chapter; it offers only adjacent local guidance (geotargeted keywords, Map Pack vs organic, GBP, NAP, citations, reviews). Matt promised a dedicated Insiders training on "what to do and what not to do" on location pages (prompted by whether city pages nest under service pages); that training is not in the transcripts read here. [book ch. 3, 18; call 010]
- One service per city page (Matt: "I just choose one that is the easiest to rank for") vs Leanne's "focus on the location first... and then in that location you explain your two different services" with separate general service pages. Both were given to the same comedian. [calls 014, 016]
- How unique is unique: Matt and the team say "totally, totally, totally unique", "100% original", "not duplicating any content. At all"; Leanne described a ranking agency that "just changed up the wording... changed up the lingo for each of these service pages"; duplicate booking pages can get by on title + meta alone; and Matt himself allows a fallback to "make them all the same" if unique pages fail after three or four months in low competition. [calls 119, 116, 118, 106, 017, 021]
- Serve it or be based there: "Unless you're actually based in that location. I wouldn't" sits next to the Orlando advice ("you're not technically saying you're in Orlando, you're just saying web design Orlando") and "wedding photographer Hamilton... because Hamilton's a much bigger place." The reconcile offered in the calls is whether you genuinely serve the place. [calls 004, 085, 096]
- Small towns first vs the nearest big city: the dominant advice is micro markets, but a suburb-based photographer was pointed at Hamilton, and Jennifer was pointed at Orlando with small towns as momentum builders. [calls 088, 096, 085]
- Footer placement: the playbook says footer-only links index slower and to link from the homepage; Denroi says the footer "is fine as long as those pages are accessible somehow." [playbook VI 2.2; call 082]
- Geotagging images on the site: "worth investing a day" and "why not" vs "I don't know that that has like a significant impact." [calls 005, 003]
- Length: 400 to 500 words is enough for low competition, yet the team's internal checklist (written for blog posts) sets a 1,000-word minimum; the calls reconcile it as "match the three competitors." [calls 020, 002; email 2025-07-03]
- Map pack in new cities: "you probably will not enter the map pack there without a GBP" vs expansion advice that leads with GBPs; no call tested location pages alone against the map pack. [calls 114, 022]
- Accordion FAQs on a location page were endorsed by Denroi (the roofing page) while the general rule elsewhere is not to hide important content behind toggles. [call 082; playbook III 2.10]
- A state-by-state money page strategy (Medicare broker, Mid-Atlantic) was deferred to a one-on-one rather than answered. [call 073]
- Separate website vs location pages for distant or competitive markets is conditional advice (Albany, six dental clinics, a Botox clinic in another state) with no stated threshold. [calls 074, 075, 117]

---

## 4. Ecommerce pages: product pages and collection / category pages

Matt's ecommerce split: stop trying to rank the store homepage for one keyword; give each featured product a clean homepage section with descriptive text, and push the ranking work onto collection and product pages. The collection page takes the broad, competitive keyword ("paddle T-shirts", "tennis t-shirt"); the product page takes the specific, attribute-based one ("polyester paddle shirt", "18-karat gold engagement ring"). "Collection page is a money page. Product page is a money page," and Matt's emails treat product and collection pages "identically to service pages in every prompt." The recurring diagnosis is that collection pages are "the biggest missed opportunity when I look at a Shopify store" and "one of the most underutilized types of page when it comes to E commerce and ranking"; the book says the same: "Category pages are easily one of the most underutilized and under-optimized assets on most online stores ... Category pages can rank. And not just rank, they can rank really well." Terminology note: "a category page is WordPress. It's for blog posts... A collection page is on Shopify. That can be a collection of different products... You can rank, and you should rank your collection page, but not your category pages." [playbook VI 3.1; calls 041, 022, 116, 017, 001; email 2025-12-08; book ch. 39]

### Product pages: what goes on them, top to bottom

- Name the product what people search, and make that the H1 and title. Book rule 1, "Unique Product Names Are NOT Always Beneficial": "Have you ever heard of the Florpavia couch? No? Exactly. Neither have your potential customers." "The product title is usually the H1 tag and the title tag on your page. Both are essential for SEO. If your H1 says 'Florpavia' instead of 'Mid-Century Modern Sofa,' you're losing the game before it even starts." "So, yeah, maybe save the fancy names for your packaging. On your site? Call it what it is." The proposed fix for client John was to move the creative name "underneath in the description or subtitle"; he refused and cancelled six months later. [book ch. 38]
- The naming cases: a "cream beaver fur hat" under a made-up name became "beaver fur top hat" / "white beaver fur felt hat" (260/mo Canada, 880/mo US, easy); Irish-named candles became "Vanilla soy candle blend - [Irish name]" (~30/mo), named by the forward note, called a blend, other notes in the description, never four scent notes in the name; a generic "novelty bongs" listing became "octopus bong" and the store renamed across the catalogue; a heart-shaped vibrator is named "heart shaped vibrator" ("And then you can have like the actual name, whatever it is"); goat milk treats became "Goat milk cookie for dogs" with the flavour in brackets ("peanut butter flavored") and a statement of who it is for (dogs, cats), which the pages never said; "moisturizing lotion for face" "is what your product is now going to be called"; "horse mug" (1,700 searches, 2% difficulty) beats "equestrian"; "funny apres ski T shirts" not "1027" (an inside joke nobody searches); "single Mother's Day e-card"; worship flags: "whatever the biggest keyword is, that is the easiest rank for, that is the title of it. That's your heading", in the actual Shopify product title, not an alt title; Nikki's H1 "Stink File #1 scratch and sniff sticker bundle" became "Scratch and sniff sticker bundle - Stink File #1" (keyword first, brand convention second "because that's not important for SEO"). Put the made-up name after a dash, and get explicit client sign-off before overriding a preferred name. [calls 012, 013, 084, 118, 125, 078, 066, 103, 074, 119; playbook II 1.5, VI 3.3]
- "Always follow the keyword research and never guess. Never," even against your attachment to a name; "your job is not to train people ... use the terms that people actually use": a UK "yoga rug" seller puts "organic cotton yoga mat" in the H1 and can say "rug" in the description, and copies competitors' naming because "they've done the keyword research." [playbook II 1.5; call 019]
- Long-tail product naming when the head term is too hard: "navy leather ballet flats" with the keyword at the front of the product name and the shoe's own name at the back; ranking #1 for "pewter woven leather loafer" is worthless if no one searches it. Never name products with city names. [calls 058, 012]
- One product, one page: don't carry two names (woodchip boiler / biomass boiler) on one page, pick one and put the comparison in a blog; for synonyms (balcony / demi / half-cup bras) research which term people search, pick the winner, and optionally one page explaining "type one vs type two vs type three", not a page per synonym. [calls 019, 053]
- Novel product nobody searches: don't force a keyword nobody Googles; build content around the underlying problem ("nobody buys my book, what do I do"), write "[established competitor product] alternative / alternatives" pages listing your products, or "old thing versus new thing" comparisons and a "why I built blank" post; a competitor brand name works as a secondary keyword in body copy ("Compared to Suavecito pomade, our pomade is..."), never the primary. [playbook II 1.5, II 2.6, VI 3.3; calls 013, 048, 076]
- Above the fold, clean and conversion-focused: "Product photo(s); Product name (with the keyword!); Star rating (if you've got 'em); Price; 'Add to Cart' button", modelled on Amazon ("product name, star rating, price, and the 'Buy Now' button. Just below that is the About This Item and Product Details section"). Matt's wireframe: "pictures on the left, you have a short description with an add to the cart and like all that stuff on the right. And then you're gonna have a little bit of information underneath that. Underneath there you'll probably have reviews and then related products." Natalie's supplement page passed with "add to cart's nice and big" and subscribe-and-save present. [book ch. 38; calls 117, 041]
- Short description and bullets: "people just want to know, what the hell do you sell? What does this thing do right? What features do I get? Like, they want bullet points, they want easy to understand" (said of an ad copywriter's descriptions that were "overwritten in terms of poetry, but not well enough in terms of SEO"). Punchy copy that never repeats "beard" or "hair" will not rank; the category money keyword ("hair pomade", "best hair pomade for guys") appears a few times on the product page. [calls 012, 076]
- The description itself: book rule 2, "Product Descriptions Don't Have to Be Dull". The two mistakes: "Copy-pasting from the manufacturer. Google hates duplicate content. If your product description appears word-for-word on 50 other sites, guess what? You're not going to rank" and "Treating product descriptions like an afterthought. Throwing something together to fill the white space." The process: "Find the keyword for your product. Analyze your competitors. Write a helpful, original description that makes you the best result on the page." "Treat your product page like a blog post (sort of)": H2s for sections, internal links to related products or helpful blogs, FAQs, "Your own expertise and opinion." [book ch. 38]
- Below the fold: "Everything else can go underneath that. Most visitors may not scroll all the way down, but the ones who do are likely on the fence. Your helpful, well-structured content down there could be the thing that gets them to buy. And for search engines? All that additional context, keyword usage, and structure gives you a huge leg up." Extra SEO text sits under reviews and related products ("add it underneath reviews") or in a details box; text below "all of the important information that people need to buy, check out, look at the product" is fine. [book ch. 38; call 117]
- Headings: Shopify product pages usually have no place for extra headings, so add an FAQ block (theme block "collapsible row" / "collapsible content"), set the rows to heading types, and put keywords in them; or add an H2 inside the description that mirrors the title ("Series one bundle includes"), "it's fine to make your heading two tag the same as your heading one tag in some cases" (competitors' only H2s were "vendor unknown, customer reviews, quick links"). Natalie's page had 10 H2s with the keyword in only one: add it to one more ("Why is our 5-HTP plus formula different?" instead of "Why buy from us"), make related products H3 not H2, make "Directions" an H3 phrased with the keyword ("Our 5-HTP plus is formulated to exclude..."). [calls 041, 125]
- FAQs on product pages: yes, "as long as you can have different FAQs for each product"; identical care/cleaning/storage FAQs across products are a problem ("you don't want the same FAQs on every single page"). Write product-specific ones: "what carat is this ring? ... if you had that ring out and a customer was talking to you, what kind of questions would they ask about that specific piece"; worth the work "to sell more product". FAQs feed long-tail AI searches ("everyone's not typing in just four words anymore... what temperature they play"), remove purchase blockers, and are "good for the bots to know the differences between your product and others." Accordion FAQs with one-line answers should be expanded with the keyword, "Otherwise why are these in dropdowns? They could just be listed as is." [calls 020, 041, 097; playbook VI 3.2]
- Reviews: "Keep the reviews there, because that'll help you sell more. It shows that people are buying it"; "especially on E commerce sites, so on your products you should definitely be asking your customers to leave reviews once they've received the product", with "a system to monitor that", because "Google will look and say this product has you know, 5,000, you know, five star reviews. It must be a good product so we should show it on top." Link testimonial blocks to where the review lives (Google, Yelp, the product page), and make a shared review block page-specific ("Reviews of the Yellowstone Day tour"). [calls 116, 097; course: reviews; playbook VI 3.4]
- Related products: "on a product page it'll be like here's some related products like that keeps Google on your website, that keeps people on your website" (no dead ends); "You may also like" blocks (often four products) count as internal links toward the 10 to 20 per page ceiling; strip heading tags from "Related products", cart/quantity modules, and breadcrumbs. [course: dead-end-page-has-no-outgoing-links; call 007; playbook IV Heading Structure, IV Internal Linking]
- Links from a product page: "If you have blog posts talking about how to use that product or how to clean that product or how to care for that product, you can absolutely link to those from that product page"; a sizing sheet at the bottom is good but "it does seem kind of like a dead end", so add a shop now / buy now button beside it because "Users are at the end of the page. There's nothing left for them to do." [calls 122, 097]
- Quote forms on custom-product pages: keep the full Get a Quote page and add a short form (name, phone, email, short message) on each custom-product page; conversions signal to Google and it removes a click. [call 013; playbook III 3.4]
- Unique descriptions at scale: standard shared descriptions across 1,000 doormat designs: "They should be different. Cannibalizing myself. Okay."; 100 engagement ring designs get unique meta descriptions and headers ("18 karat gold engagement ring" vs "14 karat gold engagement ring"), each optimized around one long-tail keyword, tracked in an Excel sheet; vary wording per item, "otherwise Google just restricts when the T shirts are too similar"; 400 vendor-fed diamonds with only a spec table get researched names ("Canadian diamonds", "ethical diamonds", "engagement diamond"), each a different H1, the unique shape/carat/clarity specs being "enough to make that a unique keyword", the bigger keyword ("ethically sourced Canadian diamonds") on the collection, and a pace of 10 or 20 a week "so you learn before touching all 400." [calls 013, 015, 056, 010]
- Variants and duplicate SKUs: "Add canonical tags. If you have to keep duplicate or similar pages, you can tell search engines which version is the 'main' one... You'll see this often on Shopify product pages with small variations." Hundreds of near-identical size pages (steel corner guards, every size a page): keep the pages that rank and get traffic, "do keyword research to be like, what sizes are people looking up the most and make sure I have those specific pages", and consider a category page with a filter for the rest; duplicate collections with "-1" URLs (couch-cushion-covers-1) get deleted. [book ch. 25; calls 116, 101]
- Seasonal and out-of-stock products: "We always recommend to not disable those URLs"; hide the Add to Cart / Buy Now button or set inventory to zero and leave the page live, because taking it down sends people to 404s or redirects, Google "lowers the importance of that page," and on return Google must recrawl and re-find it; a "one and done" item can be turned off and 301-redirected (passes authority, removes nav clutter); a permanently discontinued service gets a 410. Theatre tickets for closed shows: keep the page if people still search it, say it is sold out, set inventory to zero in schema, offer sign-up for notifications, link to other shows. Sold real-estate listings are "a dead page... carrying down your site": archive (unpublish), keep a few as case studies. Long-unsold designs: "just make a sale category and put things on sale and just get rid of them." [calls 030, 088, 047, 013; playbook IV Migrations]
- Seasonal product and gift pages: "have a money page about holiday gift cards", one page each for Black Friday, Christmas, Valentine's Day ("If it's a product page, that's totally fine to say Black Friday"); out of season remove the menu / homepage link but keep the URL live, with blog posts ("best Valentine's Day flowers to get your fiance") linking to it year-round so Google still finds it. Annual pages keep one evergreen URL with no year; update title / meta / H1 each year (2026 to 2027). Start seasonal optimization about six months out (a florist was told on a mid-June call to start Valentine's Day now; write "gifts for a May birthday" months before the spike); Jill published a blog about a B2B product line before the product page existed and will launch the page with a press release and LinkedIn content. [calls 021, 027; playbook III 2.3, VI 3.4]
- Product schema: name ("black boots"), image URL, brand, a description containing the product keyword plus descriptive factors, price currency and price, pasted as JSON-LD into the page header; reviews and prices feed rich snippets (star ratings, pricing); Yoast's default schema is enough for most. [calls 039; course: schema-markup; book ch. 22; playbook IV Schema]

### Collection / category pages: what goes on them, top to bottom

- Grid first, always. "Put marketing/info copy BELOW the grid, never above it"; "If somebody's on this collection page they want to buy... Don't make them scroll down. I want to see the title" and the products; "I want to buy a tennis shirt. I don't want to read about tennis shirts"; a workbook store had "all of this information before people get to the products... Especially on mobile, I got to scroll past all this... It's like recipe websites where you have to keep scrolling to get to the ingredients... it annoys people. They leave", so move the text "down here" near the footer (Shopify allows it); a fly-net-hat page lost its "we're taking pre orders" block and "Sick of the great Aussie salute" copy above the products ("is this really adding value? ... are they going to be more likely to buy? Probably not"). The book's step 4: "Keep the product grid at the top where people expect it, and place all your optimized content below the product listings. Search engines will still crawl and index it." (A theme may block adding a heading above the category tiles.) [playbook III 3.3, VI 3.2; calls 066, 084, 094, 055; book ch. 39]
- The shape: "at the top you could have a sentence or two, and then obviously the products in that collection. Underneath that you can have paragraphs. You can almost write, like a blog post with pictures and all this kind of other stuff." A four-SKU collection: "you're almost going to write a mini blog post underneath those four skus" (how often people should eat it, etc.) plus FAQ questions, "products on top and then FAQ blog below", drafted with the Rank Week blog prompt ("I'm writing a collection page for ... I need to have some FAQ questions underneath it"). A collection that is "just pictures of products" needs, underneath, "almost like a blog post, but more information about those products." [calls 116, 017, 020]
- Kira's content list for a thin collection (a page can rank with "two sentences at the top", but enrich it): why wear a padel shirt, benefits, differences vs a pickleball or regular sports shirt, history of the sport, what to pair it with (shorts, sneakers, with credible external links to where to buy), and FAQs at the bottom under "Frequently asked questions: paddle shirts", "a sneaky little way of including the keyword"; "I always like to think about pages as, like, we want it to have as much information as a blog post." Leanne: a collection "could have more text here, and then at the bottom, we could have another section with like, more descriptive text." [calls 076, 100]
- The book's four steps: (1) keyword and competitive research (Semrush, Ubersuggest, "or even ChatGPT for ideas"), then check the top 3 ranking pages for structure and keywords; (2) "Write Content Like a Blog Post": keyword in the H1, H2s below the grid ("How to Choose the Best Drip Coffee Machine"), FAQs real customers ask, opinions, tips, buyer's guides; (3) internal links to related posts, products, or services ("Coffee Machine Maintenance Guide", "How to Brew the Perfect Cup"); (4) content below the grid. Payoff: "sell products AND bring in organic traffic." The default layout being improved: filters on the left (brand, price, star rating), a product grid on the right, "And that's where most people stop." A thumbnails-only page misses the chance to "Rank for big, valuable keywords; Keep users engaged; Funnel visitors into deeper product pages; Build topical authority." [book ch. 39]
- Question-style H2s under the grid, not a labelled FAQ: "I wouldn't do FAQs, no. I would just do like a heading two tag with the question, how long does a temporary tattoo last? And then" the answer, which carries the keyword ("our motivational temporary tattoos are designed to last three months"), written differently from the product pages to avoid duplication; the playbook's version: plain H2 questions answered in natural language that reincorporate the keyword ("How long does the temporary tattoo last?", "Can I shower with a temporary tattoo?"), built with Shopify's native collapsible rows, "most importantly, you're using the keyword a couple more times on the page." Answer the SERP's question on the page when the SERP shows product pages: "Where do you get fly net hats? You can get them right here. They're made in Australia..." [calls 119, 101; playbook VI 3.2]
- FAQs on collection pages first: broadest reach and far less labour than per product; "start with collection pages because doing every product is a lot of work"; never duplicate the same FAQ block across pages. [call 041; playbook VI 3.2]
- One keyword per collection, and split mixed ones. A collection covering tennis T-shirts, polos, and hoodies is "three keywords in one and confuses Google": make the parent "explore our tennis apparel collection(s)" and give T-shirts, polos, and hoodies their own collections; don't keep two collections for the same thing (tennis polo shirts vs performance tennis shirts), pick one. Book: instead of one generic "coffee machine" category, separate pages for drip coffee makers, espresso machines, French presses, stovetop coffee pots ("More pages. More keywords. More ranking opportunities"), "But don't go overboard. You don't want hundreds of nearly identical category pages." Workbooks: one page was mixing "Canadian curriculum press", "80 page expanded edition workbooks", math, reading, coding, money (with coding products not even present); build by subject and grade ("math workbooks" 140/mo, "grade 10 math workbook" 20/mo, very high intent, "pre kindergarten workbooks"), in parent language (ages not grades, "PDF" not "ebook"); "Let the keyword research dictate which pages you actually create." [calls 061, 084; book ch. 39]
- More split examples: a "Nutrition" tab ("is that dog food?") becomes "Nutrition supplements for dogs", "Nutrition supplements for cats", "Nutrition supplement bundles for pets"; "necklaces" stays and a new "Murano necklaces" collection is added if people search it; ski T-shirts get a collection per keyword ("T shirts for skiing", "apres ski T shirts"); long-tail buyer-phrase collections (funny T shirts for women / for adults / cat / Christian / golf); a "little black dress" collection named for the keyword; "swimwear" (better: cup-size swimwear) ranks on the swimwear category, not the homepage; "New this season" reads "New lingerie this season" and "Signature pieces" reads "Signature lingerie pieces"; a perfume store missing "mini perfumes" (18,000 volume, 28 difficulty): "I should make a page about mini perfumes." [calls 066, 062, 078, 103, 118, 055, 107]
- Portfolio of small transactional collections when no head term has volume: per zodiac sign, per birth month, "funny tennis shirts"; "horoscope candles" (~110/mo at ~19 KD in the playbook, 190/mo in the call), "Scorpio candle" zero difficulty: "If they're searching for Scorpio candle, there should be a Scorpio page"; multiple collections with similar stuff are fine, with one hub/master post linking to each, while blogs handle "best gifts for Scorpio birthday / girlfriend / mom". [playbook VI 3.3; call 087]
- Reinforce the collection keyword in every product title inside it: "canvas art prints" collection with every product name containing "canvas art print", the category title, and a little text underneath; product names on a Shopify collection should render as H2 / H3 tags (a theme that didn't needed code edits). [calls 055, 100, 076; playbook VI 3.1]
- Brand sale collections: a separate collection per brand ("collection / brand name sale") listing the discounted products, with "at least one or two paragraphs explaining what the brand is, that your products are on sale and something that differentiates you from that brand's actual website"; the brand always ranks #1 for its own name, so go all in on "sale", "cheap", "discounted" (whichever has most volume), build internal links, and a press release mentioning the sale can land you directly under the brand site. [call 030]
- Marketplace or high-turnover stores: "your money pages could be your category pages for brands or for different styles or different pieces of clothing, like different collections"; a product page that appears and "then it goes away. That is not going to work... because it takes too much time to optimize"; a client with out-of-stock products: "we optimize the category pages and... any kind of brand pages." [call 118]
- Shopify quirks to fix on collection pages: the H1 often sits in the logo or at the bottom while the top text is an H2 ("H1 tag always the very top"; "every single page heading one should always be the first heading on that page"); menu items rendered as H2 / H3 above the H1 get changed to divs or paragraphs in theme.liquid and restyled with CSS; stray H2s like "country, region or cart" live in theme code; the collection H1 must be an H1, not an H3, and cart / footer items must not be headings; duplicate H1s come from page-builder apps (GemPages), fixable in product.liquid / theme.liquid via Edit Code (ChatGPT or Shopify Sidekick can help find the code, Denroi is better when available; the workaround is a redundant second H1 containing the keyword plus escalation to Shopify support); one Shopify H1 sat on a "limited time offer" element far down the page and had to be moved to the top; Dawn-based themes append the store name to every title (find the toggle or the "[store name]" shortcode, flipping it changes every page); the URL-edit screen has a checkbox to auto-create a redirect; a URL slug that contradicts the H1 (polo-shirts slug, "tennis shirts" H1) means "people have two different intents", so change the slug or make a new page and return the old one to polo shirts; an SEO app's robots.txt was disallowing blogs, collections, and products (disable the app); dynamic URLs with "?" or "=" are inventory or search pages that will not rank. [calls 066, 097, 094, 064, 007, 068, 084, 055, 059; playbook IV Page-Builder, IV Heading Structure, IV Robots.txt, IV Canonicals]
- WordPress / WooCommerce quirks: a product's long description not rendering on the front end (audit tools see no text) is fixed in Appearance > theme file editor, product.php / single-product template and the description class, pasting a description in if the block is missing; a category page with three H1s including an empty one in the category-description code view; blog category pages with low word count are acceptable if you do not need them to rank (HeyTony's Facebook-ads work category reached via Work > filter; to improve, add words at the top: "here's some of our work that we've done on Facebook ads. Here's the strategy we take", and more items add words); category pages need no featured / OG images "because these aren't getting indexed anyways"; a work category's topic comes from the anchor text pointing to it ("work category, web design... now Search engines know that this is about web design"); category H2s change by themselves as content is added. [calls 072, 041; course: low-word-count-how-to-fix, open-graph-issues, internal-linking, spy-on-your-competition]

### Layout and design (both)

- Reference stores: Huckberry is Matt's and Leanne's reference "for just how to lay out your products" (and its journal); product sites reuse the homepage wireframe with product categories; a model store splits the shop dropdown into shopping sections and gives each homepage section one product with explanatory text ("This website could improve their SEO by having more text on their website"). [calls 008, 100; playbook IV Web Design]
- Ecommerce UX from the book: "there's a big green 'Add to Cart' button staring you right in the face. No scrolling. No searching. It's just there"; offer Apple Pay and Google Pay; abandoned-cart email ("Finish Checking Out" with a 10% coupon); instant confirmation and tracking; a delivery-window message; a post-purchase check-in / upsell email. Bad UX to avoid: an ad that lands on the homepage instead of the product; "20 near-identical products so the one from the ad can't be found"; no wallets; no follow-up. Ads should advertise one product and link to that product page (a bundle landing page is fine). [book ch. 21; call 016]
- Header size: a logo bar taking "one third of the page" vs HeyTony's at about one sixteenth: "what the fuck is your logo doing so big? ... Where's the value?" [call 084]
- Small interaction fixes: make horizontal product-scroller buttons bigger or bolder with right padding; light grey thin text on white is hard to read and "Google might be hitting you with accessibility on this one." [call 097]
- Accordions: supplementary technical detail (specs) in an accordion is fine; "some people saying that drop down menus don't work as well anymore because people are using it to hide a lot of words. So if you can make sure it's not in a dropdown menu, that's fine"; acceptable on technical product pages "if people are using it... as long as it's not, like, every piece of information you're trying to hide." [playbook III 2.10; calls 117, 116]
- The benchmark for "make me want to buy your thing": the Rank Week landing page, where you know immediately what it is, the dates, "reserve your spot" CTAs halfway down, a countdown timer, results shown. [call 084]
- Change one thing at a time: a BigCommerce-to-Shopify relaunch moved conversion from 0.3% to 1.2% and the owner only knows she "changed a little bit the way the sizes were laid out"; if you change everything at once you cannot tell what worked. [call 032]
- Product-discovery tools: a bra size guide should show products under the result, ask a qualifying question ("what type of bra are you looking for?") that leads to a collection, offer a "check out all our bras in 38B" filter button, save the size, and capture email. [call 033]
- No sticky header means the footer must carry navigation, "even something as simple as collection list" linking the main collections, because with nothing in the footer "there's no button for you just to click for them to continue to look around the site." [call 086]
- Platform: Shopify is "the best e commerce platform in the world" (blogs, "all the like schema, markup and all that stuff that Google is looking for"); "if you have a shop, like I would use Shopify, it makes it so much easier for people to buy"; Shopify "is kind of limited in terms of what you could do with design, but you could do this in Shopify for sure"; if a Wix site already outranks competitors keep it and add the shop on the same domain; WordPress has the most options, Wix and Squarespace slightly fewer "but you can still really rank those sites"; Square is very limited (no text over the hero, hover text is alt text not an H1, no true H1 field); Squarespace if few products; WooCommerce / BigCommerce fine but need a developer; SmugMug galleries canonicalize image URLs as duplicates; Ghost handles images and alt text well. Checkout can live off-site ("You don't have to take payment on your own website"). [calls 024, 043, 016, 017, 067, 022; playbook IV Page-Builder]

### Visuals (both)
