- Real product photography, in context: a person using or wearing the item, with descriptive alt text; competitors show shirts in context (snow, ski cabin, snowplow in the background), "Google can now read what's in a picture" and realistic context helps conversion, "look at what these guys are doing that I'm not"; a tennis collection with "no photo of someone wearing a tennis shirt" needs one. [playbook VI 3.2; calls 078, 084]
- File names and alt text: upload "black-flame-paddle-shirt" not "1234.jpg" because Google reads the file name, people "shop with their eyes" and may be on the images tab, and keyword in file name + alt text + on page is "controlling every variable"; every engagement-ring shape thumbnail had alt text "diamond", so match alt text to the shape ("1 carat round brilliant diamond", "cushion cut diamond"), fill the empty ones, and rename and re-upload thumbnails with matching file names ("easier than fixing the code... probably more impact, double win"). Tools: Image Alt Text Viewer; On Page SEO AI. Alt text is "five or six words", should not be used to add words to the page, and on Shopify is edited in the image's alt field or by re-uploading. [calls 041, 117, 125; playbook III 2.8]
- Image specs and speed: a template wants 1200 x 1200 product images while Google pulls 138 px thumbnails and 640 px; convert JPEGs to WebP; Shopify speed checklist: CDN set up, images served as WebP, lazy loading enabled, CSS / JS deferred, delete old oversized images still on the server; "The best thing you can do is use next-gen image formats (like .webp)"; a Fiverr Shopify speed specialist (roughly $100) is "the only time you're going to hear me say, go to Fiverr." [calls 060, 007, 009; playbook IV Site Speed; book ch. 19]
- Product images as links: an image "showcasing a product that clicks through to its detail page" with descriptive alt text; a "my go to cleaning supplies" graphic that was only an image is "a lost opportunity for linking", it should go to a page where people can add everything to their cart. [book ch. 9; call 124]
- Video: brand pages can host supplemental product videos, but video without a keyword target "is not gonna do much." [call 056]

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO (both)

- Placement checklist (Matt Demers): keyword in the product name (usually the H1), SEO / meta title, meta description, URL slug, the first 100 words of the description, naturally throughout, at least one subheading, image alt text, and image file names; the playbook adds an FAQ. Keyword first in the title and meta: "The sooner that keyword appears in your title, in your meta description, in the content as a whole, the more important Google and crawl bots will view that keyword." [call 041, 007; playbook III 2.6]
- Tag fixes from the reviews: move "cute combat boots" to the beginning of the title (adjective "colorful" later unless it also has volume) and shorten the meta to "140, 145 characters just to ensure it doesn't get cut off"; a category SEO title that was just "Bracelets" becomes "authentic Murano glass bracelets", a 63-character meta is extended to about 140 to 155 characters (under 160), a slug typo ("brace") is fixed only with a 301 and "extreme caution" (she once "paid dearly" for a slug change); a title shortened to under 60 characters via ChatGPT with a meta keyword near the front ("Scratch and sniff stickers lovers will enjoy the Series 1 bundle featuring 338 scented designs..."); a sustainable-fashion title carries the differentiator with the lowest difficulty and enough volume (eco friendly, sustainable, organic cotton, Ecovero). [calls 097, 041, 125, 086]
- Shopify meta descriptions: never rely on the auto-pulled excerpt; one store's product metas were pulling the required EU representative / product-warning text, "if it's taking a snippet of the content, that's basically a tell that a custom meta description was never written"; write each one in the product or collection's "search engine listing" / "search engine snippet" box (title tag, URL handle, meta description) focused on the one keyword with volume, low competition, and conversion; "if you can put in the title tag and meta description, that's all you really need. Like, you don't need Yoast for that"; optimizing every page's title and meta at once is fine. Collections missing metas or with over-long excerpt metas get rewritten the same way. [calls 086, 116, 106; playbook III 2.2]
- Every product needs its own title, meta, H1, and headers; two pages sharing the same title, meta, or H1 means Google "may not show any of them." [call 015; course: duplicate-tags]
- Keyword density has no set number: "If they only mentioned it once, you likely will only need to do it twice. If they mention it 20 times, you'll likely have to do it 21... You don't want to over optimize your page by going 20 extra keywords above them. That's redundant." A collection had "leather ballet flats" 41 times (mostly via product names, fine) but "women's leather trainers" only 8 times in one middle paragraph: "your keyword density might not be high enough. Like, if you got rid of half of this stuff and kept four of the keywords, would your density be better?" Big brands' pages are simplistic, compare against them; a tool flagged Leanne's own product page "too high", same lesson as service pages: stuffing reads as gibberish. [calls 007, 058, 115]
- Word count: same rule as the homepage, compare to the top two or three ranking competitors; with 15 to 20 reviews on a product, "look at the word count of the actual, like, what your competitor is doing, minus, if they have reviews, try to match their word count, and then try to match their keyword density and just pretend like your reviews don't exist on that page"; one member's "600 words on the product page is gonna be maybe too much"; Jorg's ~1,000-word collection is "probably around the right range... you could have a hundred more words" against competitors showing 2,200 to 3,400 words that were not visible ("it could be in so many different places... a pop up... when you click on this, more stuff loads"); the #1 ski T-shirt competitor ranks with a plain "T shirts" collection, one H1, and barely any text ("our complete T shirt collection for women and men"), "this should be easy for you to rank." [calls 076, 116, 117, 066, 078]
- Over-optimization: a 4,000-word fly-net-hat category against page-one competitors at roughly 174 to 590 words was "just doing too much... over optimized": repeated variants (indigenous art fly net hat, mosquito net hat, protection print fly net hat) collapse to "fly net hat"; the FAQ accordion (roughly 1,000 to 1,750 words) was removed to draft ("get rid of all these FAQs", don't delete); two H1s (one was the cart) became one; then wait a month or two before more changes. Don't benchmark the #1 competitor if they have a ton of backlinks, "let's try to go for the second position or third position" (the ranking competitor had hidden H3 / H4 link text and no internal linking, so good SEO was not why it ranked). [call 094]
- Hidden words count against you: an audit showed 808 words but only 511 visible, the rest from the pop-up, rewards program, cookie notice, recommended products, cart drawer, and "content hidden that's in the Shopify files or a mobile render only version"; "These are hidden words on your page that if a human can't see it, Google's like, why are you doing this?" [call 125]
- Heading hygiene: one H1, first on the page, not the logo, cart, price, or quantity ("cart, price, quantity should not be in and around your heading one tag"); a GemPages product with two H1s (the short product title shown on the collection, and a padded custom one) keeps the abbreviated one as H1 and demotes the other to H2 or text (two headings back to back with no text between is fine; "Only heading one is limited to just one per page"); a theme coding product titles as H3 instead of H1 is fixed product by product; brand name is not needed in H2s on a non-homepage ("You will rank for your brand name no matter what you do"), and "Why Rainbow City? Colorful, cute combat boots are different" reads "a little bit keyword stuffy"; buttons can carry the exact keyword ("Shop vegan colorful cute combat boots"); Shopify "always messes up their H2s and H1s in their order of things" (fixable). [calls 125, 007, 012, 097, 106]
- Keyword selection numbers: to be worth the time, "if it's a product, probably no less than 30. If it's a collection, no less than 50" monthly searches (lower for a brand-new site); cluster variations (a microwavable heat cap list: 17 keywords, 690 volume, difficulty 14 to 17) turn a 70-volume keyword into a real target, used as concepts (product feature icons like "deep conditioning", "microwavable"), in FAQs, headings, and alt text variations, not jammed into one page; singular vs plural carry different difficulty ("tennis t shirt" difficulty 8 vs "tennis t shirts" 16 to 17, the singular won); "Tennis shirts for matches, practice and everyday wear" is too long, "funny tennis shirts" has zero competition and volume, "if they're looking for a funny tennis shirt, I don't care where they're wearing it"; "custom" packaging (300 pages) uses "custom" where searched but researches sub-intents (eco friendly, wholesale, sizes) with H2s like "custom pizza box designs include ... here's how we do it." [calls 041, 066, 017; playbook II 1.4, VI 3.1]
- Match the SERP's page type: Googling "ski T-shirt" returns collection pages (and Amazon collections), so "people are looking for a collection page and you're trying to optimize for a product page"; "Google wants to see a collection page. I'm going to optimize my collection page for that keyword"; "where do I get fly net hats" returns Amazon and product pages, so optimize the product / collection page, not a blog; check whether searchers want the net, not the hat; yarn stores with small needle selections are still competitors because their pages rank for the keyword. [calls 078, 024, 101, 094, 022]
- Amazon: "you can ignore Amazon because they're going to have shit SEO anyways. And they're only ranking because they have that product and they have so much authority. But we can actually outrank Amazon by having better content"; ranking on Amazon "doesn't necessarily mean those products will rank well on Google." [course: creating-a-blog-outline; book Introduction]
### CTAs and conversion (both)

- The Add to Cart is the CTA: big, visible without scrolling, with Apple Pay and Google Pay; then the follow-through (abandoned-cart email "You left something in your cart" or "Here's 10% off if you complete your order within 24 hours", confirmation and tracking, delivery window, post-purchase upsell); retarget product-page visitors who didn't buy. [book ch. 21, 47]
- Buttons with the exact keyword ("Shop vegan colorful cute combat boots") are "great" ("for buttons, you can just go for the exact keyword"); add a buy button beside any bottom-of-page element (sizing sheet) so the page does not dead-end. [call 097]
- FAQs convert: "people often have questions that stop them from purchasing... addressing them can help with conversion." [call 041]
- Blog links sell cheap products: Matt spent $30,000 after a blog post about "can you start a community on Slack" linked to a product page; "if you have internal links and you have products like under a hundred bucks, people are probably going to be a lot more likely to buy that or fill out the form." Ecommerce backdoor when product keywords are too hard (pre-workout powder): one or two question blogs a week ("why take pre workout", "can you take pre workout when you're pregnant") with "a pop up with like a discount code... that leads them to your product or you can mention your products inside your blog posts." Informational content (perimenopause and sexual health for a sex-toy store) is worth writing even if you can't beat Mayo Clinic: "if they can find the information on your site ... they're staying in your ecosystem." [course: the-power-of-blog-posts; calls 117, 013]
- Google Merchant Center flags: shipping speed and return policy are matched against competitors; either match them or change the fundamentals (raise the price and offer free shipping, "people like free shipping"). [call 081]
- Etsy sellers who aren't showing: "look at how long their descriptions are... what their products are called, how many images they have", and start your own website "because then you control that ecosystem"; treat Etsy and Shopify separately (backlinks to the Etsy shop help it rank; a press release can be titled toward Etsy, "funny tennis T shirts on Etsy"). [calls 118, 096, 029]

### Links, indexing, and site structure (both)

- Menu depth: "if you have to click more than two times to get to that page, it's probably hidden. Those products are hidden"; dropdown menus themselves don't hurt SEO; orphan products must be reachable from the homepage within their subcategory path, with the main categories listed on the homepage; six collections linked on the homepage is good, but take them out of carousels; a product flagged "not accessible" is orphaned or buried. [calls 013, 017, 010, 065]
- Orphans at scale: ~600 Shopify "orphan" flags traced to nofollow footer links (Command F "nofollow" in the page source; product links from collection and brand pages are fine); practical fix: pick the 10 most important pages and add one or two internal links to each from blogs or elsewhere, and turn off any nofollow toggle when creating links in Shopify or Wix. 118 orphans on another store: delete the "-1" duplicate collections; a keyword collection nobody links to ("red wine glasses") is pointless, "if it's a keyword, you can't hide that page... you need to link to those from somewhere", the menu or blog posts. [calls 044, 101]
- URL and hierarchy: "example.com/product/protein-milk/" with /product/ as the subdirectory; category subdirectories /collections/, /shop/, /store/, /products/; breadcrumbs like "Products > Dog > Collars > Small Collars" create "a logical internal link structure that signals hierarchy and relevance"; Shopify's flat collections / products structure changes URLs on migration, so "keep all the URLs and the content as similar as you possibly can", never re-change URLs to fake nesting ("a redirect to a redirect to the actual page, which is not ideal"), and ask linking sites to update redirected URLs. [book ch. 6, 9; call 049]
- Collection-page linking rules: never link the page's own keyword away: "that keyword doesn't get linked to any other pages. It only gets linked to this page... tennis t shirts means 10 pages on your website instead of just one"; linking "tennis polo shirts" or "tennis hoodies" to their own collections is fine; don't link "premium quality ski T shirts" to a blog about "what makes a good T shirt" (wrong signal); the product tiles already give a category page "lots of internal links to other pages"; external links can point to where to buy pairing items, never to another store in your niche. [calls 066, 078, 084, 086, 076]
- Blog posts feed product and collection pages: "if you offer products online or products, you're going to link to a product page and you're going to use the keyword to link to that page"; "best of" articles ("best paddle shirts for rain", "best paddle apparel for cold weather") list your products with an image and internal links using the product names, and mentioning competitor products honestly is fine ("Google views this as an honest interpretation"); linking many products from one post is fine if relevant (funny doormats for baseball fans); but no product gallery at the bottom of every post, "I would link to one product that is relevant to that blog"; every post carries 2+ links to service or product pages; focus links on best sellers and the collections that already convert. [course: the-power-of-blog-posts; calls 041, 020, 073, 086; email 2025-07-03]
- Where to start on a big catalogue: 2.2 million products, "just choose five. Choose five of your most popular ones" as the first money pages to link into; 3,000 product pages "may be too many product pages", one page at a time starting with the most important; with many dead or unindexed pages, focus on indexed, important product pages first (internal links and backlinks); a jewelry store audits collections and the top 10 products first. [calls 116, 118, 047, 020]
- Cannibalization rules: a "natural granola" collection and a "health benefits of natural granola" blog don't cannibalize (the blog targets the longer keyword); a blog on the same topic as the collection would; a blog outranking your own collection (silk nightwear, ranking 10 where competitors show product pages): first "adjust the blog post to feature some of your products", else scrap the post and optimize the collection, or redirect the post to the collection (losing some of its backlinks); when collection, product, and blog pages all target the same keywords, give each page one keyword, redoing collections first, then products, then blogs; a blog ranking for a brand name instead of the brand page can't be forced, "You have to hope that they click on a link. You can try doing a pop up if you want or put a link higher on that page"; product-comparison pages (Botox vs Dysport vs Xeomin) are valid consideration content, not cannibalization. [calls 012, 116, 009, 048; playbook II 3.1, III 2.13]
- Same products in several collections: fine when each collection has a genuinely different keyword and content (multiple horoscope collections), not when "all the collections are the exact same with just a different keyword." [calls 087, 101]
- Sale and seasonal categories: a sale category to clear long-unsold designs; brand sale collections; seasonal money pages kept live year-round with the nav link pulled out of season and blog links pointing in year-round. [calls 013, 030, 021; playbook VI 3.4]
- Indexing expectations: 722 indexed vs 10,800 not indexed after a migration is normal, the not-indexed set is mostly tag pages and categories that "won't be indexed"; don't keep pressing "start validation"; request indexing only for key pages to bump them up the queue; ignore dynamic URLs in GSC and keep them out of the sitemap. [call 035; playbook IV Canonicals]
- Tag pages and blog categories: WordPress tag pages are "dead pages... duplicate content from the blog post... no unique content", HeyTony never uses them; categories only, "10 categories max... Never set it to uncategorized", a post may sit in two relevant categories; blog category pages link from the homepage so every post is two clicks from home; thin tag archives can be kept out of the index with robots.txt. [calls 110, 010; course: uploading-publishing-your-content; book ch. 24, 27]
- The blog lives on the store's domain, on the native blog: "your blog should be on the same domain as your main site... all you need is one link from one domain... merge everything onto your Shopify blog"; Shopify's native blog needs no app (one column, picture, text, text, picture, text; pagination default 6 posts per page, set to ~30, then 60, 100 as it grows; every post needs a featured image); BigCommerce uses its built-in blog, not a third-party blog with "?" and "=" URLs on another domain ("it needs to be on the same domain and like an actual URL"); a multi-vendor marketplace must not let vendors publish their own posts on your domain ("spammers are going to get in there, sell your backlinks"). [calls 091, 075, 065, 024; playbook IV Page-Builder, VI 3.2]
- Off-site: if directories outrank you, get listed on them (free); a nationally-shipped brand with no storefront skips GBP and local directories (Yellow Pages) and copies competitor backlinks instead; print-on-demand stores struggle to enter AI Overviews, what helped Denroi's tests was many low-authority outside mentions describing the products (material, how it was printed, print quality) and linking to them; a whole site dedicated to one product (GutterSaver Pro) outranks competitors' single product pages and is being recommended by AI. [playbook VI 2.4, VI 3.4; calls 062, 030, 012]
- Blog-to-paid-product pipeline: publish the free, shorter version as a blog post and sell the long, detailed version as a standalone paid guide (Jackie's curly-hair guide). [playbook VI 3.4]

### Don'ts

- Don't put text above the product grid on a collection page; "Chat GPT will make shit up. It doesn't know the SEO best practices." [call 066]
- Don't invent product names; don't put the brand or inside-joke name first. [book ch. 38; call 125]
- Don't copy-paste manufacturer descriptions or share one description across designs. [book ch. 38; call 013]
- Don't reuse the same FAQ block across products or collections. [call 020; playbook VI 3.2]
- Don't link a collection's own keyword to other pages; don't mismatch anchor text and destination. [calls 066, 078]
- Don't keep two collections for the same keyword, and don't clone a collection with "-1" URLs. [calls 061, 101]
- Don't put "best" on your own collection page or the brand name in non-homepage H2s. [calls 066, 097]
- Don't hide words in pop-ups, drawers, or mobile-only renders, and don't push everything into accordions. [calls 125, 117]
- Don't 404 a product that might restock; don't put a year in an evergreen URL. [call 030; playbook III 2.3]
- Don't rely on the auto-generated meta description; don't let the theme append the store name to every title. [calls 086, 055]
- Don't use tag pages, third-party blog apps, or a blog on a separate domain. [calls 110, 065, 091]
- Don't over-optimize to 4,000 words when page one sits at 174 to 590; don't benchmark a backlink-heavy #1. [call 094]
- Don't change everything at once after a relaunch; don't change a ranking page's copy because a tool suggests copy that is "bad for our brand" ("if the page ranks leave it"). [calls 032, 069]
- Don't copy competitors' encyclopedic blog volume: "If they have 10 posts on the topic, you only need to necessarily maybe do one because nine of their 10 might be doing nothing." [call 069]
- Don't send ad traffic to the homepage; don't put a product gallery at the bottom of every blog post. [book ch. 21; call 073]
- Don't name products with city names. [call 012]
- Don't external-link to a competing store in your niche. [call 086]

### From the calls: live examples

- Jorg (Lalu / Lari Design, tennis, paddle, and ski apparel, Shopify): a text block above the grid removed, H1 moved to the top, "tennis t shirt" (KD 8) chosen over the plural (16 to 17), own-keyword links stripped, ~1,000 words judged about right; ski T-shirts re-targeted from a product page to a collection per keyword; the tennis-apparel parent split into T-shirt / polo / hoodie collections; Demers' audit found "Ignite Smash performance paddle shirt" H1, keywords in headings, alt text present, only FAQs and file names missing; print-on-demand meta descriptions rewritten. [calls 066, 078, 061, 041, 086, 084]
- Coco Rose London (women's footwear, ~2,000 pages / 1,100 indexed): grid-then-text layout endorsed; density diagnosis on "women's leather trainers"; systematic list by highest volume / lowest difficulty; long-tail product names for hard generic terms. [call 058]
- Samantha (Canadian educational workbooks, Shopify): information before products moved down, title changed from "Prepare with Confidence" to "Pre Kindergarten workbooks, early learning essentials", collections rebuilt by subject and grade; blog merged from a separate Wix domain onto the Shopify blog. [calls 084, 091]
- Jill (dreamtimes fly net hats, Australia): 4,000-word category cut down, FAQ accordion drafted out, two H1s to one, pre-order block removed, each FAQ turned into a blog post linking back; SERP check showed product / collection intent for "where do I get fly net hats". [calls 094, 101]
- Kate (goldsmith, 400 vendor-fed diamonds, GemPages double H1): short title kept as H1; diamonds renamed by research at 10 to 20 a week; products done in French and English before collections, landing pages, and blog. [calls 007, 010, 009]
- Catherine (lingerie, Jersey, BigCommerce to Shopify): seasonal / out-of-stock URL handling, "Prima Donna sale" brand collections, ~600 nofollow orphans, migration URL advice, conversion 0.3% to 1.2%, bra size guide funnel, 722 vs 10,800 indexing. [calls 030, 044, 049, 032, 033, 035]
- Linda (Murano glass jewelry, WooCommerce): "Bracelets" title fixed, 63-character meta extended, slug typo left unless a 301 is possible, three H1s to one; a new "Murano necklaces" collection alongside "necklaces". [calls 041, 062]
- Natalie (5-HTP Plus supplement, WooCommerce): keyword added to a second H2, related products and Directions demoted to H3. [call 041]
- Mariana (engagement rings): shape-specific alt text and file names. [call 041]
- Nikki (scratch and sniff stickers, Shopify): H1 reordered keyword-first, title under 60, new meta, H2 added in the description, 808 vs 511 hidden-word audit. [call 125]
- Rainbow City (vegan combat boots, Shopify Dawn): title reorder, 140 to 145 character meta, menu headings to divs, keyword-stuffy H2, exact-keyword buttons, expanded accordions, buy button beside the sizing sheet, testimonials linked to source. [call 097]
- Todd (Cosmos Within astrology candles): "Scorpio candle" collection pages; "horoscope candles" hub. [call 087; playbook VI 3.3]
- Jackie (candles): "Vanilla soy candle blend - [Irish name]". [call 125]
- Goat milk dog treats: "Goat milk cookie for dogs (peanut butter flavored)", "Nutrition supplements for dogs" collections. [call 066]
- Thomas (motivational temporary tattoos): question-style H2s under the grid. [call 119]
- Andreas (15 to 20 reviews on one product): match competitor word count minus reviews. [call 116]
- Steel corner guards and kick plates (Wix, a page per size): keep ranking pages, research sizes, filter category for the rest. [call 116]
- Yvonne (knitting needles and crochet hooks, Canada, Shopify): audits every product's title, description, and meta with an SEO app and an indexing app. [call 022]
- AJ (holiday gift cards): seasonal money pages for Black Friday, Christmas, Valentine's Day. [call 021]
- Keith (brand-new product): "old thing versus new thing" and "why I built blank". [call 048]
- Goshopia (sustainable fashion): differentiator keyword in the title, blogs linking to converting collections. [call 086]
- Perfume store: "mini perfumes" gap at 18,000 volume, 28 difficulty. [call 107]
- Colin (party balloons, Scotland): rebuild the Wix shop on Shopify, or keep Wix if it already outranks. [call 024]
- Golf cart seat towel (page five on Shopping): meta tags, product page optimization, "best kind of golf cart seat towel" blogs, organic social. [call 116]
- The book's John (Florpavia couch) and the Amazon above-the-fold model. [book ch. 38]

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- Accordions and FAQs: the playbook endorses Shopify collapsible-row FAQs on collection pages while also stating that important FAQ content should never sit behind a toggle; the calls run from "I wouldn't do FAQs, no. I would just do like a heading two tag with the question" to "add an FAQ section (collapsible row)" to "get rid of all these FAQs" (the 4,000-word case) to "Otherwise why are these in dropdowns?" The common ground: visible question H2s with keyword-bearing answers, no hidden walls of text. [playbook VI 3.2, III 2.10; calls 119, 041, 094, 097]
- Same products in multiple collections: "multiple collections with similar stuff are fine" vs "You don't want the same products in three different collections if all the collections are the exact same with just a different keyword." [calls 087, 101]
- Product page length: "600 words... maybe too much" for one member, while the book and Kira want blog-level depth below the fold; no fixed number exists beyond "match the top two or three competitors minus reviews." [calls 117, 076, 116; book ch. 38]
- Keyword volume floors (~30 product, ~50 collection) sit beside zero-volume wins ("Scorpio candle" zero difficulty, "funny tennis shirts" zero competition and volume). [calls 041, 087, 066]
- Amazon as the model for page layout (book) vs "ignore Amazon because they're going to have shit SEO anyways" (course); both are about different things (UX vs content) but read as a contradiction. [book ch. 38; course: creating-a-blog-outline]
- Schema: product schema with reviews and prices is recommended in the course, book, playbook, and Elliot's call, while Matt's emails say it is "not about schema markup" and "Not schema markup" is on his list of what doesn't matter. [course: schema-markup; book ch. 22; call 039; emails 2026-06-18, 2026-05-09]
- Collection-page layout specifics are referred onward: Matt's generic note for Shopify sellers is that Leanne is the person to ask "how should these collection pages look", Leanne said she would cover product pages in a later session, and finding Shopify H1s is "join Denroi's call." [calls 084, 067, 069]
- Almost every worked example is Shopify; WooCommerce gets two audits, BigCommerce and Wix one note each, Etsy a few asides, and the book's Amazon-style wireframe; Google Merchant Center gets one exchange. [calls 041, 072, 065, 116, 118, 081]
- Keyword density: "no set number, match the competitor plus one" (Denroy) vs the density tool flagging Leanne's own page "too high" vs "your keyword density might not be high enough" (Coco Rose), all reconciled only as "benchmark the ranking pages." [calls 007, 115, 058]
- The only revenue number tied to a product page is Matt's own $30,000 purchase from a blog link; no call reports product-page or collection-page revenue lift after an optimization, only ranking movement and Catherine's conversion-rate jump. [course: the-power-of-blog-posts; call 032]

---

## 5. About / team pages

In Matt's system the About page is a trust page, not a ranking page. It carries no dedicated keyword (it overlaps the homepage), it is not expected to rank for anything beyond your own name, and Matt himself does not have one (his bio sits on his homepage). Its job is to prove that a real person runs a real business, so visitors who arrive from money pages and blog posts feel safe enough to contact you. Team, provider, and author pages are folded in here because Matt treats them the same way: E-E-A-T support, not keyword targets.

### What it is for and what it targets

- The page is about the visitor's trust, not about you. Leanne: "when you're creating your about page, it isn't about us, but it's about gaining the trust of our customers... they're really looking to see if you're a real person, can they help you with what they're looking for, and if this is a good fit... it's really about like a trust page. And when people trust you, people will shop with you." The playbook condenses it to "a trust page, not a life story." [call 089; playbook IV Web Design]
- It is not a ranking target: "Your about page is never going to rank and your contact page is only going to rank if somebody says business name, contact or call... So don't worry about optimizing those pages." Jorg's framing on the same call: the about page is for trust, "you can go a little bit more maybe private about yourself"; Matt: "even if you have an about page, it doesn't mean that page needs to rank." [call 052]
- "No keyword needed" rule: Matt "I don't care if an about page ranks or not"; it needs no dedicated keyword because it overlaps the homepage. The about page is "a little bit more about me" and does not compete with the homepage for keywords. The playbook lists About with the homepage, contact, and blog category pages as pages that are not keyword-targeted, not expected to independently rank, and whose SEO gaps usually do not matter. [calls 088, 021; playbook III 3.2]
- Matt has no about page at all: "I put my bio basically on my homepage." Marisa designs hers in Canva then rebuilds it on the site and is "not really trying to rank for our contact page or for our about page." [calls 052, 021]
- If your about page happens to rank (Ariel's did, because it uses her keyword and has a lot of information): "don't mess with that." [call 052]
- A thin about page cannot rank for anything and competes with your homepage for your own name: "there doesn't look like to be enough content on here for this to rank for anything. And because of the nature of it, this would be competing with your homepage for your name." [call 097]
- It is a standard page in Leanne's default service-site page set (About alongside Services, Locations, Blog and Contact), and a member added one as a normal part of finishing her comedian site (menu: Home, About, Contact me, Buy my book, Services, Locations). A homepage plus an about page ("a couple of pages") is enough to submit the sitemap to Google on a new site. [calls 036, 019, 014, 066]
- "Meet the founder" sections on funnel landing pages (Leanne): "good for like service based businesses... if you have like a product, you don't necessarily need to have meet the founder type thing, but if you're doing a course or something like that, then this is important." [call 016]
- Team / provider pages: for a mental health practice with about 100 independent-contractor providers, "I wouldn't even try to optimize for those pages because unless people are looking them up specifically, their website is probably going to come up first. Right. Or their Google business profile." The only keyword such a page can own is the person's name plus "dash therapist" or similar; put the SEO effort into informational searches instead. [call 019]
- Directory expert profiles (profile title "Renata Clark" plus one specialism such as "identity architecture researcher" or "whatever coach"): "unless these people are like highly searched and they're not doing their own SEO, I don't think this is a good strategy in terms of getting new people to your site." Their value is that the experts can backlink to their own profile. [call 017]
- Health niche: sub-pages per team member (doctor, licensed therapist) are an E-A-T idea, but more important is quoting them with their credentials on every relevant page and linking to their LinkedIn or studies. [call 054]
- Author pages: blog posts should show the author with a link to an author page that "has your social media links, that has any certifications... any associations that you're a part of... anywhere that you've won awards"; this gives authority with Google by tying the site to your profiles. Matt on his own: "my author page isn't good, I need to fix it up." His unfinished author page ("just says Matt D... not formatted properly") was still getting 167 clicks; "I should probably fix that." [calls 038, 013]
- "Written by this person" / "clinically reviewed by" author boxes on health content: "I don't know if that really matters all that much. Like sure, if there's extra credentials and you could do that. Doesn't take you much more time." [call 005]
- In the topic-gap analysis, author pages are "junk" to ignore, alongside tag pages, pagination, /cart/ and /privacy-policy/. No email covers about or team pages. [email 2026-08-04]
- The site name (the brand Google shows) should be the business name, not the CEO's name; a developer had set it to the CEO. [call 013]
- The curriculum and the book have nothing specific on building an About or Team page; the book only lists /about/, /team/, /careers/, /press/ among normal subdirectories. [course: universal per-page rules; book ch. 6]

### What goes on it, top to bottom (the structure)

- Leanne's About-page build (Figma mock, Winnipeg massage therapist), in order: (1) an H1 hero band, "About your Winnipeg massage therapist", descriptive, keyword plus location, with one short supporting line (she cut the paragraph down to just "my approach to massage therapy"); (2) a centred H2 trust line, "Helping Winnipeg clients feel better with their bodies", better as "...with massage therapy" to hit the keyword; (3) a half-width, left-aligned text block plus an image ("a professional, comfortable, consent based massage therapy experience", which answers a real worry); (4) a reversed image/text block, "why I became a massage therapist in Winnipeg", which "humanizes you that like, hey, you are a real person. You're not just a stock photo template"; (5) a final CTA block with a button. [call 089]
- H1 line breaks: break the H1 where it reads naturally ("About your Winnipeg / massage therapist") but use a <br> inside one element, never two text elements, or you get two H1 tags; GoHighLevel users can force the break with container padding instead. "We don't need to be too showy about our H1 tag. We want it to be descriptive so people can understand what this page is about quickly. And also it helps Google understand." [call 089]
- The founder story is the core block: Leanne's sales-page advice (Maritza's book) was to tell your own story, "I've been there. I've been in your shoes. I've been nervous. And this is kind of like what I've learned as a stand up comic." Clients want to hear their own voice and personality in their content ("the first time that we hear our voice finally"); a homepage story builds emotional connection. [calls 028, 013, 016]
- A "my letter to you" style personal section gets a photo of yourself next to it, "showing who you are." [call 049]
- The winning cat-behavior competitor had certifications at the top of the page, "her story" as a section, and featured-in logos linked to the external sources. [call 034]
- Anya (experiential travel, Africa): move the founder photo and "about me" higher on the page, call it "meet Anya", add videos of her and of guests in the destinations: "show me that you're a real person... make me feel safe... your industry... people are spending a lot of money with you and they're worried about their safety." [call 088]
- An "Our clients" section listing big-name companies is a trust asset ("You've worked with some massive companies. I love that for you."); set the company names in a brand-like font when the logos cannot legally be used. [call 103]
- A behind-the-scenes section is good; also show finished products and more testimonials. [call 105]
- If you have a published book, keep it on the same website on its own page, link to it, link to the Amazon listing, and "really push the book... does a lot for SEO in terms of authority and being qualified as an expert". [calls 068, 067]
- Diana "reorganized the about page" after asking whether toggles belong there; no further detail was recorded. [call 032]
- The About section inside the homepage (the about job when there is no about page): an About Us section sits right below the hero, "introducing ourselves to them, explaining what we do, how we do it, who we serve"; Leanne's wireframe places "introducing ourselves" and "trust building elements" near the top, and the "introduce ourselves" H2 section does the about job (who we are, who we work with). The playbook version: what you do, how, who you serve, with an audience-identifier layout (icon boxes per persona) so visitors self-identify, plus its own CTA. Leanne's homepage About section is an H2 that includes the focus keyword, icons of the ideal audience, a CTA, then testimonials. [calls 028, 008, 079, 067; playbook III 3.3; playbook IV Web Design]
- Rename generic "About us" headings to carry the brand or keyword: "About Pets Gone Wild"; "Just more instances to put those keywords into the page." An "about her" H2 on an artist homepage is fine alongside keyword H2s; a comedian homepage includes "about you / about me" content. [calls 068, 106]
- The homepage about blurb must link deeper: "you do need to describe to people who you are. I would encourage you to have a button down here that goes to a page, that goes to the about page." The homepage carries the short blurb, the about page holds the detail; Marita's about section gets a "read more about" button. [calls 050, 055, 058]
- A long About block on a homepage should be broken into bullet points. [call 045]
- On a service page, Leanne adds an "about me" section partway down: text on one side, a real photo of herself on the other, alt text "Winnipeg logo designer Leanne Jones." A brand-story section on a product page is fine as is (it had been mistaken for testimonials). [calls 115, 097]
- A homepage that is essentially "about us and like short story" with few keyword-specific sentences is not a money page; money pages must be built separately. A realtor whose about section lives on the homepage treats that as his "go to page or sell page"; Elliot verified its indexing by URL inspection; no separate about page existed. [calls 116, 047]
- If the homepage and about page show what services you offer, visitors arriving from blog posts understand you are, for example, a sex therapist and are more likely to contact you. [call 074]

### Layout and design

- Keep the same colours and type styles as the homepage: "we don't want it to look like people are landing on a completely different website." Use the same H1 font and size on interior pages as on the homepage H1. The playbook: keep the exact same palette as the rest of the site. [call 089; playbook IV Web Design]
- At least one visual element per section. [call 089; playbook IV Web Design]
- Alternate the blocks: a half-width left-aligned text block with an image, then a reversed image/text block. Never right-align body text; minimize centered text. [call 089; playbook IV Web Design]
- Soften pure black text on pure white for long About copy. [call 045]

### Visuals

- Use a real photo of the practitioner or founder, not stock, when it is a real business; the photo is what "humanizes" the page ("you are a real person. You're not just a stock photo template"). Real photos of the actual team build more trust than stock or AI. [call 089; playbook IV Web Design; playbook IV Conversion elements]
- The photo must be sharp: a clear photo of the owner builds trust; a blurry headshot was the first thing Matt flagged on the cat-behavior site. [call 034]
- Real photos of yourselves and of you working belong throughout the site; photos of the owner and the shop on a money page are a unique advantage: "I love that there's pictures of you here, your shop. Like, nobody else can post that." Owners' and studio photos read as "this is them. This is actually them." [calls 060, 066, 100]
- Replace over-used stock humans: "I've seen this photo of this woman from unsplash a billion times. So replace that with a photo of you." Real photos "make you look more authentic and real"; "even using photos that you take from your phone are better than most stock photos because it really showcases who you are as a business." [calls 113, 111, 115]
- The book agrees: "Photographs are a great way to show that you're a real person behind a real business. Nothing builds trust like images of you, your team, your office, or your happy customers. Photos don't always need to be professionally taken; your phone camera can do the job just fine." Avoid the generic stock "same smiling woman in a white coat" every dentist uses; "use your own photos whenever possible." [book ch. 12]
- Putting your own name in image descriptions and captions on the about page is fine: "you're showing that you're a real person". [call 088]
- Video: the direction is "more authenticity in 2026... people don't want a faceless company"; show "Jim's on a roof today" style videos and real photos of the owner and crew. 30-second real clips of an actual team member on site beat faceless content; AI avatars kill trust (about 12 views capped versus about 1,000 to 2,000 once the real person appeared). [call 095; playbook II 4.6; playbook V 5]
- Headshot attachment pages: provider headshots in WordPress create their own attachment pages; disable them via Yoast > Advanced > Media pages (turn off "enable media pages" so attachment URLs redirect to the media itself). [call 013]

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO

- No word count is given anywhere for an about page. The only length guidance: Leanne's finished mock was keyword-correct ("massage therapist, Winnipeg massage therapist all in our headline tags") but "the only differences I would make to make this more SEO friendly would be to expand on this copy, it's pretty light. I would have more copy in here." [call 089]
- If you do put a keyword on it, Leanne's build uses keyword plus location in the H1 and H2 ("About your Winnipeg massage therapist"); Matt's rule is that no dedicated keyword is required. [calls 089, 088]
- E-E-A-T self-audit: after editing, paste the page to Claude or ChatGPT and ask it to score the page on experience, authority and trust and suggest specific improvements; "no one knows the industry like you do," show experience and expertise. [call 039; playbook II 4.3; playbook VI 1.7]
- LLMs want to see you as an expert across many places (guest posts, podcasts, mentions), and expertise and experience should come through in every piece of content. Articles written about you elsewhere should link to your site (unlinked mentions feed E-A-T, Jeremiah the retired dentist turned gutter contractor). Leanne's own about page is where her directory listing / backlink example lives. [calls 024, 054, 055]
- The book's E-E-A-T stance: "it's my personal stories, case studies, and real-world experience that make it unique and genuinely helpful. These elements not only make the content more engaging but also build trust, showing that I've actually lived what I'm teaching." [book ch. 2]
- The curriculum gives no about-page rules; only the universal rules apply (one H1 matching the page topic, title tag and meta description present, a featured image, no dead ends, and its general link minimums, see the disagreement note below). [course: universal per-page rules]

### CTAs and conversion

- End the page with a CTA block and button (Leanne's final section). [call 089]
- The homepage About section carries its own CTA; the homepage blurb's button is "read more about" into the about page. [call 067; calls 050, 055, 058; playbook III 3.3]
- Trust elements double as conversion elements: certifications at the top, featured-in logos, big-name client lists, behind-the-scenes, testimonials, and (for high-ticket or safety-sensitive services like Anya's travel business) videos of you and your customers. [calls 034, 103, 105, 088]

### Links, indexing, and where it sits in the site

- About and Contact pages are exempt from the external-linking minimum and from the one-external plus three-to-five-internal link rule: "Your contact page or your about page doesn't necessarily need" it. [playbook III 2.12; call 112]
- The about page is one of the pages a blog post can link to so the site is not linear: "this blog post can link to the about page, to the main blog page, to the services page". [call 073]
- Navigation: "About" can hold secondary items to shorten the menu (career / hiring link underneath About; blog under about or about under blog). Put employment / careers under an About dropdown rather than the top nav "unless you're really trying to push people applying." A med spa's "Meet the Team, partnerships, collabs, careers, policies" lived under an About hover menu; "most of that stuff, I would say can be in the footer" but keep the About item. The playbook: move non-essential nav items (store, careers, policies) into the footer or an About dropdown. [calls 070, 066, 096, 058; playbook IV Web Design]
- Do not nest blog posts under About: "about-us/news/the-roof-replacement-process" should be flattened; blog posts are "just individual pages" at the root. [call 101]
- Area-served pages hidden under About Us (Scott) was part of why service intent was not being met. [call 109]
- Consolidate cluttered navs: John's SmugMug photography nav had "purchasing, about the artist, contact, Instagram, Facebook"; keep only the top destinations. [call 008]
- Readable URLs: Angela's live site had "meet Angela or about us" pages with readable URLs; her temporary WordPress build showed code-number URLs instead, and "anybody should be able to tell what the page is about just by the URL." Alex's about page lives at a "meet the team" slug; an audit tool flagged it as broken though it loads (sent to Matt's developer). [calls 124, 088]

### Don'ts

- Don't optimize the about page for your main keyword or worry about whether it ranks. [calls 052, 088, 021]
- Don't make it a life story; it is a trust page. [playbook IV Web Design]
- Don't use a blurry headshot, over-used stock humans, or AI avatars. [calls 034, 113; playbook II 4.6]
- Don't let it look like a different website (different palette or H1 styling). [call 089]
- Don't create two H1s by splitting the heading into two text elements. [call 089]
- Don't repeat the same "people you work with" / "written by" blocks on every page (duplicate text), and don't put the author box at the top of a blog post: "Why do we care that this person wrote this post? Like, is that really that important right at the top?" Move it to the bottom and use the top real estate for the CTA. [calls 010, 081]
- Don't set the site name to the CEO's name. [call 013]
- Don't nest blog posts or area-served pages under About. [calls 101, 109]
- Don't try to rank roster pages for 100 contractors or directory expert profiles. [calls 019, 017]
- Don't touch an about page that is already ranking. [call 052]

### From the calls: live examples

- Leanne's Winnipeg massage therapist about page: the five-block build above, the <br> H1 fix, the "pretty light" copy verdict. [call 089]
- Ariel (photographer): about page ranking because it uses her keyword and has a lot of information; leave it alone. [call 052]
- Matt: no about page, bio on the homepage; unfinished author page getting 167 clicks. [calls 052, 021, 013]
- Caroline (cat behavior): blurry headshot flagged; the winning competitor's certifications, "her story" section, featured-in logos. [call 034]
- Anya (experiential travel, Africa): "meet Anya" higher up plus videos for safety reassurance. [call 088]
- Hosting site: about page too thin to rank and competing with the homepage for the brand name. [call 097]
- Mental health practice with about 100 providers: don't optimize provider pages. Directory expert profiles: backlink value only. [calls 019, 017]
- Med spa: Meet the Team / partnerships / collabs / careers / policies under About, most of it footer material. [call 058]
- A realtor whose about section on the homepage is his sell page, indexing verified by Elliot, no separate about page. [call 047]
- Emily's coach site: homepage, testimonials, "work with me", "about me", no blog; add a blog section. [call 077]
- Comedian site finishing with an About page in the menu. [calls 019, 014]
- Jeremiah (retired dentist turned gutter contractor): get the articles about him to link to his site. [call 054]
- Angela's readable "meet Angela" URLs versus code-number URLs; Alex's "meet the team" slug flagged by the audit tool. [calls 124, 088]
- Big-name client list in a brand-like font; behind-the-scenes section; a published book on its own page linked to Amazon. [calls 103, 105, 068, 067]

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- The curriculum, the book, and the emails have nothing on building an About or Team page; the playbook covers only palette, visuals, alignment, nav placement, and the "trust page" framing, and says so explicitly. Only one full build exists (Leanne, call 089). No title tag, meta description, word count, or image count is specified for an about page. [course: universal per-page rules; book ch. 6; playbook IV Web Design]
- Link minimums conflict: the curriculum's universal rules (at least one external link, 3 to 5 internal links) versus the playbook and call 112, which exempt About and Contact from those minimums. [course: universal per-page rules; playbook III 2.12; call 112]
- Keyword on the about page: Matt says no dedicated keyword is needed and he does not care whether it ranks; Leanne's build deliberately puts keyword plus location in the H1 and H2, and call 068 says to rename "About us" headings to carry the brand or keyword. Both positions are kept. [calls 088, 089, 068]
- Author pages and author boxes: call 038 says an author page with socials, certifications, associations and awards gives authority with Google; call 005 says "clinically reviewed by" boxes probably do not matter much; the email gap analysis classes author pages as "junk"; the playbook's trend note ("Who wrote this?") leans toward authorship mattering more. [calls 038, 005; email 2026-08-04; playbook II 4.6]
- Team pages: call 019 says do not optimize provider pages; call 054 floats per-member sub-pages as an E-A-T idea while ranking on-page credential quotes higher. [calls 019, 054]
- Diana's question about whether toggles belong on an about page was raised but the answer was not recorded. [call 032]

---

---

## 6. Contact / quote / booking pages

The contact page is a conversion page, not a ranking page. It ranks only for your business name plus "contact", "call" or "get in touch", it is allowed to be thin, and Matt and Leanne judge it entirely on whether the right people call, book, or submit the form. Booking and appointment pages are treated the same way: the service page is the money page, and it sends the visitor to the form or booking tool; the form itself is not the money page. Quote forms are the exception that spreads: Matt wants a short one on every product and service page as well as the dedicated Get a Quote page.

### What it is for and what it targets

- Leanne: "the contact us page isn't a page that we're like, striving super hard to rank high for. It's. We mostly want to make sure that it is optimized for converting the right customers." [call 045]
- The contact page ranks only for brand plus "contact / call / get in touch"; do not try to optimize it for the main keyword. Contact pages are not ranking targets (Marisa, confirmed by Matt); the playbook lists them as not keyword-targeted and not expected to rank. [calls 052, 021; playbook III 3.2]
- Contact, booking and appointment pages are not money pages because "those pages don't rank"; send SEO traffic to the service page, which then links to contact. A service page CTA into a contact or intake form "is the right way to do it... That counts as money page. The contact form doesn't." The booking page is not the money page; the money page can link to the booking page or an external booking platform, and a third-party booking tool is explicitly not your money page. [calls 075, 074, 114; playbook III 3.1]
- The opposite framing also appears: "Your contact page might be a money page", and booking and reservation pages are money pages ("It could be your booking page. Airbnb reservation page. Sure."). See the disagreement note. [calls 058, 025]
- The only KPIs Matt cares about: "people clicking on the booking page and filling out the form or buying the thing." [call 026]
- Booking friction loses the lead even if you rank. Matt, searching for a therapist: "their website doesn't let me book a thing online. So there's a barrier there. I have to call somebody. And then when I call them they're like, yeah, you have to do an intake. We have to set up a time for that... I go to the next person on the list." Ranking number two or three with easy booking can beat number one. [call 005]
- Contact pages are allowed to be thin: "Another example of a page that could have low content is your contact page... This page is okay to not have a lot of content. Google's going to index this as your contact page." [course: low-word-count-how-to-fix]
- Define the conversion first: "what do we want them to do? Do we want them to fill out a form or do we want them to call you?" Decide the one conversion action and make it the dominant button; the electrician's answer: "I want people to fill out this form over anything else." Bill (caravans) prefers phone calls over email, so his site pushes people to ring and leave a message rather than submit forms. Think from the prospect's point of view: "they might want to book a call, they might want to just call. They might want to fill out a contact form and get more information." [calls 106, 060, 024, 124]
- A booking page alone is not enough: a fertility coach with "booking page, general info page, no specific service pages, and a pop up lead magnet" needs dedicated service pages before anything can rank. [call 119]
- Forms used by existing customers (security-document request forms) should stay indexed if customers Google them: "that's a navigational keyword." A candidate application page (nanny agency) can stay noindex while the "work with us" page is indexed. [calls 054, 068]
- A podcast website's page set: "a homepage, a booking, Work with us page, featured guests, and then maybe like a sample of the podcast" (embedded Spotify playlist). [call 017]
- The book: on service pages, "Tell people what to do next. Book a call? Fill out a form? Don't leave them guessing." Free quotes count as a lead magnet; sticky banners on product or service sites offer "something like '10% off your first order' or 'Get a free quote.'" If someone lands on a service page "but don't fill out your contact form," trigger a follow-up email. GBP reports "How many calls, messages, or bookings you're getting directly from Google." No email covers contact pages. [book ch. 40; book ch. 46; book ch. 47; book ch. 35; email 2026-08-04]

### What goes on it, top to bottom (the structure)

- Leanne's full contact page build (Figma, mock massage therapy clinic), in order: (1) hero / banner, cohesive with the rest of the site so it "makes people feel like they are traveling on the same website", H1 simply "Contact us" (no keyword chase), plus a short bio about booking with the clinic; (2) phone number, email address, hours, socials; (3) the contact form; (4) testimonials; (5) the map; (6) optional accreditation logos and an FAQ block; (7) footer. [call 045; playbook IV Web Design]
- The form goes as close to the top as possible: "We want to have our contact form as close to the top of the page as possible... so people don't have to scroll." Above the fold means what is visible before scrolling (her newspaper analogy). On a Google Ads landing page: "we really want the contact form as close to the top as possible and our call to actions just right at the top. So nobody has to do any scrolling before they can like get to actually purchasing." [calls 045, 016]
- Put the highest-priority contact method above the fold: "If you wanted, like, someone to call you the most, then we probably want to highlight your phone number" at the top and put the form below. [call 045; playbook IV Web Design]
- Reassurance line beside or under the form (Leanne): "I respond within 24 hours of every form inquiry," "a real person will review this," or "Amanda is going to review this and she'll get back to you, kind of personalize it so it feels real." [call 016]
- Testimonials on the contact page reduce hesitancy: "they might have some hesitancy when they reach a contact form, like, this is the next step. And so we want to help reassure them." "You do want to make sure that you're using real reviews." [call 045]
- Map: a screenshot of the map uploaded as an image and linked to the Google Maps URL, not a live embed (HeyTony's site does this). Details under Visuals. [call 045]
- Accreditation / association logos "would help increase trust in your customers." [call 045]
- FAQs on the contact page answer booking questions ("how often should I get a massage? Do I need to bring anything? What can I expect"): "Once people feel kind of reassured that all their questions are answered, that typically helps increase conversion rates." Simple FAQs can sit on the contact page; the curriculum offers them as the optional word-count lift: "Google knows that contact pages don't typically have a lot of words on it, but if you wanted to, you could add some FAQs here to increase that word count." Chuck's Garage's contact-page FAQ accordion is fine, but its text must not be duplicated on the homepage. [calls 045, 007, 055; course: low-word-count-how-to-fix]
- Form length is a lead filter: "if you're getting too many, ask more questions. If you're not getting enough, remove some questions." For high-ticket B2B add "what's your budget?"; to maximize volume you might not even request a phone number. The short inline quote form is name, phone, email, short message. [calls 045, 013]
- Useful intake fields: a "how did you find us?" field on a booking form surfaced "I found you on the Internet." A Gravity form with a pre-ticked "subscribe" box on a surveyor's booking form feeds a Mailchimp list that gets each new blog post; that weekly email traffic to posts is a positive "traffic from other sources" signal. [calls 053, 020]
- A thin contact page that is not indexing (Kai, realtor): "there isn't really anything on this page that kind of is doing much besides the contact"; add "even like a short paragraph underneath the get in touch so it's like contact me like I'm your Muskoka realtor... just adding a little bit more value and context." [call 047]
- Skip filler instructions: on the magician's "book now" page, visitors "are already on the contact page. Like complete the form below. Like, why do you even need to say that?"; show the form on the left or right and beside it call / email / WhatsApp. [call 063]
- Local businesses: include the information people are looking for. Two dentist sites compared: one had "request appointment" at the top but no hours, no location, no phone ("I can't just call these guys"); the better one had phone number, text, email, address, contact us and book consultation at the top and "Open Monday to Saturday." "For most local businesses, it's like, include the information people are looking for and you should be good." An old bin rental site with phone number, accepted payment methods (Visa, MasterCard, cash), sizes, and an "order disposal bin" button was judged enough. [calls 101, 105]
- A form page still needs an H1: on a sign-up form page with no H1, add a text block at the top set to Heading 1 ("sign up form page"), "I always have that at the top." [course: no-h1-heading-multiple-h1-headings-defined]
- HeyTony's own contact page: heytony.ca/contact has a form that routes to the team (the example of how to inquire about done-for-you SEO); its headline was cited in passing as "Ready to rank on Google." [calls 013, 114]
- Keep intake information in one place: Elliot's "What to expect in your first call" block repeated on every page is duplicate content. [call 010]
- A praised intake flow (Ash, cleaning): enter your information, get a clean for about $155, or learn more. A praised competitor contact pattern: clear locality, short copy, "contact us button right there." [calls 113, 109]

### Layout and design

- Form on the left or right with call / email / WhatsApp beside it; the hero must match the rest of the site. [calls 063, 045]
- Button contrast is the first conversion fix: if you get traffic but few contact form fills or contact page visits, "The first thing I would try changing would be your button colors and try to pick a button color that has enough contrast." Button styling that passed: accent color with enough contrast (white on teal failed, dark navy text on teal passed), border radius 16px. A CTA button should not have the same font color as body text. [calls 028, 115, 038]
- One action, one color: too many CTAs and colors dilute the action ("Call now for a complimentary consultation," "download your report," and a blue button all competing on Scott's site); the CTA should be "a different color than everything else on the screen." [call 109]
- Identical-looking buttons must not go to different places. Neri's bookkeeping site had one button to a 15-minute Calendly consultation, one to the contact page, and "Schedule call" to the same Calendly, which confused Glenn: "If they go to the same spot, why are they labeled differently?" Suggested labels: "click to book now" / "book a 15 minute consultation". [call 038]
- "Contact us" as a nav item in the same color as everything else on every page does not read as the action; make it a button, or relabel it "Get a free quote" / "Book a free call." [call 103]
- Mobile: phone numbers and emails must be clickable ("if people are on mobile... how are they gonna write it down?"); pest control location page: "Call us. This should be clickable. If you're on mobile, it needs to be clickable. That's just a conversion thing." Tap-to-call everywhere; for a mobile-heavy audience anchor a labeled clickable number at the bottom of the screen; an icon-only floating button with no visible number tanked call conversions. The book's mobile checklist: "Can someone call you with one click? Can they find your contact page or checkout easily?" [calls 058, 070; playbook III 3.4; playbook IV Mobile; book ch. 20]
- Speed: a "Book now" button that lands on a slow-loading contact page hurts (that contact page also had a blurry video). A photographer's embedded scheduler (connected to her CRM) "was really taking up my speed"; she replaced it with a button linking out, despite the advice that booking should be "as easy as possible." A Google reviews plugin "can slow down your website, which can negatively affect your SEO"; test with Google PageSpeed Insights and only remove the plugin if it is slowing the page. [calls 103, 019, 045]
- Testimonial styling on the contact page: plain text in a box with a quotation icon in the brand color, reviewer name bolded, and stars (yellow like Google, or brand color); no plugin needed. Pasted review text works the same for Google as a plugin, and more review text raises word count. [call 045]

### Visuals

- Replace the embedded Google Map with a screenshot image (WebP) linked to the Google Business Profile / Google Maps listing: the official embed triggers "this is slow" in Google's own PageSpeed test, and "people aren't coming down here and looking at this, they're just going to click on the map that will open Google Maps". "You can just upload an image of the map. Like, take a screenshot of the map and upload that into your website and then make this whole image a link." Confirmed on HeyTony's own site. [calls 070, 045; playbook IV Web Design; playbook IV Images; playbook V 4.4]
- Accreditation and association logos as a trust row; real-review testimonial boxes with stars. [call 045]
- Do not leave a blurry video on the contact page. [call 103]

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO

- Thin is fine and expected; audit flags (orphan page, low word count) on contact, thank-you, ad landing, category/archive, and funnel pages are routinely intentional and fine to ignore. [course: low-word-count-how-to-fix; playbook III 4.1; playbook III 4.6]
- The H1 does not need the keyword; label it "Contact Us." Optional lifts: a short contextual paragraph (Kai), FAQs, pasted review text. [call 045; playbook IV Web Design; call 047; course: low-word-count-how-to-fix]
- Booking pages duplicated per location: differentiate on title tag and meta description by city; low-stakes booking pages for two cities (escape room, Anaheim versus Connecticut) can differentiate on title plus meta alone ("Booking - Anaheim"). [call 017; playbook IV Canonicals; playbook VI 3.3]
- External booking systems (Zenoti, med spa): put the service content on your own site, link to the booking page from each service page, and noindex or custom-domain the external booking pages if they compete. [call 014]
- Search forms that create "?query=" URLs produce canonical noise; the static page is what should rank. [call 110]
- Agentic crawlers now fill out mock contact forms and click menus (Denroy), so interactive elements are seen. [call 015]
- NAP and address: keep NAP identical across every listing and present and crawlable in the site footer; make the on-site business address a clickable CID link rather than plain text (these links exist for the search engine, not human click-through); do not publicly list a home address in schema or GBP for a remote business (safety). [playbook V 4.4; playbook V 4.5; playbook V 4.3; playbook V 4.1]
- Open Graph "type" can describe "is it an article, is it, you know, contact page". [course: open-graph-issues]
- Webinar registration confirmation pages: set to draft once the webinar is over. [call 050]

### CTAs and conversion

- Forms are a ranking signal: people completing a form are "showing Google that people are taking action on your website and that's good for ranking." Embedded (third-party) forms still count: "I think that's fine. Like, just have like a redirect to like a thank you page or something." Form fills from out-of-area visitors still count as positive signals even if you decline the work. [calls 013, 026]
- Thank-you redirect and page: redirect form submissions to a thank-you page; Jennifer's post-booking page says thank you, here is what to expect on our call, plus optional extra reading. Thank-you pages reached only after a form submission are legitimate orphan pages. [calls 013, 085; course: orphaned-page-no-internal-links-point-to-it]
- Quote forms on product and service pages: keep the dedicated Get a Quote page (full form) and add a short form (name, phone, email, short message) on each custom-product page, because conversions signal Google and it removes a click; a "Get a quote" button already sits in the header. Top-ranking pages often have the contact form on the service page itself; one shared quote form fed from every money page and connected to the CRM is fine (electrician, Framer). [calls 013, 105, 073; playbook III 3.4]
- Booking widgets and external systems: Leanne's hero CTA "Book a massage appointment" links to the Contact page, or to an external booking platform (Jane app) if you use one; a Calendly link right next to the price on a sales page is better UX than sending people to a contact form; you can link every blog post to the booking page and even embed the booking form on those pages; sending a "book an appointment" CTA to a separate landing page is fine, and a "Book an appointment" CTA on a service page does not break the one-topic rule. [calls 036, 052, 116, 007; course: every-page-1-main-topic]
- The "Call Brett" critique (Brett's probate page): a "Call Brett" button plus a phone number right beside it is "the same call to action there twice"; a button that instantly dials is off-putting ("If you click a button, you're like, oh, shit, I'm already calling this person"). Replace it with "a contact button here that brings them to a form", or "get a free quote, contact, book a call... Because people might not be ready at the moment. And if they are, they can click on your phone number." [call 124]
- CTA wording names the action and the service: "Book a massage appointment", "Request a quote", "contact a Vancouver roofer", never generic "contact us"; "Contact Us and Book Today are still a little bit vague of what we want people to do", "Start your corporate video today" is clearer; "Start your logo design" ("giving them the quickest way to do that is the best way to convert them"). The book: CTA buttons ("Book a Call," "Schedule an Appointment," "Get a Free Quote") are conversion tools, not SEO links: "that kind of text is vague. It doesn't tell search engines anything specific about the page it links to... Use buttons to guide users toward the next step in their journey, and support those buttons with clear text links nearby when it makes sense for SEO." [calls 083, 082, 098, 115; book ch. 9]
- Hyper-specific calls to action (contact, get a quote, read more reviews) should be buttons, which can be styled, animated and made to stand out; in-content links should be anchor text. [call 086]
- CTA placement on every page: a CTA at the top and bottom; the hero CTA visible before scrolling on desktop and mobile; every page ends with a CTA section ("book an appointment with us"). Leanne's logic: top, "if someone's landing on our website, we want to convert them as soon as possible. So I always like including a call to action right at the top", and bottom, "as they're scrolling down, they get more and more convinced... when they get to the bottom... now we want to convert them." Her bottom CTA block links straight to booking, with the phone number as the secondary button if you want calls too. [calls 008, 067, 115, 079]
- Flow examples: the movers homepage ends with "request a quote" before the FAQs and final CTA, and every service box links straight to booking; a solid service site has "your call to actions like right at the top here. So that makes it really easy for people to contact you right away", plus a button under the hero text linking to the free quote; HeyTony's service-page pattern is a "get a free quote" button that brings the visitor to the form; service pages end with "contact us for more information. Contact us for a free quote."; location pages need at least a "go book an appointment" link; a sales page (Maritza's book) gets a contact form at the bottom: "I think we definitely want to make sure that we have like a contact form." [calls 083, 106, 070, 001, 002, 028]
- Therapy practice: the action is "book an appointment", so the menu and the above-the-fold area should push booking, not the career link; put "Therapy available seven days a week", the phone number and the email on the other side of the header. [call 070]
- Scroll-to-section CTA: give the contact section an ID (for example id="contact") and link buttons to "#contact" so the visitor jumps down without loading a new page; "this works for every website" (Elementor, Divi, Showit, Squarespace, basically any builder). Maritza had a separate, different form on the contact page from the homepage form; either approach is fine, the scroll-down approach just avoids loading a new page. [calls 045, 106; playbook IV Web Design]
- One form per page: "I wouldn't have two contact forms on one page... unless it's like a really long page." If the footer already carries a form (Matt had recommended a footer form on every page), don't duplicate it in the body of the contact page. [call 045; playbook IV Web Design]
- Header and footer: have both a "Contact me" button in the header and "Contact" as a menu item ("Should I also have it in the menu? Yes."); the button at the right of the menu on Maritza's site was "perfect". Footer contact elements for a local service business: a contact button, phone number, email address, plus a list of cities served; adding "contact us" in the footer "might help them with conversion". [calls 032, 042, 081]
- Blog to conversion: "Have several call to actions on that blog page. Whether they're buttons, just general anchor text. Create as many opportunities for a user to go from the learning side of things to your more transactional, more conversion based page." In a post, "if you're looking for a wedding photographer in Hamilton, give us a call" with the words linked to the contact or booking page; the CTA at the end of a post should send people to the contact page, not "leave us a note in the comment section." [calls 068, 077, 103]
- Off-site routes into the contact page: every YouTube video description gets "want us to play at your next event, contact us here" with a link, because people find you on YouTube, Google and LLMs; a social "link in bio" can point to the contact page. [call 042; book ch. 17]
- Email capture on a tool or result page: "if you want to know when your size is in stock, sign up here" or "10% discount off your first order... we'll email you your suggested size." [call 033]
- Answer the phone: "somebody needs to answer that phone" (towing lead gen); missed or unanswered calls hurt local rankings (Google reads it as a bad user outcome). [call 109; playbook V 4.1]
- Fewer options convert better: the cat site's appointment tiles linked to different calendar bookings; Matt still pushed for fewer options and problem-led copy. Make the next step obvious for lead-hungry businesses (Kira). [calls 034, 092]
- Keep the navigation on sales and checkout flows: Google indexed Marla's sunset-cruise checkout page while the sales page had its navigation removed; "add the navigation menu back in... is this website trusted? This is just one page. Like what if they wanted more information? What if they need to contact you?" [call 113]
- Campaign landing pages can send CTAs straight to a tool ("start your claim against Porter Airlines", "check eligibility" into the claim tool). [call 072]

### Links, indexing, and where it sits in the site

- Contact belongs in the main navigation: "you should have your contact page in your navigation menu so that people can find it." HeyTony's nav is Work, Services, Blog, Contact. The book: "Your main navigation menu should feature your highest-priority pages, like your services, products, contact page, or blog", and nav and footer must include "a contact page (because people need a way to reach you)"; the subdirectory is /contact/. [course: low-word-count-how-to-fix; course: internal-linking; book ch. 9; book ch. 27; book ch. 6]
- Contact pages are exempt from the external and internal link minimums. [playbook III 2.12; call 112]
- Social media links belong "either on your contact page or on your footer or something. Or you could even put them at the top somewhere. But don't link out to all your social media" elsewhere. [call 066]
- A contact page named "Inquire" returned 404 in GSC only because the wrong URL (/contact) had been submitted. [call 061]
- Abandoned form pages with a random-number URL and no purpose should be deleted rather than linked. [course: orphaned-page-no-internal-links-point-to-it]
- Lead-magnet and intake pages: build the GoHighLevel form on the website (not inside the CRM) so the page can be optimized and found via search, with internal links plus external links to the LinkedIn company page; internal-link to a lead magnet page from blog posts ("even if it is an orphan page... Now it's not orphaned"); lead-magnet quizzes and internal-only pages may be deliberate orphans, paired with a noindex tag. [calls 054, 104; playbook IV Crawl Depth]
- When pitching local publications, use their contact form or contact page and "look for any press or media" contact. [call 029]

### Don'ts

- Don't optimize the contact page for the main keyword or treat the booking tool as the money page. [calls 052, 075; playbook III 3.1]
- Don't embed a live Google Map. [call 070]
- Don't put two contact forms on one page. [call 045]
- Don't use an instant-dial button as the only CTA, and don't put the same CTA twice side by side ("Call Brett" plus the number). [call 124]
- Don't label the CTA generically ("contact us", "Book Today") or leave "Contact us" as a plain nav item in body-text color. [calls 083, 098, 103]
- Don't use identical buttons that go to different destinations. [call 038]
- Don't leave phone numbers or emails unclickable on mobile. [calls 058, 070]
- Don't duplicate contact-page FAQs, intake blocks ("what to expect"), or review text across pages. [calls 055, 010]
- Don't remove the navigation from a sales or checkout page. [call 113]
- Don't let a scheduler widget or reviews plugin slow the page. [calls 019, 045]
- Don't write "complete the form below" filler on a page that is already the form. [call 063]
- Don't rely on a booking page plus a lead-magnet pop-up with no service pages. [call 119]

### From the calls: live examples

- Leanne's massage-clinic contact page build (form above the fold, reassurance, text testimonials, screenshot map, logos, FAQs, form length). [call 045]
- Leanne's Google Ads landing page: form and CTAs at the very top, "I respond within 24 hours" line. [call 016]
