- Brett's probate page, the "Call Brett" critique. [call 124]
- Kai (Muskoka realtor): contact page not indexed until it carried a short contextual paragraph. [call 047]
- The magician's "book now" page: drop "complete the form below", show call / email / WhatsApp beside the form. [call 063]
- Two dentist sites compared on contact information; the old bin rental site that had enough. [calls 101, 105]
- Neri's bookkeeping site: identical buttons to Calendly and the contact page. [call 038]
- Maritza: header contact button plus menu item; a separate form on the contact page versus scroll-to-section. [calls 032, 045]
- Custom-box manufacturer: short quote form on every product page plus the Get a Quote page; thank-you redirect for embedded forms. [call 013]
- Photographer who pulled her CRM scheduler for speed; med spa on Zenoti; escape-room city booking pages. [calls 019, 014, 017]
- Electrician in Framer with one shared quote form; the electrician who wanted form fills over everything; Bill who wanted calls. [calls 073, 060, 024]
- Jackie: education and breathwork bookings through the contact form after local SEO alone; Jennifer's thank-you page; the surveyor's Mailchimp subscribe box; the "how did you find us?" field. [calls 042, 085, 020, 053]
- Marla's indexed checkout page with the nav stripped; Ash's $155 cleaning intake flow; Scott's competing CTAs; the towing phone rule. [calls 113, 109]
- Therapy practice header (book an appointment, seven days a week, phone, email); pest-control clickable "Call us"; the Porter Airlines claim tool. [calls 070, 072]

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- Money-page status: calls 075, 074 and 114 and the playbook say contact, booking and appointment pages are not money pages ("those pages don't rank"); call 058 says "Your contact page might be a money page" and call 025 calls booking and reservation pages money pages. The emails class "product or pricing pages" as money pages but never mention contact. Both positions are recorded. [calls 075, 074, 114, 058, 025; playbook III 3.1; email 2026-08-04]
- Forms everywhere versus one form: Matt recommended a footer form on every page; Leanne's rule is never two forms on one page, so the footer form replaces the body form on the contact page. [call 045]
- Booking widgets: booking should be "as easy as possible", yet an embedded scheduler was removed for speed; no rule is given on which booking tools to embed versus link out to (Jane app, Calendly, Zenoti are all mentioned as link-out targets). [calls 019, 036, 052, 014]
- Nothing specifies a word count, title tag, meta description, image count, or the full field list of a quote form beyond name, phone, email, short message. The curriculum covers contact pages only through audit-fix lessons; the book only through navigation and CTA chapters; no email covers them. [course: low-word-count-how-to-fix; book ch. 9; email 2026-08-04]

---

## 7. Pricing pages

Matt's pricing page is usually two things at once: a page called "Pricing" in the navigation that answers the question "how much does X cost", and a price range or "starting at" figure on the service page itself. The emails class "product or pricing pages" as money pages; on the calls Matt says a page that answers "how much does it cost" is a blog post in his setup, not a services page. Either way the target is the searcher typing "[service] pricing" or "how much does [service] cost", and the rule is transparency: name the number, the range, or the starting point, or you are "getting left out entirely."

### What it is for and what it targets

- "If anybody doesn't have a pricing page on your website, add a pricing page, because people are also always looking for pricing... that's more commercial intent. They're looking, they're comparing... if you think you're going to convince somebody to hire you over your competition without giving them the price, it's going to be a bit tough." Oliver's "Ayahuasca cost" page and retreat price calculator are the example. [call 054]
- Call it "Pricing": nobody Googles "boudoir investment"; they Google "boudoir photography pricing." "Just call it pricing. Call it what it is." People search "[service] pricing"; brand plus "pricing" ("Spotify pricing") is the book's example of a navigational keyword. [call 049; playbook III 3.4; book ch. 3]
- Pricing pages are money pages in the emails ("product or pricing pages... Anything someone lands on ready to buy"), and the book calls a pricing page a high-intent page: "If someone visits a high-intent page on your website, like a service, product, or pricing page, but doesn't convert," retarget them. [email 2026-08-04; book ch. 47]
- On the calls, a page answering "how much does it cost" is a blog post in Matt's setup, not a services page. Caroline (cat behavior consultant) should put a "Pricing" tab in the top navigation that links to a page answering "how much does it cost to hire a cat behavior consultant" (that question is the URL and the topic), and on it break down "what goes into it, maybe your credentials... why you cost X amount of dollars per hour per service." [call 052]
- Cost questions are the best blog questions: "how much it costs" is one of the best because customers literally ask it at expos; "you can bet your ass they are going to Google and typing in that exact same thing." "how much does SEO cost" searchers "are probably really close to pulling the trigger and hiring an SEO agency. And they're like price shopping." Cost questions ("how much does a brand photographer cost") are the blog topics most related to hiring you; "How much do professional headshots cost" is a fine post; "how much is an ayahuasca retreat" (110 searches a month) should cover high, mid and low ranges plus locations, mirroring what the #1 result covers. [calls 051, 102, 065, 088; course: writing-your-first-blog-post-using-chatgpt]
- Marcus Sheridan's Big Five, prescribed as blog posts: "How much does your product or service cost? What factors affect your price? Is your product/service worth the cost? Cheap versus expensive options"; "Some of the highest converting blog posts I've ever seen are price related". "How much does SEO cost" was the Rank Week Day 1 exercise, and price is the first thing a visitor to a money page probably needs ("Probably price, how long does it take"). [call 108; playbook II 4.3; calls 120, 117]
- Transparency strategy (from Marcus Sheridan's Endless Customers): "be the most transparent business in your industry." Matt published "How Much Does SEO Cost in 2025? (The Brutally Honest Guide)" to "address ALL of your questions about how & why SEO costs what it does" and folds every reader question back into the post. Write the posts that answer pre-sales questions "even if there's no SEO value to them", e.g. "What SEO Software & Tools Do We Use at HeyTony?" and the SEO cost guide. A "how much does SEO cost" post "is good to have on your website just from a client perspective", and Matt wants to show up in AI results for it, seeding LinkedIn posts with a short answer and the link. [email 2025-06-22; email 2025-06-30; calls 102, 099, 096]
- List prices or ranges even as a premium provider: "if you don't have any prices, you're getting left out entirely" from price-shoppers who will shop regardless. Nuance: a luxury provider whose buyer decides through an in-person or call experience can validly hide prices; the test is your buyer's actual decision process. [playbook VI 5.1]
- On money pages: "I know you don't like talking about price on your website, because it depends. But stuff like that, you can give a range." A service page "doesn't have to have the price on it necessarily"; describe the service and invite a free quote. Ariel no longer wanted specific package prices on her headshot page (new system); removing that packages/prices section is fine and will not hurt. [calls 024, 001, 035]
- Adjacent pricing pages that earn their place: a med spa payment-plans page ("we get asked a lot this question") stays in the main nav; a monthly specials page is fine and will show for "[business name] specials"; a telehealth military page exists "because we have special pricing for military personnel" and is treated as a money page. [calls 058, 012]
- The book on service pages: "What it costs (or a general price range) (If you're not ready to list prices, at least address the cost.)"; the chosen HVAC company listed "Hourly rates and pricing info"; transactional pages must "Make it obvious what you're offering, how much it costs, and how they can get it." [book ch. 40; book ch. 3]

### What goes on it, top to bottom (the structure)

- Matt's own "How much does SEO cost" page, in order: a too-long-didn't-read at the top ("quality SEO typically costs between 1700 and $3500 a month. For most small businesses, it takes this long. Cheap SEO often costs more in the long run"); a breakdown by option (Fiverr, freelancer, small SEO agency, mid size, enterprise); what Redditors say it costs in various situations; a cost breakdown (studio rental, camera gear, hourly for a photographer, in the photography analogy); and "here's what you get with those different price points." [call 051]
- The same model given to Jeff (web designer whose pricing post felt like a humble brag): a TL;DR right at the beginning, the different options, what people on Reddit say (with screenshots), an SEO cost breakdown, what you get at $1,000 / $3,000 / $10,000 / $50,000, and results; answer the reader's real questions (basic five-page site, template versus custom, animation or not). [call 074]
- The TL;DR answers the price directly: the range per month, 6 to 12 months to results, cheap SEO costs more. [call 094]
- "Tell them your prices... you can say starting at, you know, whatever the price is, and then break it down based on the different tiers you have or if you wanted to add this on." [call 051]
- Packages must be concrete: the string quartet's "packages and pricing" copy ("explore a variety of live music services and packages") is "very vague"; lay out quartet / trio / duo / single with rates per event type, ideally on their own pages. [call 061]
- Pricing calculators: "write that blog post, give them the actual pricing or give them like a pricing calculator... ChatGPT can make one for you, or Claude... calculators that you can just embed on your website" (movers, "how much do movers cost in Toronto"); Oliver's retreat price calculator. [calls 101, 054]
- What is included goes with the price: the Yellowstone tour page shows $5,000 to 6,000 but not what is included or group size; Melissa's competitor shows a payment plan (50% deposit) and "what's included" on the service page as proof elements; the top cat-behavior competitor lists a "payment method" block near the bottom of the page. [calls 084, 081, 034]
- A cost or comparison table on a service page "always works fantastic for Google". [call 082]
- Value framing: state a value like "$100 of value, free," but "don't make it unrealistic... I have to back that up" (is it normally sold at that price, or are bonuses creating the value); on a PR-services ad landing page, frame the cost against hiring a full-time employee ("the cost might seem high... but it's actually way less compared to hiring someone full time"); an agency page saying "Saves you thousands for just 25 bucks a month. Here's what you get" still needed to state what the service actually is. [calls 005, 016, 105]
- Real evidence: cost content should show "the real screenshots of what it costs" (Victoria's state-specific cost pages); Bill's brochure-style site offers "information, get price list and stuff like that." [call 024]
- Send pricing seekers to the pricing page rather than stuffing packages into the service page: ceramic coating page CTA "Book an appointment", then "go to the pricing page, for example". [call 066]

### Layout and design

- Nothing in the playbook or curriculum is specific to pricing-page layout, tables, or tiers; the playbook's pricing material stops at naming, transparency, linking and schema and says nothing else is specific to pricing-page layout. The only layout cues are the pieces of Matt's cost page (TL;DR, option breakdown, Reddit screenshots, bulleted cost breakdown, tier comparison) and the quartet advice to lay packages out per ensemble and event type. [playbook III 3.4; playbook VI 5.1; calls 051, 074, 061]
- Regina's header navigation reads "Home, About, Packages and Pricing"; Matt did not object to the pricing item, he focused on adding Occasions and Ensembles. [call 042]

### Visuals

- HeyTony's "How much does SEO cost in 2026" post uses a chart, Reddit screenshots, a bulleted cost breakdown ("here's what you're paying for"), and videos; "it makes sense to have more images depending on the content." Real screenshots of actual costs are the evidence layer. [calls 102, 099, 024]

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO

- Meta description: Jennifer's "WordPress website design and pricing" page was missing one; add it (unlike legal pages). [call 078]
- No year in the URL; change the year in the title each year ("How much does SEO cost in 2026"). [call 074]
- Keep the numbers current: Matt updated his prices but not the blog post, and clients demanded the old price; "if you're changing your pricing, make sure that you're updating it everywhere". He would write "these are our prices" rather than "as of March 2026". Leads quote the page to his sales team, so it has to stay current. [calls 065, 051]
- Verify AI-written pricing claims before publishing: "hourly 20 to 200 an hour for SEO? It's probably not 20 to 200 an hour, right? Project based. We don't do any." Remove anything that does not make sense. [course: writing-your-first-blog-post-using-chatgpt]
- AI Overview citation pattern for price claims: state the consensus figure first, then pivot: "The average kitchen renovation costs $50-75,000 in [area]. However, we can do it for $25,000." Writing only "we do it for $25-35K" will not get cited. [playbook II 4.7; playbook V 7.2]
- Schema: prices (and reviews) need a third-party schema tool beyond Yoast's standard schema; manual JSON-LD Product-type schema can carry prices if you choose to add it (paste into the header); schema can expose "product pricing" in rich snippets; GBP should include "services, pricing". [call 072; playbook IV Schema; book ch. 22; book ch. 18]
- Different pricing in different currencies is one of the only reasons to use country-tagged URLs; location pages where only the fee differs are still separate pages, "just change up the wording." [calls 020, 106]
- No backlinks are needed for a keyword like "how much do movers cost in Toronto". [call 101]

### CTAs and conversion

- Put the price on the CTA where possible: for the lien-filing CTA, "file your lien today, $10", so the shortcut is obvious. [call 081]
- A Calendly link right next to the price on a sales page beats sending people to a contact form. [call 052]
- Retarget visitors who hit the pricing page and do not convert. [book ch. 47]

### Links, indexing, and where it sits in the site

- Navigation: a "Pricing" tab in the top nav (Caroline); "Packages and Pricing" in the header (Regina); the payment-plans page in the main nav (med spa); "pricing or the book now" is a normal nav item a visitor clicks and must stay on the same domain and look. The book: nav and footer "can also include links to pricing, case studies, or anything else that directly supports conversions." [calls 052, 042, 058, 114; book ch. 27]
- A pricing money page can link to a relevant blog post and vice versa. [playbook IV Internal Linking]
- Pages with prices or packages found in backlink-gap lists are often paid directories; "you might not want to do all these." [course: backlinks-gap]
- Competitor pricing changes can be watched with HeyTony Watchdog ("if they're changing their pricing, if they're doing anything"; before/after diff; "Updating pricing" is one of the tracked changes). [course: bonus-spy-on-your-competition; playbook VI 1.4; book ch. 34]

### Don'ts

- Don't call it "Investment" or any euphemism. [call 049]
- Don't hide prices entirely unless your buyer genuinely decides in person or on a call. [playbook VI 5.1; call 054]
- Don't write vague package copy ("explore a variety of live music services and packages"). [call 061]
- Don't leave stale prices anywhere once you change them, and don't date them "as of March 2026". [call 065]
- Don't put the year in the URL. [call 074]
- Don't publish unverified AI price ranges. [course: writing-your-first-blog-post-using-chatgpt]
- Don't write a pricing post that reads as a humble brag (Jeff). [call 074]
- Don't claim unrealistic value you cannot back up. [call 005]
- Don't show a price without what is included (Yellowstone's $5,000 to 6,000 with no inclusions or group size). [call 084]

### From the calls: live examples

- Matt's "How much does SEO cost" page: TL;DR ($1,700 to $3,500 a month), option breakdown, Reddit, cost breakdown, tiers; leads quote it to sales; the stale-price incident. [calls 051, 074, 094, 065, 102, 099]
- Lily's boudoir site: "Investment" renamed "Pricing". [call 049]
- Caroline (cat behavior): "Pricing" tab to a "how much does it cost to hire a cat behavior consultant" page. [call 052]
- Oliver: "Ayahuasca cost" page and retreat price calculator; "how much is an ayahuasca retreat" at 110 a month. [calls 054, 088]
- Regina's quartet site: "Packages and Pricing" in the header nav; the string quartet "packages and pricing" page with the vague "explore a variety of live music services and packages" copy. [calls 042, 061]
- Toronto movers: cost post with embedded calculator, no backlinks needed. [call 101]
- Jeff's web-design pricing post; Jennifer's pricing page missing a meta description. [calls 074, 078]
- Ariel removing package prices from her headshot page. [call 035]
- Med spa payment-plans and specials pages; telehealth military pricing page. [calls 058, 012]
- Yellowstone tour price without inclusions; Melissa's competitor's 50% deposit and "what's included"; the cat-behavior competitor's payment-method block; the lien CTA "file your lien today, $10". [calls 084, 081, 034]
- Victoria's state-specific cost pages with real screenshots; Bill's price list. [call 024]

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- This is the thinnest of the four page types. The curriculum does not teach a pricing page; the playbook has no layout, table, or length guidance and says so; the book only asks that cost be addressed on service pages; the emails give the transparency rule and the money-page classification. No word count, H2 list, image count, or table format exists for a standalone pricing page; everything structural comes from Matt's own "How much does SEO cost" post. [course: writing-your-first-blog-post-using-chatgpt; playbook III 3.4; book ch. 40; email 2025-06-22]
- Blog post or money page: Matt says a "how much does it cost" page is a blog post in his setup, not a services page; the emails class "pricing pages" as money pages and "guides" and "comparisons" as content pages. Both are kept. [call 052; email 2026-08-04]
- Show prices or not: call 054 says everyone should add a pricing page; the playbook allows luxury providers to hide prices; Ariel was told removing her package prices would not hurt. [call 054; playbook VI 5.1; call 035]
- Dating the page: no year in the URL but the year in the title, versus writing "these are our prices" rather than "as of March 2026"; the two are compatible but come from different calls. [calls 074, 065]

## 8. FAQ pages and per-page FAQ blocks

FAQs in Matt's system are blocks, not a page: three or four questions tied to the one focus keyword of the page they sit on, written as plain question-then-answer text, answered in the first sentence, and linked onward to the fuller blog post or the money page. A central FAQ page is discouraged because "what are we ranking for?"; each real customer question that needs more than a paragraph becomes its own blog post instead. FAQs are content, not money pages, and Matt's testing puts FAQ schema and accordion formatting far below the quality of the answer itself.

### What it is for and what it targets

- One FAQ block per page, tied to that page's keyword. Leanne: "I'd like to include an FAQ on every page because it's a good way to include answering questions on that focus keyword of the page because every page should have one focus keyword... If you have all your FAQs on one page, what are we ranking for?" [call 028; playbook III 2.10; playbook IV Conversion elements]
- No central FAQ page: "I would have FAQs on all of your pages, but have them relevant to the topic of the page" (deep tissue massage FAQs at the bottom of the deep tissue massage page). "We don't want to have the same FAQs on each page. We want to make sure that each page has unique text on each page... Google doesn't like that." Yaniv's rebuild: "Every page should not should, but could have their own FAQs." [calls 045, 004]
- An FAQ section on a service page lets you place a long-tail question exactly while the page targets the shorter keyword; an accordion on a thin section "really pads up the keyword density and helps this page rank for more keywords while associating it more to the location". [calls 074, 082]
- FAQs are content pages, not money pages, in the email taxonomy; each real customer question is a blog post, not an FAQ line: answer it "Like you'd explain it to a friend over coffee." [email 2026-08-04; email 2026-05-09]
- FAQ or blog post? Denroy's test: "can the question be answered in one paragraph or does it need more information? Does it need visuals? Will someone benefit from having longer form content? If the answer is yes, turn it into a blog. If the answer is something simple, well, you can keep it as an FAQ on your homepage, service or contact page." [call 007]
- "Turning your FAQs into blog posts. This is one way to create content ideas for your website." Matt's own blog has a post on FAQs turned into blog posts (suggested as an internal-link target for Joyce, who built an FAQ page). A FAQ question can be expanded into a blog post if its keyword differs from the page's keyword; each standalone FAQ question can become its own post. Recurring customer questions (summer camp: "do we have to pick our kids up between sessions") are worth a blog post even if answered everywhere: it saves support time, and sending people that link brings non-Google traffic that builds trust with Google. [course: internal-linking; calls 108, 092, 065; playbook V 7.3]
- Conversion purpose (Leanne): on landing pages "I like to include our FAQs, kind of like handle objections, just answer any questions that people might have just to help reassure them that this is the right choice"; on funnel pages "FAQ section. I always love including FAQs. Answer people's biggest concerns and questions. This also helps reassure people and reduce anxiety." On the homepage an FAQ section eases anxieties about booking or buying, "it's also great for SEO because now you can answer people's questions. And that's what Google wants to see", and "it's a good point to add to increase your word count without it being a big wall of text." [calls 016, 067, 079]
- Expectation-setting: FAQ-style Q&A on a tour page pre-empts problems ("Am I guaranteed to see a wolf during a wolf tour in Yellowstone? No, we can't control the animals..."); summer art camps: "FAQs looks great", another way to give parents information while "putting all those keywords in". [calls 084, 072]
- Money pages, collections, products: the book wants an FAQ section on every service page ("Common questions (Yes, add an FAQ section.)"), "FAQs for common customer questions" on product pages, and "Include FAQs that real customers ask." on category pages. Ecommerce: FAQ blocks on collection and product pages for long-tail / AI context and conversion; start with collections; "products on top and then FAQ blog below"; product-page FAQs are fine if unique per product; FAQs on an ecommerce homepage are not where to focus (prioritize collections and products). [book ch. 40; book ch. 38; book ch. 39; calls 041, 017, 020]
- Contact page FAQs answer booking questions and lift conversion and word count (detail in the Contact chapter). [calls 045, 007; course: low-word-count-how-to-fix]
- Adding a couple of FAQs is one of the "small changes" to make to a non-ranking money page; Laura (Hire Dragons) keeps adding FAQs to current pages, which is fine, but expect temporary ranking wobble. [calls 022, 029]
- AEO: a member "started adding FAQs in our content and... optimizing our content as per the answer base"; Matt: "answer engine optimization... it's all just SEO. If you rank on Google, your odds of being recommended in the AI results are a lot higher." His study of 100+ AI Overviews: "it's not about formatting, it's not about like FAQs or schema or anything like that. It's more, does your content agree with the general consensus that AI overviews is already talking about... and then do you have your additional expertise underneath that?" The emails: "It's not about schema markup or advanced strategies." [calls 020, 029; email 2026-06-18]
- Question-shaped headings win snippets: "when you use H2 tags and when you're answering questions, Google might show whatever your, you know, first paragraph is or that relevant information on a page to the user right in the search results... it could help you jump right to number one." The book: "When your headings align with commonly searched questions or phrases, they can boost your chances of landing in rich snippets (aka rich results) or AI-generated overviews in search results... putting your page front and center, even above the traditional #1 spot." [course: heading-tags; book ch. 7]

### What goes on it, top to bottom (the structure)

- Count: 3 to 4 per page. "If you have 12 FAQs, that's too many... typically it's like three or four FAQs for a specific topic"; Matt: "FAQs should be limited to like three to four questions maybe. But I haven't been using them in blog posts." If you have 12, split them onto a dedicated page or spin each into a blog post. [calls 058, 091; playbook II 4.7; playbook III 2.10; playbook V 7.3]
- Questions are H2 tags with paragraph text underneath: "These should be like H2s and then just paragraph text underneath"; "Matt recommends using H2 tags for the FAQs" (Elementor lets you set accordion titles to H2 or H3). Write plain question then answer, no literal "Q:" / "A:" prefix; in the AI markdown view of a page it is just question then answer ("I don't even know if you need Q and A") so Google and AI "can pull from that". [calls 032, 058, 115, 094; playbook III 2.10]
- Answer-first, then link: keep answers short, answer right away, then "if you want more information, check out our full blog post about this topic"; the blog links on to the product or service page (the FAQ to blog to money page chain). "we like if... you have, like, a brief description of the answer, or, like, you have the answer, and if somebody wants to learn more, they can click over to, like, the full piece of content." Linking to a money page is fine; if the answer lives on the money page, link with a hashtag anchor URL that scrolls to the answer. Homepage FAQ entries: a question, a couple of sentences, then a link to the full blog breakdown. "there's no way to know how far they crawl on every page, which is why I like to put the answer to whatever the topic is right at the top." [calls 058, 043, 021, 013; playbook III 2.10]
- Homepage Q&A block (Denroy): short, focus keyword plus location in every question header, internal links under each answer to the pages you want to rank. [call 030]
- Write questions the way people ask them ("How long do temporary tattoos last?", "Do temporary tattoos come off in the shower?"), not forced to contain the full keyword every time; on a collection page Matt prefers question-style H2s over a labeled FAQ block: "Would you do FAQs under the guise of question answer? I wouldn't do FAQs, no. I would just do like a heading two tag with the question... and then" the answer. Shopify: FAQs on collection pages first (broadest reach), as plain H2 questions (not labeled "FAQ") in natural language that reincorporates the keyword ("How long does the temporary tattoo last?"), built as the native collapsible-row component; product-specific FAQs for near-identical SKUs. Kira: FAQs at the bottom of collection pages with the keyword in the FAQ heading. [calls 119, 076; playbook VI 3.2]
- Don't stuff keywords into FAQs, "make sure that you're actually answering the question." [call 058]
- One-line accordion answers are useless: expand them and include the keyword, or do not hide them in dropdowns at all. [call 097]
- Lists where they fit: dumpster page "prohibited, what is allowed, what's not allowed, that's fine. You could probably do bullet points." [call 105]
- Blog posts: Matt's content engine ends each blog with FAQs; Christopher's format is a short video answering the question at the top, a quick summary under the video, the article, FAQ questions at the bottom; a "what does a contractor do" post can use FAQ-style H2s such as warning signs / "what does a contractor not do." But don't stack "Key takeaways", "FAQ" and "Conclusion" as three endings that restate the same thing; keep key takeaways separate from the FAQ block, and address the FAQ points in the content or keep one closing block. [calls 034, 039, 107, 081, 094; playbook V 7.3]
- Tony on "10 FAQs at the bottom of every blog post helps show up in ChatGPT": "No. Like what's going to be helpful for a human? Just listing FAQs at the bottom after your call to action does not help SEO. You could have some FAQs in there. But realistically is that gonna be helpful to a human being? Probably not. But if it's like an FAQ style piece of content, that might." [call 069; playbook II 4.7]
- Sourcing the questions: Google's People Also Ask (click a question and close it to reveal more; pick four distinct questions such as "What is hot sauce made of," "What is the healthiest hot sauce", not near-duplicates); answerthepublic.com (3 free searches a day with an account, 1 without; use list view; copy three questions); HeyKeywords Find Questions (scans Reddit, Quora, Facebook and People Also Ask and clusters them; manicures: "why do some nail salons charge more for manicures?", "what should I look for in a clean, high quality nail salon?", "how long do I typically wait at a good nail salon?"; "most of these are really easy to rank for"); Looker Studio "content optimization ideas" quadrants (why / what / how / can: if "why can't I post on Instagram" has 1,500 impressions, "maybe I should write a blog post or add that to the page that's ranking for that keyword"); the last 10 questions in your inbox and Findquestions.com; Reddit-sourced questions; questions customers ask in person, on social, by email or on a sales call; Marcus Sheridan's model questions ("How much does a fiberglass pool cost?", "Fiberglass vs. concrete pools, which is better?", "What are the problems with fiberglass pools?"); record yourself answering "the questions your customers ask you all the time," transcribe, and turn it into content. [call 114; book ch. 36; course: how-to-find-keywords-your-customers-are-searching-for; course: find-questions; course: tracking-your-growth; email 2026-05-09; playbook II 1.3; playbook V 6; book ch. 2; book ch. 14]
- Competitor comparisons inside FAQs (Botox vs Dysport) may be what helps a competitor rank; Matt: "Sure, maybe", and framed comparison as separate consideration content. [call 048]

### Layout and design

- Placement: near the bottom, before the final CTA. Leanne's homepage and funnel wireframes put FAQs after the process section and just before the final CTA; the movers homepage goes "request a quote", then FAQs, then final CTA; a service-page FAQ is built near the bottom before the final CTA; Marita's homepage ends with FAQs before the footer (approved); the electrician homepage has "our FAQs at the bottom here" among things done right; Mark (Squarespace consulting firm) has FAQs "towards the bottom of every page"; FAQs can sit low on the page. Counter-note: Melissa's FAQs were "pushed to the bottom" under too much content while the ranking competitor surfaces questions higher, and Gail's bottom-versus-top question was framed as a CRO / design test, not an SEO rule. [calls 008, 016, 028, 083, 115, 058, 100, 023, 081, 024]
- Plain text versus accordion, Matt's side: Yaniv's rebuild: "Don't put them in a drop down though. Don't do the toggles. Just have them like question answer underneath. Question answer underneath." Accordion design? "No, no, I think Google and AI are less likely to look at that than if you just have it on the page because you're making more work for them." Matt elsewhere: "having drop downs like this... can we just not do drop downs anymore... just have all of these open basically"; "when AI and Google's crawling your site, they're not necessarily opening up all the dropdowns"; "if you're technically hiding text in toggles, it's not going to be as effective" (the hey Tony site uses lots of toggles, menus and side-swipes that he wants to move away from; "it's fine in some cases, but not in every case"); after testing dropdown Q&As: "It's better if it's not in a box... if this information is so important, why is it hidden in a box?"; "I don't like all the toggles and pop downs"; on a pet store site: "let's open up these FAQs. Great." The playbook: never hide FAQs behind an accordion for genuinely important SEO content; supplementary technical detail (product specs) inside an accordion is fine, and accordions are acceptable to pad thin word count where the expanded content is genuinely substantive. [calls 004, 032, 034, 116, 091, 068; playbook III 2.10; playbook IV Conversion elements]
- Plain text versus accordion, the other side: Denroy: "I've used accordion toggle menus on over 100 sites now. They all rank." Google does a visual crawl, then a raw HTML crawl where it sees toggled content, plus agentic AI crawling that clicks into menus. Leanne's balance: SEO blogs say keep FAQs open for the crawler; about five can be open without overwhelming; with ten long ones use an accordion; four FAQs can be laid out in two columns; accordions add word count without visual bulk; her own service page FAQ is an accordion. The curriculum recommends FAQ dropdowns as the way to raise homepage word count: "if you look down here at the FAQs, we did the same kind of thing. So we listed out the services that we offer there. Do we do organic, social, you know, each one of these has more information." [calls 015, 028, 055, 115; course: word-count]
- Enclosed / boxed FAQ formatting repeated across Shopify blog posts is fine as long as the FAQ text itself differs per post. [call 001]
- A FAQ link inside a section should become a button. [call 060]

### Visuals

- FAQ sections do not need a photo; "as long as you have some photos and it's not just like a huge wall of text." [call 100]
- No other visual guidance exists for FAQ blocks; the relevant rule is that they are the word-count lever that avoids a wall of text. [calls 067, 079]

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO

- Too many FAQs can hurt: Jill's category-page FAQs added about 1,000 to 1,750 words to a page that competitors win with a few hundred; remove them (draft) and spin each question into a blog post that links back. Compactors page: "why are there so many FAQs on here?... You don't even need to have FAQs... if you're trying to get to a word count, sure"; the top competitor's FAQs "are not really long, which is good." A FAQ block at the bottom of a collection page plus quotes plus text may be too much; check density, not just volume. Long FAQ sections on treatment pages are fine. Ask first "is this page ranking?" before judging a page's FAQs. [calls 094, 105, 058, 092]
- Unique text per page: never repeat identical FAQ text verbatim across pages (not the contact-page set again on the homepage); on service and location pages ask "are FAQs necessary on this page? Are they unique on this page compared to your other pages?" Counter-note: on one call the same FAQ block repeated on every page "is probably fine" while identical review text "is likely showing up as duplicate content on your site." [calls 045, 055, 061, 020, 096; playbook III 2.10]
- The keyword placement checklist includes "an FAQ" as one of the places the keyword should appear; question headers on the homepage carry focus keyword plus location. [playbook III 2.6; call 030]
- Static FAQs are among the "paragraphs, text blocks, FAQs" that a dynamic page lacks and needs. [call 110]
- FAQ schema stance: Matt: "I haven't found that they're any more or less useful than using them or not using them"; deprioritized by his own testing (after a year, no measurable difference); skipping it carries no penalty; only worth the manual dev effort for high-competition keywords. Denroi: "You will not be punished for not doing this... The impact of it is very minimal. So if you're going for a high competition keyword, do it. If not, it's not the end of the world." Yoast's schema toggle covers standard schema on every page type; FAQ schema for blog FAQ sections needs third-party tools "which can help. However, schema is a bit lower priority than content"; a member's Acumatica AI Studio blog hit page one and an AI citation within eight hours using good content, an AI-style FAQ and answering the question in the first paragraph: "In the past schema was much more important. Nowadays, not as much." Elliot's counter-view: "I would recommend definitely having schema for FAQs because that obviously is really productive." CJ was fixing FAQ schema "so that I can do better there to answer some of the other questions that are coming up on search." [call 074; playbook I 2; playbook IV Schema; playbook VI 4.4; calls 023, 072, 047, 043]
- How to add FAQ schema if you want it: describe your on-page Q&A to Claude / ChatGPT / Gemini, ask it to generate the schema, and paste it into the page's code-injection block. [call 023; playbook IV Schema]
- The bot-flood side effect (Carolina, tiny one-person niche site): hand-added FAQ schema snippets at the bottom of each page "turned my site into catnip for AI bots" (100,000+ legitimate bots from Apple, Meta crashing shared hosting; on another telling 150,000 visits a day versus about 40 normal, needing Cloudflare and a new IP); Google AI started recommending her. The fix: upgrade hosting (about $30 a month cloud / VPS plus a WAF), ask Claude for a snippet that blocks the AI / spam bots, do not block the AI crawlers you want, never strip the schema that earned the visibility, keep publishing two posts a week; whether Cloudflare's human check hurts SEO was still to be researched. [calls 034, 047; playbook IV Schema; playbook V 7.4]
- When auditing why a competitor outranks you, check whether they use a "summary at the very top... key takeaways at the bottom", plus internal links. [call 091]
- Competitors "adding so many FAQs" is a CRO / design test, not an SEO rule. [call 024]

### CTAs and conversion

- FAQs sit immediately before the final CTA so objections are handled before the ask; "Once people feel kind of reassured that all their questions are answered, that typically helps increase conversion rates." [calls 016, 028, 045]
- Each answer carries an onward link (blog post, money page, or hashtag anchor) so the FAQ block is a conversion path, not a dead end; homepage answers link to the pages you want to rank. [calls 058, 043, 030]

### Links, indexing, and where it sits in the site

- The FAQ section is the best place for a service page to link out to blog posts; the chain is FAQ to blog to product / service page. [call 082; call 058; playbook III 2.10]
- Anchor text to a FAQ page: "One of the most common questions about pest control", link the whole phrase, because it points to a pest control FAQ page. [call 072]
- A dedicated FAQ page, where one exists: the cat-behavior competitor keeps a few answers on the page and links "to read more FAQs, click here" to a dedicated FAQs page; the book puts a standalone FAQ page in the footer ("Your footer... is where you can link to secondary, but still important, pages. Things like frequently asked questions (FAQs), policies, support pages, or location-specific content live here."); Joyce built an FAQ page. [calls 034, 108; book ch. 9]
- Two pages on the same topic (a "guide" and an "FAQ" for mechanics liens in California) is a cannibalization question to investigate. [call 081]
- Schema "Clarifies what kind of page it is: Whether it's a blog post, product page, service page, recipe, or FAQ"; rich snippets can show "FAQs". [book ch. 22]

### Don'ts

- Don't build one central FAQ page in place of per-page blocks ("what are we ranking for?"). [calls 028, 045]
- Don't exceed three or four per page; 12 is too many. [call 058; playbook III 2.10]
- Don't hide important answers in toggles or dropdowns (Matt's rule; see the disagreement note). [calls 004, 032, 116]
- Don't repeat the same FAQ text on every page. [call 045; playbook III 2.10]
- Don't stuff the keyword into questions and answers instead of answering. [call 058]
- Don't bolt 10 generic FAQs under the CTA of every blog post and call it AEO. [call 069]
- Don't stack TL;DR, Conclusion, Key Takeaways and FAQ as redundant closers. [call 081; playbook V 7.3]
- Don't write one-line hidden answers. [call 097]
- Don't strip FAQ schema that is earning AI recommendations because bots arrived; fix hosting and block bad bots instead. [playbook IV Schema]
- Don't use FAQs to force word count onto a page that competitors win with a few hundred words. [call 094]

### From the calls: live examples

- Yaniv's rebuild with his designer: per-page FAQs, no toggles. [call 004]
- Leanne's builds: FAQs before the final CTA on the homepage wireframe, the massage-clinic contact page, and her logo-design service page (accordion, H2 titles); "what are we ranking for?" [calls 028, 045, 115]
- Denroy's 100+ accordion sites; his homepage Q&A block with keyword plus location headers. [calls 015, 030]
- Carolina's FAQ-schema bot flood (100,000+ bots, 150,000 visits a day) and the hosting / WAF fix. [calls 034, 047]
- Caroline's cat-behavior site: "Do you need these FAQs?"; the competitor's short on-page answers linking to a FAQs page. [call 034]
- Jill's category page: 1,000 to 1,750 words of FAQs removed and spun into posts. [call 094]
- The compactors and dumpster pages: too many FAQs; bullet points for allowed / prohibited items. [call 105]
- Mark (Squarespace): FAQs at the bottom of every page; FAQ schema via code injection. [call 023]
- The Acumatica AI Studio post: page one and an AI citation within eight hours with an AI-style FAQ and an answer in the first paragraph. [call 072]
- Melissa's buried FAQs; Gail's bottom-versus-top; the pet store's opened FAQs; summer art camps; the Yellowstone wolf-tour Q&A; the temporary-tattoo collection page. [calls 081, 024, 068, 072, 084, 119]
- Laura (Hire Dragons) adding FAQs to live pages; CJ fixing FAQ schema; Joyce's FAQ page; Christopher's video-summary-article-FAQ blog format; the hot-sauce People Also Ask pick. [calls 029, 043, 108, 039, 114]

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- Accordion versus open text is the biggest disagreement in the whole set. Matt (calls 004, 032, 034, 116, 091) and the playbook say keep them open because crawlers and AI may not open toggles; Denroy (calls 015, 082) says accordions rank on 100+ sites and Google's raw-HTML crawl sees them; Leanne (calls 028, 055, 115) uses accordions when there are many or long FAQs and builds her own service-page FAQ as one; the curriculum's word-count lesson recommends FAQ dropdowns. All four positions are recorded above. [calls 004, 032, 034, 116, 091, 015, 082, 028, 055, 115; playbook III 2.10; course: word-count]
- Central FAQ page: Leanne, Matt and the playbook say per-page blocks, not one FAQ page; the book lists a standalone FAQ page among footer links; the playbook itself says to split 12 FAQs onto a dedicated page; the cat-behavior competitor and Joyce have one. [calls 028, 045, 034, 108; playbook III 2.10; book ch. 9]
- Duplicate FAQ blocks: never repeat FAQ text (calls 045, 055, playbook) versus "probably fine" for FAQs, not for reviews (call 096). [calls 045, 055, 096; playbook III 2.10]
- FAQs in blog posts: Matt "hasn't been using them in blog posts" and rejects 10 FAQs under the CTA, yet his content engine ends each blog with FAQs and Christopher's format is endorsed. [calls 091, 069, 034, 039]
- FAQ schema: Matt and Denroi call it minimal or unmeasurable; Elliot recommends it "definitely"; Carolina's case shows it can both win AI recommendations and flood a site with bots. [calls 074, 023, 047, 034; playbook IV Schema]
- Placement: bottom-before-CTA is the default everywhere, but Melissa's competitor surfaces questions higher and Matt treats top-versus-bottom as a CRO test. No word count per answer is given beyond "short", "a couple of sentences", and "not really long". [calls 081, 024, 058, 021, 105]
- Thin: no title / meta guidance for a FAQ page, no image guidance beyond "no photo needed", and no email covers FAQ layout; the emails only classify FAQs as content pages and push questions toward blog posts. [email 2026-08-04; email 2026-05-09]

---

## 9. Blog posts and the blog index

### What it is for and what it targets

- Blog posts are informational-intent pages, not built to convert directly. They exist for seven reasons: traffic and trust, internal-linking equity, unlocking harder keywords, organic backlinks, the "avalanche" compounding effect, consumer trust and recognition, and topical authority ("Google wants to see experts in a specific field") [course: power of blog posts; playbook II 4.1].
- "For most websites, blog posts are the primary driver of visitors. They're the front door." [book ch. 2] They also power the money pages: linking every post to one service page with keyword-rich anchor text got those pages ranking #1 a year later with zero edits to them [book ch. 2, ch. 9].
- A post's title is the literal question a real customer would type into Google, sourced from AnswerThePublic (pages 2-4), People Also Ask, Reddit, and client calls [calls 026, 065; playbook II 1.3, 4.2].
- One topic per post, matching the "every page is about one main topic" rule: a title like "could you be ruining your teeth with these five bad habits?" is cited as a bad, keywordless topic [course: one topic per page].
- The blog post's target keyword should never double as the money page's own primary keyword: the money page owns the buyer-intent phrase, and a post can reference it once near the bottom as a link back [playbook II 2.4, 3.3].
- Long-tail (4+ words) is favored; roughly 10 searches/month is acceptable; keyword difficulty 0-15 is ideal for a new site, up to 25 possible; a page-1 result with 0-2 referring domains is winnable without any backlinks [playbook II 1.1, 1.4].
- Low-volume math: 2 posts/week at about 50 searches each is roughly 400 potential searches/month, about 4,800/month after 12 months; authority to compete for harder terms opens up around the 6 to 9 month mark, the "avalanche technique" [book ch. 36].
- Evergreen beats dated: a 2023 HeyTony post has brought in over 82,000 visitors and just needs updating "now and then" [book ch. 3].
- Sometimes a post's only job is passing internal-link authority to a money page, or acting as a sales/support tool, not a ranking asset: Matt's "how to add and remove users from Meta Business Manager" post saved 20 to 60 minutes per onboarding call [playbook II 2.3, 4.12; calls 024, 087].
- The blog index itself is not built to rank: "we don't really need our blog role to be ranking... usually the post itself gets more traffic than the blog role." It exists so Google, and humans, can find every post [call 008; playbook IV Web Design].

### What goes on it, top to bottom (the structure)

- Single post, top of page: title, then a short featured snippet or excerpt, author, date, then a full-width featured image, then the body [call 008]. Byline: author name linked to an author page, date, and read time, a custom script that counts words, shown as "8 minute read" [calls 038, 030].
- Answer-first opening: the literal first sentence answers the question in the title, no hedging. Matt's rule of thumb: "can the first sentence be an answer to the question in the title tag." Cut "the short answer is no" in favor of a direct "No. Real estate agents cannot perform official appraisals in the Quad Cities." [calls 056, 094].
- Optional TL;DR / "Key Summary" block directly under the title, only for longer posts whose opening paragraph does not already carry the full answer: about 100 words, five bullets max, written so an AI Overview could lift it as-is. Call it "Key Summary" or "Key Takeaways," not "TL;DR" ("that is a Reddit term") [calls 099, 112; playbook II 4.3, 4.7].
- Table of contents: sits in a left sidebar (roughly a quarter width), pulling H2s only or H2 and H3, anchored or sticky. Never a large block sitting on top of the content itself, that "looks stupid" and reads "like a recipe website where you have to scroll through a bunch of bullshit" [calls 054, 080, 111].
- Body: one H1 (the title, with the keyword), H2 and H3 headings only in the generated draft [calls 122, 124; playbook II 4.3].
- Images every 200 to 300 words minimum, ideally one per H2 [calls 012, 048, 057, 111; course: images].
- Optional embedded video, hosted externally, placed under its own H2 such as "How to Clean X, Video" [calls 073, 124; playbook III 2.9].
- Key Takeaways block near the end: 4 to 5 bullets, worth doing "regardless of length" [calls 091, 099, 112; playbook II 4.7].
- Optional FAQ block at the very end: 3 to 4 questions max, plain H2 questions with plain-text answers, never an accordion [playbook II 4.7].
- CTA at the end, and optionally mid-post: a natural sentence, not a shouty heading, e.g. "we can help you with your content marketing, just contact us today" [calls 073, 081].
- Comments disabled sitewide [calls 013, 103, 111].

### Layout and design

- The fullest build on record is Leanne's Elementor template copying HeyTony's own layout [call 111]. Blog roll/archive: a hero with the blog name, an archive-posts widget, 2 to 4 columns (5 "looks very busy"), card title around 22px, a background color per card so posts do not visually blend, medium image resolution rather than large, and a configurable excerpt length with a per-post override.
- Single-post template, same source: two sections. Hero is two columns, featured image roughly one-third width on one side, title plus post-info (author, date, read time) on the other, plus an author box. Content is two columns: a sidebar half the width of the content column, next to the Post Content widget. HeyTony runs sidebar-left, content-right; a single long column also works [call 111].
- Sidebar contents: the table-of-contents widget (H2/H3 only, numbers switched to bullets), plus optional static items such as a phone number, hours, or a "shop with us after reading this" CTA [call 111].
- Featured image ratio: 16 by 9 is the standard, though HeyTony itself uses a square crop; the same image shows on the blog roll card and in the post hero [call 111].
- Typography (call 111): body copy starts at 16px minimum (10px is "too hard to read"); Leanne's build runs 18px, HeyTony's 20px; headings on Leanne's site run H1 48px, H2 36px, H3 26px, HeyTony's post-title H1 at 72px. A simple sans serif (Inter, Open Sans) covers most body copy; save personality for the headline font, where serif reads "classier."
- Huckberry reference numbers, a separate teardown (call 008): single column capped at 940 to 1,000px, body 17px desktop and 16px under 720px wide, line height 1.8, darker headline against gray body copy, near-white background.
- Spacing: use ems, not pixels, for padding so it scales on mobile; hero padding tested from 2em up to 4 to 6em top and bottom [call 111].
- One H1 per post, always: a theme or index that prints its own title as H1 above the post's real H1 must have that duplicate demoted to H2 or plain paragraph text [calls 008, 010]. Denroy's caveat: the styling does not matter, the H1 does not have to be the largest text, as long as the tags run in order [call 007].
- Make internal links visually obvious: underline them, bump link font weight to 700, or shift the color so they "pop" against body text [calls 010, 008, 103].

### Visuals

- Cadence: an image, chart, or embed every 200 to 300 words is the number repeated across nearly every source [calls 012, 048, 057, 111, 073; playbook II 4.8; book ch. 12].
- Minimum: at least a featured image on every post, plus roughly 3 to 4 in-body images per the internal checklist, or "at least four" per call 111, ideally one per H2.
- Source priority: your own real photos or screenshots first ("nobody else can make those real pictures"), then stock, then AI, except for graphs and infographics, where AI generation goes to the top of the list [calls 012, 017, 019, 048; playbook II 4.8].
- Featured image prompt pattern: upload 2 to 4 on-brand reference images and ask for a new image "using the style of the images attached," with no text or title baked in [calls 069, 112; playbook II 4.3, 4.8].
- Format: convert to WebP, compressed to roughly 70% quality (1.1MB down to 27KB, 537KB down to 32KB); GoHighLevel and some Squarespace/Wix setups reject WebP, fall back to compressed JPEG [calls 010, 030, 111, 112].
- Dimensions: in-body images around 750px wide; featured or OG image 1200 by 628px [calls 009, 067; playbook II 4.8].
- Alt text and file names: describe the image plainly, like a photo caption, not keyword-stuffed, and rename the file the same way before upload. Keyword in at most half a page's images, never sequentially, e.g. never "Newborn photographer Chicago 1, 2, 3, 4" [calls 002, 065, 069; playbook III 2.8].
- Embeds: your own Instagram posts and YouTube Shorts are fine and keep visitors on-page longer; avoid TikTok embeds, which force a sign-in; never upload video directly to WordPress or Wix, host on YouTube or Vimeo instead [calls 002, 064].
- Geotagging blog images is unnecessary, "that is more for your Google Business Profile" [calls 001, 066].

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO

- No single magic number: check the actual top 3 ranking pages for the target keyword and match or slightly exceed them individually, not combined [email 2025-07-03; book ch. 2, ch. 37].
- The 10% rule (Denroy): "aim to be 10% better than whoever is doing the most" among the top 3, so leaders at 800, 1,000, and 500 words become roughly 1,100, not a jump to 2,000-3,000, "we want to avoid over optimizing" [call 030].
- Program sweet spot: 750 to 1,500 words, Matt's standing guidance ("you don't really want to go over 1500"), with 1,000 to 1,200 words as "perfect" [calls 010, 012]; the internal checklist sets a 1,000-word floor, "equal to or more than the top 3 individual search results" [email 2025-07-03].
- For zero-competition long-tail keywords, length stops mattering: "I write 300 words and I rank" [call 120]; a 2,000-word page has even beaten a 3,200-word one on format fit alone [playbook II 1.7].
- Keyword density "plus one" rule: "if they say it 10 times, you say it 11" [call 030], echoed as "if your competitors are using the keyword 7 times, use it 8 times" [book ch. 37].
- On a pure question post, the question itself is the keyword and recurs naturally 3 to 4 times without deliberate insertion; ignore Yoast or Rank Math density tools built for short-tail keywords [calls 051, 065; playbook II 1.7, 4.7].
- Title: keyword near the front, 60 characters max, ideally around 55 before mobile truncation. Never append the city or service as a title suffix, e.g. never "Post Title, Chicago Newborn Photographer": "if it feels spammy, it probably is spammy" [calls 026, 070].
- Meta description: 120 to 156 characters, keyword near the front [email 2025-07-03]; generate about 5 with the prompt "write me a killer, compelling, click-worthy meta description" and pick the best [call 112].
- URL: the slug should be the core target keyword only, never the platform's long auto-generated slug, and flat off the root, e.g. heytony.ca/answering-question-keywords, not nested under about-us/news/slug [calls 026, 101]. Never date the URL of evergreen or annually-updated content [calls 049, 108, 110].
- Reading level 7th to 8th grade throughout. Heading rules: H2s carry the keyword in most, not necessarily all, of them; never skip a level; a normal target is one more H2 than the top competitor, 5 to 10 total [calls 002, 024; playbook II 4.3, III 2.4].
- Internal Content Checklist in full (email 2025-07-03): title under 60 characters, meta 120-156 characters, one H1, several H2s with the keyword in 2-3 of them, 1,000+ words matching the top 3, Yoast readability green, 3-4 internal links (one to a service or product page, favoring older pages), 1-2 external links to non-competitors, 3-4 webp images with keyword-aware alt text, keyword in the URL, a cannibalization check, a Google Business Profile link, and a sweep for punctuation, homophones, and stock AI phrases ("delve into," "enter a world of," "today's digital landscape," "a tapestry," "in conclusion," "in summary," "skyrocket").

### CTAs and conversion

- Blog posts are informational by default and usually are not meant to convert directly; a high-ranking post with weak conversion often just needs a business-relevant CTA added, not a rewrite into a sales page [playbook II 2.3].
- End-of-post CTA as a plain sentence, not a heading: "we can help you with your content marketing, just contact us today," rather than a section literally labeled "CTA" [calls 073, 081].
- Vary the CTA across posts instead of repeating the same pitch everywhere; agencies rotate to the same offer "every third or fourth post," mixing tools, lead magnets, and services [call 053].
- If a post already ranks well but converts poorly, move the actual conversion element higher on the page (a "file your lien today" button that was buried far down) and leave the ranking content untouched [call 081].
- Funnel logic inside the post: answer the top-of-funnel question first, then a contextual link deeper in, e.g. a post on "when was hot sauce invented" ending with "check out our hot sauces" [course: first blog post].
- Sidebar CTA option alongside the table of contents: a static block such as a phone number, hours, or "come shop with us after reading this blog post" [calls 111, 106].
- Local CTA line for broad how-to posts that are not location-specific: "if you're looking for a hairstylist in Denver," linked to the location or service page [call 095].
- A Google Business Profile link (the CID link) belongs inside posts "wherever you can insert the link naturally"; it passes authority and raises profile visibility [call 047; email 2025-07-03].
- Weaving your own offer into educational content is fine; the failure mode is bait-and-switch, not promotion itself [playbook II 4.5, 4.6].
- An in-content lead magnet between paragraphs can work well; a sidebar lead magnet is "not very effective," people have "banner blindness" to it [call 081; book ch. 46].

### Links, indexing, and where it sits in the site

- Outbound rule: every new post links to 3 to 5 other blog posts plus 1 money page ("two blog posts and one money page" as the floor for a brand-new site) [calls 001, 004, 022, 073, 096; playbook II 4.10].
- Inbound rule, "the half everyone skips": after publishing, link into the new post from 3 different, already-indexed older posts. This alone has gotten pages indexed within one to two hours, and one operator ranked #1 in three hours from internal linking alone [calls 001, 073, 099, 121].
- Finder technique: `site:yourdomain.com "keyword"` to find existing pages on the topic, before publishing for outbound targets and after for inbound ones [course: publishing; playbook III 2.11].
- Anchor text must describe the destination page, never "click here" or "learn more"; use the CMS's link picker, not a pasted URL, so links survive a slug change [calls 009, 058; playbook III, IV].
- Placement matters: links higher in the content "hold the heaviest weight"; in-text contextual links plus a bottom "you might also like" block is "the sweet spot" [calls 072, 050].
- External links: 1 to 2 per post to authoritative, non-competing sources near the top (government, Harvard, or MIT-tier sources "carry a lot of weight"). Never link a competitor, even an AI-suggested one, and never build a reciprocal link circle, it "destroys any link equity" [calls 002, 044, 054, 065, 071; playbook III 2.12].
- Categories versus tags: 5 to 10 categories sitewide, one per post (two max for a cross-cutting piece), never "Uncategorized"; wait until 10 to 20 posts exist before creating categories. Never use tags: each spawns a thin single-post page that wastes crawl budget without helping SEO; delete existing ones once Search Console confirms they aren't ranking or driving traffic, and noindex any tag/category archive you keep, "they don't provide any value" [multiple calls; playbook II 4.10, IV Quick Reference].
- Crawl depth and pagination: don't dump hundreds of posts on one paginated archive, every pagination click is a click deeper for Google; surface categories from the homepage, footer, or a mega menu so posts stay one or two clicks deep. Shopify's default of 6 posts/page is too low, raise it to about 30, then 60 to 100 as the blog grows [calls 015, 086; playbook IV Crawl Depth].
- Indexing: no need to request it if internal linking is solid; check back after two weeks to a month if a post still isn't indexed, then request indexing in Search Console. No indexing, no backlinks, and no traffic after months to a year makes it a drafting candidate, not a redirect or 404 candidate [calls 002, 009, 022; playbook II 4.10, IV Indexing].
- Update and Boost: Matt's own term for refreshing an older post rather than writing something new, meaning fresh stats, improved structure, and added internal links, paired with the rule that brand-new posts should chase "an easy-win keyword you'll actually rank for" [email 2025-12-04].
- Comments off sitewide: "the only thing you'll get on blogs is spam" [calls 013, 103, 111].

### Don'ts

- Don't skip the H1, and don't let a post render with two of them, whether from a duplicated blog-name header or a repeated title styled as a second H1 [calls 008, 010, 038, 094].
- Don't put the money keyword in a blog title unless the post is genuinely about that exact service, that's cannibalization; don't add the target city or a name-address-phone block to every post either, link to the money page instead [calls 004, 009].
- Don't run a finished post through a keyword-count tool and edit it to satisfy the tool, and don't publish placeholders, neither "coming soon" pages nor "(coming soon)" mentions inside a post [calls 001, 027].
- Don't paste an AI draft straight to the site unedited, and don't run it through a "humanizer" or AI-detector pass afterward; Google penalizes regurgitated generic content, not AI authorship [calls 022, 024, 026; playbook II 4.6].
- Before publishing, strip AI tells: horizontal rule dividers, random bolding, decorative arrows, emoji (especially in headings), checkmark or plain "1, 2, 3" numbering unless it's a genuine listicle, the stray leading space, generic "Conclusion"/"Final Thoughts" headers, "and finally," and self-promotional subheads in a pure answer post; em dashes are disputed, see below [calls 077, 108, 120, 122, 123; playbook II 4.5].
- Don't repeat one boilerplate "for more resources" link block verbatim across every post, that becomes duplicate content, and don't A/B test two posts targeting the same keyword or spin secondary keyword variations of one post into a second [calls 022, 026; playbook II 3.3].
- Don't use tags, on this point almost every source agrees without exception, and don't republish the same content verbatim on LinkedIn or Medium, or publish a raw podcast/video transcript as a post; never let a social platform become the "source of truth" copy [calls 004, 013, 065; playbook II 4.12].
- Don't obsess over one underperforming post: across six to eight posts a month, one or two typically carry most of the traffic, and that is normal [call 002].

### From the calls: live examples

- David, auto repair (Texas): three internal service links plus one authoritative external link per post; ranks #1 for long-tail queries like "Chevy Cruze transmission problems," and moved from around 7th or 8th to #1 in the map pack and #1 organic [calls 044, 061, 065].
- Matt's own voice-to-blog engine output: 1,500 words with title tag, meta description, internal linking, and FAQs generated automatically; he only tweaked the title and made the header image with ChatGPT, published essentially as-is, and it ranked [call 034].
- Chuck's Garage blog index review: social-share buttons ate the entire above-the-fold section so a visitor could not tell it was a blog page at all, on top of missing post images and duplicated meta descriptions across posts [call 027].
- The magician's "What magic tricks can I see on an iPad?" post repeated the same phrase three times running, title, giant graphic, first H2, "am I an idiot? Like, why are you telling me this three times?" [call 063].
- A dishwasher how-to guide at 2,500 words outranked a member's 697-word post on the same topic, using descriptive step headings instead of "Step 1, Step 2, Step 3" and screenshots pulled straight from an existing video [call 124].
- "Can I learn SEO on my own?" added a Key Takeaways block at the top and "hit Google Discover," 2,200 clicks in a few days [call 099].

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- Word count has no consensus number: ranges run from "300 words is fine" for zero-competition long-tail, to a 750 to 1,500 sweet spot, to "1,500 to 2,000 words to compete," to a hard 1,000-word floor in the internal checklist. The one constant: check the actual top 3 and match or slightly beat them [calls 010, 012, 120, 030; email 2025-07-03].
- The TL;DR / "key takeaways at the top" block is explicitly unresolved even in Matt's own words: "mixed results, leaning more towards good than bad." Test it on 2 to 3 posts before rolling it out further [call 015; playbook V 7.3].
- Em dashes: the community consensus and Kira's checklist both treat them as an AI tell worth stripping, while Matt personally says "em dashes are fine" [calls 120, 122, 123; playbook II 4.5].
- Internal link count ranges from "two blog posts and one money page" as a floor, to "three to five plus one" as the norm, to Denroy's outlier position of "go crazy with internal links, 4, 5, 6, just go insane with it." Treat 3 to 5 out and 3 in as the median, not a hard ceiling or floor [calls 012, 073, 121].
- Schema on posts is inconsistent even within Matt's own practice: one call has him adding Article schema (type, URL, headline, image, description) [call 039], another has him saying he no longer bothers with schema because "their content was actually just better" than a competitor's that used review and product schema [call 094].
- Table of contents and SEO: treated as a user-experience nicety rather than a proven ranking factor. "I don't know if the table of contents actually improves SEO. It is useful for user experience so that could result in better SEO." [call 111]

---

## 10. Portfolio, gallery, and case-study pages

### What it is for and what it targets

- Built for conversion and proof, not to rank: "the portfolio is not necessarily something that is going to rank. It's more for conversion," and "in 99% of cases" these pages aren't found through Google on their own. HeyTony's own /work and case-study pages rank only for the branded term "hey Tony" [calls 078, 035, 071].
- The job is proof of work: "if you say I'm a photographer but I can't see your work, then that's really not saying much" [call 011].
- Exception: a gallery or venue page can rank if it targets a real keyword instead of a proper noun, e.g. venue pages titled "Art Gallery of Hamilton wedding photography examples," never named after a client, nobody searches "Sarah and Michael's wedding" [calls 096, 112].

### What goes on it, top to bottom (the structure)

- Case studies live on the money page itself as proof, plus their own archive (HeyTony runs separate custom post types for work, services, testimonials, and case studies) [calls 117, 014]. One project page: project name as H1, a real-volume keyword as an H2, internal links to related projects, a closing CTA ("ready to get more traffic, fill out the contact form") [calls 047, 112].
- Gallery-style pages, each job as its own page with one image and a short paragraph, can carry most of a site's clicks and impressions [calls 013, 074].

### Layout and design

- Show the work before the praise: move video or photo proof above testimonials so visitors "see what they're getting into" first [call 032].
- Don't repeat photo sections down the homepage; move the bulk of images to a dedicated gallery page in the nav instead [call 049].
- A "portfolio" page that only lists divisions is "useless" for SEO and pulls the GBP link away from money pages; don't point GBP at an unoptimized portfolio site either [calls 101, 094].

### Visuals

- Real photos beat stock, and stock runs "about the same" as AI here; real job photos (one HVAC client uses 172,000) beat stock outright [calls 067, 051].
- Crisp embedded video with sound is the real showcase, an autoplaying compressed background video "isn't really doing you a service" [call 103]. Host on YouTube over Vimeo unless you're a filmmaker ("the world's second largest search engine") and turn off related-video clutter and autoplay [call 042].
- Gallery images stay under 1000px wide except banners, source under 2K resolution [playbook IV Images]. Carousels are a liability, Matt dislikes them, and Wix-style galleries "take too much juice" [calls 020, 064].

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO

- Titles, alt text, and captions matter more here since the photography or project is the work itself, but don't force a keyword onto every image, just describe what's in it [call 065].
- Thin, image-heavy pages can take copy above the grid toward 400 to 500 words, or stay thin if only someone searching that exact project will ever find it [call 010; course: low word count].
- Never use a graphic as the H1 (Google can't read baked-in text); logos, signage, and animation should never carry heading tags either, one audit found 13 H1s firing from exactly that [playbook III 2.4, IV Heading Structure].

### CTAs and conversion

- Case studies close with a direct ask: "ready to get more traffic, blah, blah, fill out the contact form" [call 112].
- Proof sections on money pages, "websites they've designed," real-world results, and case studies, function as trust proof, not decoration [call 081].
- Link a specific portfolio piece from inside a related blog post ("we just did this for [Children's Hospital]") rather than hoping the portfolio page gets found on its own [call 078].

### Links, indexing, and where it sits in the site

- Internally link related pieces to each other and request indexing manually, these pages rarely get found organically on their own [call 047].
- A default "clients"/"portfolio" archive competing with a hand-built portfolio page causes duplicate metadata and canonical conflicts; style and keep the default archive, or 301 it and confirm nothing still links to it [call 014]. A renamed portfolio section whose canonical tags weren't updated leaves old URLs 404ing while canonicals still point at them; fix per-page in the SEO plugin, never by hacking permalinks [call 007].
- Archive dead pages (old listings, discontinued projects); keep only what generates traffic, ranks, or holds backlinks, 301 the rest [calls 047, 074].

### Don'ts

- Don't expect a portfolio or case-study page to rank on its own, it's a conversion and proof surface first [calls 078, 035, 071].
- Don't title project pages after people's names or one-off proper nouns nobody searches [call 112].
- Don't let a default archive template compete with a hand-built portfolio page for the same URL space [call 014].
- Don't use a graphic as an H1, and don't lean on carousels for photos or reviews [playbook III 2.4; call 020].

### From the calls: live examples

- Katya, a Quebec graphic designer: 12 project pages indexed, 39 not; the fix per page was a real-volume keyword, an H1/H2 structure, and internal links between related projects [call 047].
- A vehicle-wrap company's 25-year-old site had every past job as its own portfolio-style post; the rebuild turned those pages into service pages, keeping only the ones already earning traffic, rank, or backlinks [call 074].

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- Whether to add text to a thin, image-heavy project page is situational: add copy "if there's just a lot of pictures," but equally fine to leave it alone if "that's not going to be a page that is bringing new people into your site randomly" [call 010].
- Whether a case study belongs on a Page or a Post is decided by search intent, not a fixed rule: one framing wants it to "stick out and maybe be pages on your website and not posts" [calls 112, 116], while another calls a client's session posts "basically case study pages" without moving them out of the blog [call 066].

---

## 11. Testimonials and reviews pages

### What it is for and what it targets

- Reviews build trust and conversion, and feed local map-pack ranking: review velocity, roughly one a week, can outpace a competitor sitting on a bigger lifetime total [call 052; playbook V 3, VI 3.4].
- A dedicated reviews page is optional, not the main event: "I like to include testimonials where it's relevant... on the homepage... on specific service pages... you can have a page just for reviews if you want, but I also like to sprinkle reviews all over the website" [call 016].
- Matt's own reviews page doesn't rank for "hey tony reviews" (the results are dominated by social posts, Trustpilot, Reddit, Google reviews, and BBB), yet he still keeps the page on the main site [call 001].
- Reviews shape AI picks too: ChatGPT favored the highest-rated Boston towing companies over the most-reviewed ones, and a 2.2-star company still ranked in Google Maps with no website [call 109].

### What goes on it, top to bottom (the structure)

- Reviews belong on every money page, not funneled onto one reviews page: 2 to 3 real reviews per page, pasted as plain text [call 021; playbook III 3.4, V 3].
- On the homepage, testimonials sit as their own section, after the services/products overview and before FAQs [call 028; course: word count].
- Heading should name the service or city, e.g. "Kind words our customers say in Fort Myers," or "Reviews of the Yellowstone Day tour"; the quote itself must never carry a heading tag, plain paragraph text only [calls 070, 097, 014].
- A "featured in" line with press or media logos can follow a press release, without linking the logos out [call 012].

### Layout and design

- Hero social proof: five gold star icons plus "rated five stars on Google," linked to the real Google review URL, kept as the one prominent placement rather than competing with the main quote/call CTA [calls 036, 060].
- Trust badges directly under the hero: the 5-star rating plus chamber or association logos, with Yelp, Trustpilot, or BIA badges on service pages [calls 055, 061].
- A testimonial section doesn't need photos if it already has visual interest (the Google logo and stars); icons work when clients don't want their photo shown [call 100].
- Show one testimonial section, not two: keep it once and higher, or give it a button through to more [calls 049, 060].

### Visuals

- Four formats: a live Google reviews widget, copy-pasted text, video testimonials, and screenshots, which "feel really real... especially now with a lot of things being vibe coded" [call 016].
- Screenshots build human trust, but Google likely isn't reading text baked into an image; embed or paste the actual text if you want it picked up by search or AI [call 077].
- Never a review carousel or a live plugin: one carousel added roughly 1,000 words of markup per page, a Google Reviews plugin inflated pages by 3,000 to 4,000 duplicated words, and a widget pulling 80 to 90 reviews inflated a service page to 6,500 words; visitors want "maybe three reviews," then a link to see more [calls 021, 032, 074].
- Upload the actual photos you took for a client alongside their testimonial into the Google Business Profile review itself [call 011].

### Length, keyword density, and on-page SEO

- Pasted review text counts toward a page's word count and SEO; a live review widget adds no extra trust signal beyond whatever text is actually visible [playbook IV Conversion elements].
- Keep product reviews on the page even if they skew keyword density, "that's social proof... that'll help you sell more" [call 116].
- The same review text repeated verbatim on every page reads as duplicate content; rotate which 2 to 3 reviews appear where, and keep location-specific testimonials on the matching location page only [calls 021, 032, 096].
- Adding testimonials to an already-ranking page doesn't move its ranking either way; review schema needs a third-party generator, Yoast's default markup doesn't add it [calls 035, 072].

### CTAs and conversion

- Always link a testimonial back to its real source, Google, Yelp, or wherever it actually lives: "that tells Google, that tells people that okay, this is legit... I can go into ChatGPT, generate an image, generate a review, and slap it on my website with a fake name" [call 103].
- Pair the visible reviews with a "read more reviews" button pointing at the full profile, rather than dumping every review onto the page [calls 021, 086].
- Reviews placed right under the hero "add some impact for reducing friction" toward buying [call 100].

### Links, indexing, and where it sits in the site

- Every displayed review links to its real source; connect the website to the actual Google profile rather than just quoting it [call 086].
- The reviews micro-site tactic, explicitly gray-hat, "not white hat SEO": register a separate root domain like yourbusinessnamereviews.com, fill it with real review text rather than screenshots, and link to it once from an already-indexed blog post on the main site, never the reverse. Build it cheap with Claude Code from dumped review screenshots, "shouldn't take more than a couple hours," hosted on Railway or Netlify; grab the domain early, and never connect or verify it in Search Console [calls 001, 032; playbook V 7.6, VI 1.6].
- Never host a press release itself on your own site, that's linking to something that's linking back to you, promote its reviews via social posts instead [call 031].

### Don'ts

- Don't review-gate with an NPS or 1-to-5 screener before sending the real Google review link, "Google wants you to send everybody a link to your Google business profile" [call 003].
- Don't incentivize a review, let a customer name staff in it, ask only happy customers, solicit one while someone is still at your business, or ask for a specific review or product mention, Google's own policy bans all five [email 2026-04-16].
- Don't run review trains or reciprocal review swaps, they read as gaming the system and get removed [call 071].
