- Don't repeat the same testimonial section twice on one page, or repeat identical review text across many pages [calls 049, 096].
- Don't fake a review with an AI-generated name and image [call 103].

### From the calls: live examples

- Marisa's reviews micro-site (marisabrusilloreviews.com) lists every review, including non-Google ones, as plain text on a deliberately generic page with no menu and no branding, built to read like an independent third party wrote it [call 021].
- A client's page audit turned up 3,000 to 4,000 extra words purely from a duplicated Google Reviews plugin; removing the section and comparing word count against competitors, 500 versus 3,000, was the fix [call 032].
- A press release opened with "a five-star average across 111 Google reviews," hyperlinking "111 Google reviews" straight to the GBP CID link [call 031].

### Where the sources are thin or disagree

- The reviews micro-site tactic is explicitly unresolved: a working, low-cost bet with no seen downside in one framing, unproven and disputed by other SEOs in another, "it is to manipulate the search results, which they don't want you to do" [call 001; playbook V 7.6, VI 1.6].
- "Featured in" press logos get two different treatments: "you could say featured in and then post the logos, but I wouldn't link to them" in one call [call 012], while a competitor's featured-in logos were themselves linked out as external links in another [call 034].
- The official solicitation rules flatly ban incentivizing a review, yet a QR code offering "10% off next time" for leaving one appears approvingly in the same source material, a real gray area rather than a resolved rule [email 2026-04-16; call 071].

---

## 12. Legal pages

Covers privacy policy, terms and conditions, cookie policy, disclaimers, accessibility statements, and refund/return policies.

### What legal pages are for

- Terms and conditions plus a privacy policy in the footer "shows that you're a legitimate business... and that you care about people's privacy"; running Meta ads without one means "your ads won't get shown to as many people." [call 013]
- Google wants to see the pages exist ("here's the data we're going to collect... that legitimizes the business"); their absence "can hurt, definitely hurt your SEO." [call 043]
- Accessibility doubles as an SEO lever: proper heading hierarchy and alt text serve screen readers and search engines together, "the more accessible your site, the better it is for SEO." [call 002] [call 024]

### Index or noindex

- Noindexing is normal here: legal terms and service agreements "are just legal documents... there's really no sales value in it. It's for existing customers." [call 054]
- A privacy policy showing "not indexed" in Search Console is nothing to worry about; what matters is the homepage, money pages, and blog posts. [calls 058, 059]
- Live example: Jennifer had already noindexed her privacy policy and accessibility statement; missing meta descriptions there are "really non issues." [call 078]
- Robots.txt can keep pages like these out of the index, but "Just leave it alone" unless you know what you're doing. [book ch. 24]

### What goes on the page

- Use real text, not an iframe: Katie's privacy, terms, and cookie pages pulled from a third-party lawyer site and read as low word count; "copy the text from the third party and paste that text onto your website." [call 043]
- Draft with ChatGPT or Claude ("I need a privacy policy... tell me what I should include"), then confirm it's actually compliant, Matt is not a lawyer. [call 013] [call 043]
- Refund and shipping pages get compared directly against competitors by Google Merchant Center. [call 081]
- Aim the accessibility statement at a real WCAG level (A, AA, AAA). [call 002]
- Accessibility widgets are a paid shortcut: accessiBe runs roughly $300-400 a year, a comparable widget about $300 a year, cheaper or free WordPress options exist. [call 002]
- Contrast is testable: pure black (#000) and #191919 both pass on white, failures start in mid-tone grays, gold text fails on white but passes on black; text too small, or a page that can't zoom, excludes older and visually impaired users. [call 045] [call 002]

### Placement in the footer

- "Policies" belong in the footer, not the main nav. [call 058] [book ch. 9]
- Move non-essentials, including policies, into the footer or an About dropdown, keeping roughly 7-8 top-nav items max. [playbook IV]
- A footer holding only cookies, privacy policy, and contact is too thin for local SEO; legal pages are one part of the footer, not the whole footer. [call 081]

### Don'ts

- Don't iframe legal text, paste real text onto your own page instead. [call 043]
- Don't just pay ADA demand letters ($100,000-150,000): "unless you are a place of public accommodation that has 15 employees or more, most of those lawsuits don't apply to you"; Canada's threshold sits around 50-plus employees. [call 002]
- Don't let cookie-consent or privacy-center topics bleed into unrelated content, delete those headings when outlining from a competitor. [course: creating-a-blog-outline]

### Live examples

- Katie: privacy, terms, and cookie policy as iframes, fixed by pasting real text in. [call 043]
- Jennifer: privacy policy and accessibility statement both noindexed on purpose. [call 078]
- David, a legally blind Insider, runs an accessibility button on his own site and confirmed the community platform's contrast and screen-reader order were sound. [call 024] [calls 013, 017]
- HeyTony's own site audit flagged "button background and foreground colors do not have a sufficient contrast ratio" and "button name not accessible." [call 107]

### What Matt does not cover

- The curriculum, playbook, and book have nothing on writing privacy, terms, cookie, or disclaimer pages themselves, only placement, indexing, and adjacent nav/footer rules. [course: creating-a-blog-outline] [playbook III] [playbook IV] [book ch. 9] [book ch. 24]
- Not legal advice: Matt flags repeatedly that he is not a lawyer, and sends real legal disputes, like trademark misuse in a competitor's ad, straight to a lawyer. [call 043] [call 020]
- Site ownership is a site-level rule, not a legal-page rule: "If you paid for a website, you should own that website," hosted on your own server. [book ch. 25]

---

## 13. Landing pages and other page types

### Paid-ad landing pages

Intent and process:
- Ad clickers are high intent, "in the mood to buy," so the whole design goal is reducing friction: contact form and CTAs at the very top, no scrolling required to act. [call 016]
- Build order: copy first, then wireframe (Figma, grid paper, Canva, or Photoshop), then build. [call 016]

Hero and trust indicators:
- The hero leads with an image or a form; the CTA sits in "a color that stands out from the rest of the colors." [call 016]
- One dominant action only: "if we have several different decisions that they can make and they're all about equal value people will end up just not picking one," any secondary option is styled subtler. [call 016]
- Three trust indicators sit in the hero (years of experience, number of happy clients, licensed or insured, five-star reviews), "people are automatically hesitant... assuming everything is a scam." [call 016]

Full section order, every section carries its own CTA:
1. Hero: image or form, CTA, trust indicators
2. Why choose us, with a CTA
3. A reduce-anxiety callout, which also breaks up the layout
4. Services or offerings in a grid
5. Testimonials
6. Process steps ("remove uncertainty... clear steps of what's happening next"), with another CTA
7. FAQs, to handle objections
8. Final CTA, "our last chance"
9. Optional repeat of the hero's trust indicators
10. A response-time reassurance line: "I respond within 24 hours," "a real person will review this," or naming the actual person
[call 016]

Nav and footer removal:
- Remove the navigation, or cut it to a logo linking home, "not entirely trap them, but reduce people's distractions"; the nav's usual CTA button is dropped; footer slimmed down too. [call 016] [playbook IV]
- Caveat: a sales page stripped of all navigation wasn't converting and had its checkout page indexed by Google; Matt added the menu back, "a no-nav one-pager is not trusted and cannot rank." Reserve nav-stripping for pages that are genuinely ad-only traffic. [call 113]

Noindex and out of nav:
- Never list an ad landing page in the site nav: "exclusively for paid ads... I would never put this in the navigation... you probably don't want Google to index these pages." [call 016]
- An orphan-page audit flag is fine and expected "if you intended for that to be an orphan page." [call 016] [playbook III] [playbook IV]

CTA color contrast:
- Use color-wheel opposites deliberately: a red CTA blended into a brownish-red hero and converted poorly; a muted green background makes it pop, "not a bright green and a bright red because that'd be very jarring." [call 016]
- A too-busy hero (blurry background, overlaid images) hurt conversion; "a plain color background... less is more" reads as the safer default. [call 016]

FAQ placement:
- FAQs sit late, after testimonials and process steps, right before the close; on a landing page generally, keep FAQs to three or four questions at most. [call 016] [call 091]

Worked examples: a services business picks one offer ("I would pick one") and sells the benefit; an e-card company runs ads per occasion with about five samples shown; a campaign/event page states the dates up front, with a "reserve your spot" CTA repeated about halfway down, a countdown timer, and results shown. [call 016] [call 084]

### Funnel and high-ticket landing pages

Full section order:
1. Hero hook naming the problem ("frustrated, stuck, overwhelmed... we get it and we have a solution"), with a video or product picture
2. Empathize ("we've been there too")
3. Identify their challenges ("it's not you, you just need the skills")
4. How we help, in steps
5. What to expect / what's included
6. CTAs at every section, as jump links to the bottom form, a pop-up form, or straight to checkout ("buy now")
7. Success stories, video and screenshot testimonials in a slider
8. "Who is this good for," to filter leads
9. Meet the founder
10. FAQ
11. Emotional final CTA ("You don't have to figure this out alone") with a contact form
[call 016]

- Use this template for genuinely high-ticket offers ("a luxury car... or a course that's like a grand"); a moderate bundle isn't automatically high ticket, but nothing stops you from testing the format anyway. [call 016]

Hosting and checkout:
- Keep the sales/landing page on your own domain if ranking matters at all, "I would keep everything housed on your website"; checkout itself can live off-domain without hurting it, Rank Week's page ranks number one and checks out via SamCart. [call 051] [playbook IV] [call 031] [call 022]
- Ebook sold via Gumroad: the on-site page is the actual sales page (add a video testimonial on why you wrote it); every CTA points to Gumroad, which is just the checkout, the friction is the cost, not the flow. [call 016]

Worked example, a book sales page: liked hero image clearly describing the product, a "download it now" CTA, copy speaking to the target audience, all above the fold; to lengthen, add more testimonials, more of what people get, the author's own story, an FAQ, and a contact form at the bottom. [call 028]

### Thank-you pages

- Legitimate orphan pages by design: "They can't get to a thank you page just by clicking on your website. They have to input a form and then they get redirected." Noindex them; orphan and low-word-count flags are intentional and fine; robots.txt is a valid way to keep them, and checkout flows, out of the index. [course: orphaned-page-no-internal-links-point-to-it] [playbook III] [playbook IV] [book ch. 24]
- Content: thank you, what to expect on the call, optional further reading. [call 085]
- A campaign or webinar page and its confirmation page can both be drafted (unpublished) once the campaign ends, so "Google won't see it at all"; left live and unlinked they become "a bunch of unlinked orphan pages... that's unhealthy for your site." [call 050]

### 404 pages

- Nothing in the sources on the 404 page's own layout or copy, only on handling dead URLs; 404s sitting in Search Console don't hurt rankings by themselves, "what will hurt your ranking is if you are linking from content to 404s." [playbook IV] [call 082]
- Redirect general 404 URLs to the homepage, then click Validate in Search Console; clearing can take months and "you're not getting penalized for it" meanwhile. [call 015]
- Decision rule: nothing to replace it, let it 404; content moved to a new URL, 301 old to new; a permanently discontinued service, use 410; never 404 a product that might restock. Deleting genuinely dead pages (zero impressions, indexing, traffic, backlinks) is safe; the cautious version sets them to DRAFT instead. [playbook IV] [playbook VI]

### Sitemap pages

- The sources are almost entirely about the XML sitemap; multiple slices explicitly state, "HTML sitemap pages: nothing in this slice." [calls 022, 023, 024]
- Every site needs an XML sitemap at /sitemap.xml, auto-generated by WordPress, Wix, Squarespace, and Shopify: "do not make a sitemap yourself... you want to use a plugin." [course: do-you-have-a-sitemap]
- Submit it once in Search Console; it updates itself, never resubmit after normal publishing, the one exception is after a full site redesign. [call 013] [playbook IV]
- Submitting it is "the number one thing" for crawling and indexing; a one-page site's sitemap holding just one URL is part of why it can't rank. [calls 059, 061]
- A missing sitemap is catastrophic: clients who deleted theirs saw traffic "drop from hundreds of thousands to zero," and one GoDaddy site had none at all. [calls 116, 110, 113]
- Segmented sitemaps (posts, pages, categories, authors) are fine if each sub-sitemap path is submitted individually; Showit runs a sitemap separate from its WordPress blog, submit both; GoHighLevel sitemaps don't auto-update; Squarespace and Wix have each broken sitemap generation in practice (a slug rename once stopped Wix updating it). [calls 099, 013] [calls 102, 105]

### Careers pages

- Keep careers out of the main nav, move it to the footer or under an About dropdown: a therapy practice had "Career" in the main menu next to booking-focused items, "if you want them to book, why is there a career button?", moved under About. [call 058] [playbook IV] [call 096] [call 070]
- Exception: a real-estate school's state-mandated designations page and its continuing-education listing both stayed in the nav because each targets a distinct keyword. [call 003]

### Press and media pages

- Never publish the release text on your own site or link to it, "you don't want to do reciprocal linking" since the outlets already link to you, share on social and email instead; a "featured in" logo strip with no links is a fine, lightweight press page, but a "media" page of unrelated videos is "useless." [call 021] [call 029] [playbook V] [call 012] [call 042]
- Pricing runs from about $80 web-only (AB Newswire) up to a full human-reviewed service at about $1,500-2,000; a real AB-Newswire-style package (about $200 for two) landed coverage on AP News, USA Today, Benzinga, and the Globe and Mail. [playbook V] [call 124]
- One release covers one topic, one business, one location, one keyword; cap it at 1-2 releases a month, no real news required, and don't claim "best." [playbook V] [call 088]

### Resources, guides, and lead magnets

- Guide and ebook pages should lean on "free guide," "free resource," "free," "do it yourself," "DIY" language so you're not competing against bigger institutional players. [call 001]
- Gate a PDF lead magnet behind an email address, frame it as concrete value ("$100 of value, free"), follow with email automation; never repurpose someone else's PDF as your own. [call 005]
- The resource/guide page sells the service, the how-to blog post builds trust; break long cornerstone guides (around 5,000 words) into smaller posts, a repeated paragraph of "for more resources" links is not fine. [call 081] [call 024]
- Book's placement ranking: in-content between paragraphs works best when related to the post, a blog sidebar barely works ("banner blindness"), exit-intent pop-ups are most effective; economics: "give away what other people charge for," but "do not work for free or get into a discount race to the bottom." [book ch. 46]

### Alternatives and comparison pages

- A "[popular product] alternative/alternatives" page lists your own products; an "X vs. Y" comparison lives as a blog post, while a genuine product-comparison page (Botox vs. Dysport vs. Xeomin) is a valid consideration-stage page. [call 013] [playbook II] [playbook VI]
- A "best of" or "dupe" comparison page gets linked from the footer as a resource-style page, but never a sponsored/paid-ads page found on Google. [call 114] [call 122]
- Off-site reputation risk: a competitor's "my business vs. his business" page with false claims can rank for your brand name; options are cease-and-desist/trademark action, or out-publishing it with more content and a professional rebuttal. [call 003]

### Lead-magnet sites and standalone tools

- Matt builds standalone lead-magnet sites and tools (a backlink-gap tool, a topic-cluster analyzer, a rank report card) with Claude Code in about an hour, vibe-coded, mostly unbranded beyond a "brought to you by" line, driving thousands of signups. [calls 011, 020, 063, 064]
- A minimal one-page tool can outrank major competitors: LinkGap.io runs "35 words on this page. One heading, one tag. There's no meta description," no Search Console or Analytics, one backlink from heytony.ca, and ranks number 2 for "backlink gap" above Semrush, Moz, and Ahrefs, credited to browsing and conversion signals (roughly 15,000 email sign-ups). [call 051]
- Show the tool itself near the top, not below a wall of copy, "you have to scroll past a bunch of stuff... so you go back to Google," and add a path to purchase after the result; a good one becomes a traffic magnet by itself, an employment lawyer's severance calculator "brings in so much traffic... you look like the hero." [call 033] [call 063]

### Author pages

- Missing meta descriptions on author pages are "really non issues," fix an unfinished one only if it's actually getting traffic; paginated, feed, and author pages are ordinary Search Console noise ("duplicate without canonical," "blocked"), safe to ignore. [call 078] [call 013] [playbook IV]

### Taxonomy, tag, and archive pages

- Tag archive pages are treated as low-quality dead ends: delete and 301 them; keep category pages to a small number, missing meta descriptions on them are a non-issue. [call 121] [call 122] [call 103] [call 078]
- Public taxonomy/filter pages shouldn't exist as crawlable URLs: a directory's "0-2 years in practice" filter prompted "having a taxonomy page that is public, like that doesn't make sense"; keep the filter client-side instead. [call 017]

### Other page types mentioned

- Client portal or login (e.g., app.yourdomain.com): fine to run, walled off from rankings but can build authority through time-on-site and branded "login" searches. [call 005]
- Guest post pages, on someone else's site: title becomes the H1 and URL slug, exactly one link back using a money keyword as anchor, at least one internal link to a host-site article, CTA at the bottom; never republish the same piece on your own site, "you're just going to be competing with yourself." [calls 009, 006] [playbook V]
- Directory sites: fine to host, but every entry needs a real CTA, not just a member logo; quizzes "in most cases... probably aren't doing anything for you," drop them unless they demonstrably get traffic or convert. [call 061] [call 052]
- Chamber of Commerce profile page, on their site: ask for your logo and 2-3 links to specific service pages, not one generic "visit website" link. [call 052] [playbook V]
- Podcast and event/webinar pages: a podcast site needs just a homepage, booking, featured guests, and a sample playlist, an episode-page index is fine but won't rank, nobody searches individual episodes; a webinar landing page is conversion-only, "don't have links to anything else." [call 017] [call 065] [call 052]
- Seasonal pages: one evergreen URL without the year, updating only the title tag, meta description, and H1 each cycle; pull the nav link out of season. [call 069] [playbook VI]
- One-page websites: generally avoid them for anything meant to rank, since one page can't hold internal links or attract backlinks; a landing/sales page is the accepted exception. [call 096] [call 026]
- Bilingual pages: a second-language version can rank for the same keyword as the English page; use subfolders, not subdomains. [call 074] [playbook IV]
- Never publish a public "coming soon" page; avoid Linktree-style link-in-bio pages too, "link directly to the website or the page you want." [call 027] [call 121]
- Rename a generic "News" section to "Blog" unless the content is genuinely timely; the default content strategy stays evergreen. [call 101]

---

## 14. Cross-page elements

### Header and navigation

- The nav is "the number one example of internal linking," HeyTony's own menu (Work, Services, Contact, Blog) each linking to that section; nav and footer links "send a strong signal to Google (and your visitors) about which pages are the most important," since they appear on every page. [course: internal-linking] [book ch. 9]
- Keep top-nav to roughly 7-8 items max; push non-essentials (store, careers, policies) to the footer or an About dropdown; contact must be in the nav; never hide the blog, "'Can we link to the blog but hide it so people can't find it?' If that's you, don't." [playbook IV] [call 058] [book ch. 27]

Crawl depth:
- A page in the nav is one click from the homepage; keep everything you want ranked within 3 clicks total; dropdown items don't add an extra click by themselves, "it's when you leave that page" that counts, but a hidden hamburger menu does. [course: crawl-depth] [call 022] [call 073] [call 097]
- Recovering an old ranking can be as simple as "put that page in your navigation menu." [call 071]

Design rules:
- Keep the header small, about one-sixth of the page, not half. [call 070] [playbook IV]
- The logo sits left or centered beside the nav, never shown twice, and never becomes the page's H1. [call 083] [playbook IV]
- Fit the nav on one line, nest overflow under an existing item rather than let it wrap. [call 070] [call 066]
- Design for mobile reality: 50-60% of traffic is mobile, and deep dropdowns don't work there, "I put everything on a page" instead. [call 073] [call 070]
- Menu labels should read as clear categories, not vague branding, "Nutrition" doesn't say what it is. [call 066]

Don'ts and live examples:
- Don't hide the nav behind a hamburger button on desktop, "it should just be shown"; don't let theme code inject stray heading tags into the header, creating duplicate H1s; don't add unfinished pages to the menu before they have content. [call 061] [call 070] [call 069] [call 032]
- Elliot's HTML pages used a differently colored menu per page, "this site looks like a different site." [call 010]

### Mega menus and drop-downs

- Purpose: keep core items visible, push the long tail (services or locations) into a dropdown or mega menu, each item still linking to its own page. [call 067] [playbook IV]
- Simplicity beats depth regardless of catalog size: "there's like five buttons. That's it... the more things that you have in your navigation menu, the less impact it has." [call 048]
- Locations pattern: a "Locations" dropdown lists cities, hovering one can reveal that city's service pages; Google can read content inside collapsed dropdown or detail elements. [call 068] [call 072]
- Test it like a real user: "ask somebody to go through it with you... like a mom or a grandparent... can you find this page." Don'ts: no three-level arrow-nested menus a first-time visitor can't follow, and no mega menu so dense it's unusable on mobile, "I want to see one drop down. You could have 10 on here." [call 048] [call 103]

### Footer

- Carries secondary-but-important pages (FAQs, policies, support pages, location-specific content), plus NAP (name, address, phone) and a link to the Google Business Profile via its CID link, "Google looks for that to determine if it's a local business." [book ch. 9] [call 052] [playbook V]
- Social profile links instead of an auto-updating embedded feed; location pages that don't fit the main nav can live in the footer. [call 042] [call 082]
- Mirror the top nav in the footer whenever the header nav isn't sticky, so the footer is never a dead end. [call 059] [playbook IV]
- Footer titles above menu columns must be H4 or smaller, or plain text, never H1/H2/H3. [call 125] [playbook IV]
- Footer links carry less crawl/authority weight than header or in-content links; footer text, like nav and sidebar text, doesn't count toward word count. [call 084] [course: low-word-count-how-to-fix]
- "Website design by / powered by" footer credit links work as an early-stage backlink tactic but are "not a long term solution"; if one client footer is responsible for more than about 100 backlinks (one real case ran 288,000-406,000, flagged as spam with only the homepage still indexed), remove your link, a sitewide footer credit reads as spam. [call 013] [call 095] [book ch. 16]
- A dark footer reads as a stable "end of the website" cue; use a static, linked map image, not a slow embedded interactive one; don't put an H1 in the footer, and don't reciprocal-link footer credits into an "incest hub." [call 028] [call 098] [call 040] [call 066] [call 004]

### Sidebars

- Common contents: a table of contents anchoring only H2/H3, a static CTA (phone, hours, shop link), a custom callout graphic. [call 030] [call 111] [call 008]
- A sticky sidebar table of contents (left quarter column) works well on long posts and beats one placed mid-post. [call 080] [call 054]
- In-content contextual links carry more weight than a sidebar "related posts" block, don't rely on a sidebar as your internal-linking strategy; sidebar text, like footer and nav text, is excluded from word-count measurement. [playbook III] [playbook IV] [call 124]
- The book is blunt about it: "Blog sidebar. Not very effective. People have developed banner blindness." [book ch. 46]
- Don't: rotating carousel content in a sidebar can render as H4 tags via the theme, breaking heading order. [call 086]

### Pop-ups and email capture

Placement, ranked, per the book's numbered list: blog sidebar (weak, "banner blindness"); in-content between paragraphs (works well if related to the post); a navigation menu tab (e.g., "New Owner Checklist"); exit-intent (most effective, appears bottom-left or bottom-right without blocking content, fires only when a visitor is about to leave). [book ch. 46]

- Timing beats offer: fire on exit intent or after a delay (about 30 seconds), never on page load, "unless I know who this is... I'm not signing up for anything"; a pop-up form is also a legitimate alternative to jump-links on a funnel page. [call 081] [call 124] [call 016]
- A pop-up's own heading tag creates a duplicate H1 even if it never opens, "if a person can see it all the time, Google can see it all the time"; restyle it down to plain paragraph text instead of removing it. [call 013] [call 096] [playbook III]
- Hidden pop-up text and review-widget copy inflate word-count tools invisibly, one plugin added roughly 2,000 identical hidden words per page. [call 095] [playbook II] [playbook III]
- Don't fire a newsletter pop-up immediately, it reads as aggressive; don't rely on a generic "Subscribe to Newsletter" widget, "that doesn't work anymore." [call 061] [book ch. 46]

### Chat widgets

- The sources say almost nothing about chat widgets specifically, most guidance folds them into general floating-UI rules; the closest concrete example is an accessibility widget "on the side of the screen" (accessiBe and similar). [playbook IV] [call 002]
- A floating mobile icon needs a text label ("call"), an icon alone isn't self-explanatory; a "let's chat" widget stacked with call/email links added to CTA confusion on a page with no clear primary action, treat it as one more competing CTA to prune. [call 054] [call 100]
- A slow-loading "checking your browser" interstitial "is going to be a bad signal to Google... because it's so slow," the same caution applies to any heavy chat-widget script. [call 001]

### Sticky bars and announcement/cookie bars

- Sticky bars carry an offer (a coupon percentage, a free-shipping threshold) and should stay static, not animated. [call 060]
- Book's rule, numeric: "Do NOT have both top and bottom sticky banners at once." [book ch. 46]
- Anchor a mobile phone-number bar at the bottom of the screen, not top-left, and make sure it's clickable. [call 054]
- A cookie or announcement bar, or a pop-up, adds hidden words that word-count tools, and Google, still count; cookie-consent/GDPR-style plugins can silently break page JavaScript, an "Accept all cookies" button did nothing on one site. [call 125] [call 064] [call 072]
- Don't let a cookie or announcement bar re-show on every visit or take over the screen on mobile, one owner's cookie consent pop-up was "too big... on the phone too"; don't stack a separate phone-number bar above the main header, "it almost looks broken"; don't let sticky bottom bars annoy visitors off the page, "God, these bars at the bottom piss me off so much. Get out of my way." [call 019] [call 049] [call 100] [call 066]

### Breadcrumbs

- Definition: "those little navigation trails you often see at the top of a page, just under the main menu... kind of like a GPS for your website." Example: "Products > Dog > Collars > Small Collars." SEO effect: "they also create a logical internal link structure that signals hierarchy and relevance to search engines." [book ch. 9]
- Rule: breadcrumbs must not carry heading tags, strip any H-tag to plain text. [playbook IV]
- Don't let breadcrumbs disappear on scroll: "you're hiding text on the page immediately. Google doesn't like that." [call 109]
- Live example: a custom post type kept its slug inside child URLs, functioning as "part of like the breadcrumb." [call 014]

### Search

- The sources say almost nothing about an on-site search feature; the one concrete mention is design-level, not structural: an invisible search icon on a low-contrast header bar was called out as "useless unless it's the right color." [call 103]

## 15. What the emails add (the short version)

Matt's emails (190 from matt@heytony.ca, Jun 2025 to Aug 2026) are mostly Rank Week, Insiders, and bundle promotions. About 60 carry a tip, and the tips are almost all about process (write the post, audit the money page, link internally) rather than page layout. The full digest, with every prompt and checklist verbatim and a dated index, is `heytony-email-tips.md`. What the emails add to the page chapters above:

- **The page taxonomy, in one line.** Money pages are "services, service + city combos, industries served, product or pricing pages. Anything someone lands on ready to buy." Content pages are "blog posts, guides, FAQs, comparisons. Anything someone lands on while researching." Junk to ignore when mapping a site: "tag pages, author pages, pagination, /cart/, /privacy-policy/, media attachments." [email 2026-08-04]
- **Which money pages to build next.** Run the Topic Gap Analysis against a competitor's sitemap (homepage + `/sitemap.xml`), rank gaps "by revenue potential, not search volume. A page that's one step from a phone call beats a page that gets traffic and nothing else," treat a competitor cluster of "4+ pages on one theme" as the bigger signal, and write each new money page "for a customer who's comparing two or three local companies right now, not someone doing research." [email 2026-08-04]
- **The HeyTony internal Content Checklist** (his team's per-page list): title 60 characters max with the keyword near the front; meta 120 to 156 characters with the keyword near the front; exactly one H1 with the keyword; several H2s with the keyword in 2 to 3 of them; a 1,000-word minimum that is also at least as long as each of the top 3 results individually; readability green in Yoast; 3 to 4 internal links (one always to a service or product page, some to older pages); 1 to 2 external links to non-competitors; 3 to 4 webp images with keyword-bearing alt text, at least one photo's alt carrying the focus keyword; keyword in the URL; no cannibalization (if another page already targets the keyword and is not ranking well, update that page instead); 2 to 3 links in from other posts; 2+ links out to service or product pages; a link to the Google Business Profile with the GBP target keyword as anchor; a sweep for missing punctuation, homophones, and AI phrases ("delve into", "enter a world of", "Today's digital landscape", "a tapestry", "in conclusion", "in summary", "Skyrocket"). [email 2025-07-03]
- **The keyword-placement floor for any page.** Title tag, meta description, H1, at least one H2, the content at least twice, the URL if you can. "If you do that, you'll be better off than 90% of websites out there!" because "The most common reason a page doesn't rank is because it doesn't include the keyword they want to rank for.. and even if it does, it's usually mentioned once!" [email 2025-06-19]
- **The money-page checklist in five words.** Title tag, meta description, headings, content improvements, internal links; audit first (your page plus the #1 and #2 results through PageAudit.com on 12 fields), then rewrite the tags so the keyword is "front-loaded where possible" and each tag is "better than my two competitors'", then rewrite the body to the competitors' word-count range and density without stuffing, then add 3 incoming internal links per money page from the sitemap. [emails 2025-12-04, 2025-12-08, 2025-12-09, 2025-12-10]
- **Blog post formatting for AI drafts.** "Use H2 and H3 headings only. No horizontal rules, section dividers, tables, callout boxes, or other formatting elements. Just headings, paragraphs, and bullet points where appropriate." Then strip "AI fluff or unnecessary numbered lists", add internal links and images, publish, and link in from 3 older posts. [emails 2026-07-20, 2025-12-07]
- **Update & Boost for old pages.** Fresh stats, improved structure, added internal links; 5 to 10 strategic internal links per sweep. [email 2025-12-04]
- **Pricing transparency.** "be the most transparent business in your industry" (from *Endless Customers*): publish the cost guide ("How Much Does SEO Cost in 2025? (The Brutally Honest Guide)"), fold reader questions back into it, and write the posts that answer "any questions a business owner might have BEFORE we get on a call", then redesign pages so that information is easy to find. [emails 2025-06-22, 2025-06-30]
- **Accessibility is an SEO foundation.** "can someone who is legally blind use it? Because if they can't, it's likely impacting your Google Rankings!" [email 2026-07-16]
- **Conversion.** "improving your conversion rate by just 1% could double your business." [email 2025-10-10]
- **Review solicitation rules (GBP).** No incentives, no staff names, ask everyone not just happy customers, do not ask on premises, do not ask for specific content. [email 2026-04-16]
- **The counter-example to over-building.** A one-page, 35-word, vibe-coded site with no meta description and no H2s ranks #1 "for a ton of keywords" (the why was behind a link). Read it as "intent and niche beat polish", not as a page spec. [email 2026-08-11]
- **Legal pages.** The only mention: `/privacy-policy/` is junk to ignore when mapping topics, meaning it is not an SEO page. [email 2026-08-04]
- **What the emails never cover:** homepage structure, about, contact, portfolio, testimonials page layout, landing pages, 404/thank-you pages, header/footer. Use the chapters above for those.
